 4

Abstract
The overall objective of this project was to define and evaluate strategies to minimize amine
oxidation in scrubbing processes for CO2 capture. Bench-scale and pilot plant measurements were
performed to understand oxidation mechanisms and quantify the effectiveness of technologies to
minimize oxidation of piperazine (PZ). Pilot plant campaigns were carried out at the Separations
Research Program (SRP 2022, 1040 h), National Carbon Capture Center (NCCC 2023, 5900 h),
and Technical Centre Mongstad (TCM 2025, 2,650 hrs).
Bench-scale measurements and pilot plant campaigns demonstrated that NO2 in the inlet flue gas
catalyzes the oxidation of 1–3 mol PZ/mol NO2. This effect was mitigated at NCCC 2023 by
sulfite prescrubbing to remove 80% of the NO2. Amino acids and their amides with PZ were the
most significant class of liquid phase oxidation products. The amino acids accumulate, then
solubilize and activate dissolved Fe3+/2+ that catalyzes the oxidation of PZ. Nitrogen stripping
readily removes dissolved oxygen and reduces oxidation with < 0.5 ppm NO2. Simple batch
thermal reclaiming removes the amino acids and the dissolved Fe3+/2+ and greatly reduces the PZ
oxidation rate and the production of ammonia. Without inlet aerosol, amine emissions are < 200
ppb with a single-stage water wash and < 1 ppb with a 2-stage wash. Ammonia emissions are
significantly reduced by acid wash and by thermal reclaiming.

Task Objectives and timeline
The overall objective of this project was to define and evaluate strategies to minimize amine
oxidation. Bench- and pilot-scale experiments were performed to quantify the effectiveness of
technologies to minimize oxidation of PZ and other second-generation (2G) amine solvents. The
technologies are being developed to support capture from coal-fired power plants but are also
relevant for gas-fired turbines. This work used the PiperaZine with the Advanced Stripper (PZAS)
technology for CO2 scrubbing as the baseline technology. The mitigation technologies being
developed are expected to reduce the cost and environmental risk of solvent-based CO2 capture
systems.
The milestones for this project are listed in Table 1.

Why this work is important
This work was initiated in response to PRD C-2 of the Mission Innovation (2017), “Creating
environmentally friendly solvent processes for CO2 capture.”
 “Solvents for post-combustion capture experience losses due to oxidation, nitrosation,
 aerosols, and other contaminants and mechanisms that are not fully understood. These
 losses lead to significant environmental impacts and costs for solvent makeup and
 reclamation to separate impurities. This [project] will close this knowledge gap by
 systematic studies of contaminants and loss mechanisms that will be applicable to all
 solvents and all applications. The research should develop methods to mitigate solvent
 losses that will reduce the risk, cost, and environmental impact of deploying solvent
 systems for CCUS.” (Mission Innovation, 2017)
The primary objective of this project is to safeguard aqueous amine solvents for CO2 capture
against O2 and NO2. Amine oxidation is a major risk in the deployment of amine scrubbing. We
must understand and minimize this risk:
 To establish the safety of amine scrubbing for public acceptance;
 To understand all EH&S impacts (volatile emissions, aerosols, nitrosamines);
 To close the material balance and identify risks;
 To develop mitigation methods.
PZAS baseline process
The baseline process for this study is the PZAS amine scrubbing technology for capturing CO2
from a natural gas combined cycle shown in Figure 1. This technology used 5 m piperazine (PZ)
with the stripper at 6 bar. The solvent is exposed to 11–14% oxygen in the absorber at 40 to 60 oC.
Solvent oxidation may occur in the absorber packing itself or in the sump of the absorber. Any
remaining dissolved oxygen is probably consumed in the rich solvent as it is preheated to stripper
temperature. Previous bench-scale work has suggested that there are other mechanisms that

 6

continue to oxidize the amine at the high temperature of the stripper itself (Plantz, 2025, Table
3.2).

 Figure 1: PZAS process for NGCC using 5 m PZ
Scope of Task 2 – Laboratory studies
Task 2 used three bench-scale experimental systems to make measurements that will expand our
understanding of amine oxidation. A simple batch reactor with liquid recycle was developed to
measure the rate and equilibria of treating degraded solvent with activated carbon (Plantz, 2025,
2.1.2). A high gas flow, liquid batch reactor (HGF) including circulating gas analysis by FTIR was
modified to measure the effect of NO2 on oxidation at absorber conditions (Chen, 2024, 2.3.1).
The new oxygen depletion batch reactor (ODBR) was developed to measure the rate of oxygen
consumption in amine solutions at absorber conditions (Obute, 2026, ch. 2). The existing high
temperature oxidation reactor (HTOR) includes cycling from absorber to stripper temperature
(Plantz, 2025, 2.1.3). It was used for longer term experiments to determine the effects of amino
acid degradation products.
Scope of Task 3 – Pilot plant testing
Task 3 tested mitigation methods and varied oxidation conditions in three pilot plants of increasing
size. Table 2 gives parameters that give the scale of the three plants: CO2 capture varies from 1.5
to 234 t/d; the absorber packing height varies from 20 to 60 ft.

Table 3 gives the range of testing options used at each of the pilot plants. Variations in inlet NO2

Program, University of Texas at Austin) with air/CO2 to 20 ppm NO2 at TCM with flue gas from
a fuel gas boiler (Mongstad Heat and Power (MHP) plant). Batch thermal reclaiming was applied
once at the National Carbon Capture Center (NCCC) and twice at TCM.

Scope of this Report
This summary report will present project results structured around three major topics:
 Amine oxidation,
 Emissions and water wash performance, and
 Energy and reliability performance.
It will end with conclusions and recommendations.

Results on Amine Oxidation
The important new results on amine oxidation will start with the oxidation products of PZ and a
proposed mechanism for PZ oxidation. Results will then be presented on three specific elements
of the oxidation mechanism and a remaining question on the importance of stripper conditions.
Oxidation Products and Mechanism for PZ
Oxidation Products of PZ
Several new amino acids and their amides were identified and quantified as significant oxidation
products of PZ during this project (Closmann et al., 2025). A high resolution (50,000) quadrupole
time-of-flight QTOF-MS (Agilent 6546) coupled to a high-performance liquid chromatograph
(HPLC) was used to screen degraded liquid samples and accurately identify several amino acids
and their amides by m/z determination. Free amino acids were quantified by anion
chromatography with a pulsed amperometric detector (HAPE-PAD). Total amino acids and their
respective amides were determined by prehydrolyzing the samples with sodium hydroxide before
analysis.
Figure 2 shows the pathways to important PZ oxidation products that are smaller than PZ. The
amino acid species include aminoethylglycinate (AEG), glycinate (GLY), and the lactam
oxopiperazine (OPZ). Aldehydes (formaldehyde, acetaldehyde, glyoxal) were derivatized by
DNPH quantified on the HPLC. Organic acids (formate, acetate, glycolate, oxalate) were
quantified by ion chromatography. Total organic acids and their amides were determined by
prehydrolyzing the samples with sodium hydroxide. The hazardous air pollutants acetonitrile and
aminoacetaldehyde were quantified as emissions by PTR-TOF-MS.
Figure 3 shows pathways to PZ oxidation products that are larger than PZ. The amino acid species
include piperazineacetate (PZAA), 2ethylenediaminediacetate (2EDDA),
3ethylenediaminediacetate, and their lactams: 2-oxopiperazineacetate (2OPZA) and 3-
oxopiperazineacetate (3OPZA).

Figure 4 shows the accumulation of organic acids species in the solvent during the NCCC pilot

 11

PZAA and AEG are the most important organic acids. The amino acids increased steadily until
the fifth period when all the mitigation methods were being used. Because of a corrosion episode
starting in the middle of that period, it appears that AEG (aminoethylglycine) was oxidized to GLY
(glycine). This figure also shows that reclaiming at 5,200 hrs was effective at removing the amino
acid products.

 Figure 4: Amino acids in hydrolyzed samples from NCCC 2023
Free radical oxidation mechanism
A probable free radical mechanism for the oxidation of PZ is given in m, 5. In the absence of NO2,
several results suggest that dissolved iron as Fe3+ initiates oxidation by decomposing the piperazine
peroxide product (PZOOH) to the radicals PZO. and OH.. These radicals can then extract a proton
from PZ to give the radical PZ.. If NO2 is present, it will react with PZ to produce PZ. directly.
The oxidation will propagate by the reaction of PZ. with oxygen to give the PZ peroxide radical,
PZOO.. Reaction of PZOO. with PZ will further propagate the oxidation and provide PZOOH to
initiate by reaction with Fe+3. PZOOH will also decompose to give oxygen and other PZ oxidation
products. The free radical mechanism may then terminate by the direct reaction of two free radicals
to give a molecular product.
The two important catalysts of amine oxidation are NO2 and Fe3+. The following sections will
address results for each of these catalysts.

 12

 Figure 5: Probable free radical mechanism for oxidation of PZ
Oxidation Initiated by NO2
Dominant Mechanism in pilot plants
With as little as 0.5 to 1 ppm NO2, the dominant mechanism from Figure 5 in pilot plant testing
for initiating PZ oxidation is the reaction of PZ with NO2:

Figure 6 shows results for PZ oxidation in several earlier pilot plant tests. Estimated cumulative
oxidation was 0.38 to 1.11 mmol/kg-hr in earlier pilot plants with 1–5 ppm NO2 and 0.11 mmol/kg-
hr in plants with 0–1 ppm NO2. The Tarong results suggest that there is 3–4 mol PZ oxidized/mol
NO2 in the inlet flue gas.

 13

 Figure 6: PZ degradation in earlier pilot plants (Wu, 2022)
The nominal stoichiometry for oxidation of PZ to formic acid, NH3, and N2O by reaction with NO2
is 0.25 mol PZ/mol NO2:
                     C4N2H10 + 2H2O + 4 NO2 >> 4CO2H2 +2 NH3 + 2N2O
Nominal stoichiometry for oxidation of PZ to oxopiperazine, water, and N2O by reaction with NO2
is 0.75 mol PZ/mol NO2:
                    C4N2H10 + 1.333 NO2 >> C4N2OH8 + H2O + 0.667 N2O
Since the actual effect of NO2 is 2–8 mol PZ oxidized/mol NO2, not 0.25–0.75 mol/mol, NO2 is
catalyzing oxidation by initiating the free radical reactions of oxygen with PZ and its oxidation
products.
Experiment HGF 8 in the high gas flow reactor demonstrated that 1 mol of NO2 would result in
3 mol oxopiperazine (OPZ) (Figure 7) (Chen, 2024).

 14

 Figure 7: Effect of NO2 on PZ oxidation in the high gas flow (HGF) reactor (Chen, 2024)
Figure 8 shows the result of adding 1 ppm NO2 to the flue gas in the SRP campaign in 2022. The
NO2 was introduced briefly at 220 h and again starting at 340 h. The ratio of the accumulated
oxopiperazine to the NO2 absorbed was about 8, suggesting that NO2 has a catalytic effect on the
oxidation of PZ.

Figure 8: Effect of adding 1 ppm to air/CO2 in the SRP pilot plant campaign (Chen, 2024)
Mitigation of NO2 effects by sulfite scrubbing at NCCC 2023
In the pilot plant campaign at NCCC, the effect of NO2 was tested by periodically activating NO2
prescrubbing with a solution of sodium sulfite/thiosulfate as illustrated in Figure 9. The
prescrubbing reduced the typical inlet NO2 from 2.5 ppm to 0.5 ppm. Each mol of NO2 in the inlet
flue gas required 9.5–10 mol sodium sulfite/mol NO2 and 0.9–3.5 mol sodium thiosulfate (Chen,
2024).

 15

 Figure 9: Prescrubbing NO2 with Na2SO3/Na2S2O3 in NCCC 2023 (Chen, 2024)
Figure 10 shows the accumulation of mononitrosopiperazine (MNPZ) in NCCC 2023 and
demonstrates that NO2 scrubbing was effective. The campaign tested three mitigation methods:
N2 sparging, NO2 scrubbing, and carbon treating. The initial period (gray) and the fifth period
(gray) used all three methods. NO2 scrubbing was off in periods 2 (salmon) and 3 (yellow). Initial
levels of nitrosamine were high until NO2 scrubbing was started and decreased in the initial period
as it thermally degraded in the stripper. When NO2 scrubbing was turned off in period 3,
nitrosamine accumulated from 1.2 mM to a steady-state level of 3.2 mM, then decreased by
thermal degradation when the stripper sump level was increased to 70%. When NO2 scrubbing
was restored in period 5 (gray), MNPZ decreased to 0.4 mM as thermal degradation in the stripper
continued.

 16

 Figure 10: Accumulation of mononitrosopiperazine at NCCC 2023
Figure 11 shows the ammonia emissions at NCCC 2023. The rate of change of ammonia emissions
increased when NO2 scrubbing was turned off in period 3 (yellow) and step changed down at the
beginning of period 5 when all of the mitigation methods were turned back on. This behavior
suggests that NO2 scrubbing is effective at reducing oxidation. There was an exceptional increase
in ammonia emissions at 4,400 hrs (the “tipping point”), where a corrosion event took place.
Reclaiming at 5,200 hrs greatly reduced ammonia emissions by removing the amino acid
degradation products from which ammonia is produced.

 Figure 11: NH3 generation in NCCC 2013 (Chen, 2024)
The quantitative effect of NO2 on the cumulative N degradation products of PZ in NCCC 2023 is
given in Figure 12. The rate of accumulation increased from 0.3 to 0.6 mol “PZ”/hr when NO2
scrubbing was turned off at 2,000 hrs and decreased from 0.6 to 0.2 when it was turned back on at
3,700 hrs. These results suggest that 1.8 mol PZ is oxidized for every mole of NO2 absorbed and
that N2 sparging reduces the PZ oxidation by 0.1 mol/hr.

Effect of NO2 at TCM
As shown in Figure 13, ammonia emissions at TCM were strongly correlated with the NO2 in the
inlet flue gas. The colored sections of the graph represent periods with different sources of inlet
flue gas. RFCC flue gas was low in NO2 and was used without dilution at 0.5–1.5 ppm NO2 and
with two levels of dilution resulting in lower NO2. The MHP flue gas came from a faulty SCR
unit that typically had 15 ppm NO2. NO2 in the MHP gas was lower when diluted by recycled
CO2.

 Figure 13: NH3 emissions and NO2 in the inlet flue gas at TCM
As shown in Figure 14, the degradation products in the solvent at TCM also increase faster in the
MHP periods with increased NO2. The total hydrolyzed amino acids are the most significant class
of degradation products. Ethylenediamine (EDA) and total formate are also important. These
liquid phase products are reduced by the batch thermal reclaiming that starts at 1,000 and 2,200
hrs.

 19

 Figure 14: Degradation products in the solvent at TCM (Attachment 2)
A combination of the data in Figures 13 and 14 gives the total cumulative moles of PZ degraded
at TCM (Figure 15). The empirical correlation of these data starts over at the end of the second
reclaiming period. The rate of PZ degradation is 8.9 moles/hr plus 1.6 times the feed rate (mols/hr)
of NO2 in the absorber feed gas. This means that one mole of NO2 oxidizes 1.6 moles PZ.

  Figure 15: Cumulative nitrogen in degradation products in the liquid and gas phase at
 TCM, expressed as moles of PZ degraded (blue pts). 1 mol NO2 in the inlet flue gas
 produces 1.6 mol PZ oxidation products (empirical correlation as red dashed line)
 (Attachment 2).

NO2 varies from 1.5 to 8 mole PZ oxidation/mole NO2.

Oxidation Catalyzed by Fe3+
In the mechanism proposed in Figure 5, iron dissolved as chelated or complexed Fe3+ initiates
oxidation by decomposing amine peroxide to produce reactive free radicals. This mechanism
occurs in parallel to initiation by NO2 and will become important when there is little or no NO2 in
the flue gas. Since Fe3+ is not soluble in water at basic conditions typical of amine scrubbing, the
dissolved iron must be solubilized with complexation/chelation by the amine carbamate or other
species in the solution.
This discussion will present results from ODBR and HTOR experiments that demonstrate the
dissolution and activation of Fe3+ by PZ carbamate and amino acid degradation products. Pilot
plant results will demonstrate the effectiveness of mitigation by thermal reclaiming and the
inconclusive effectiveness of carbon treating.
Fe+3Clustering
As shown in Figure 16, the oxidation rate (oxygen consumption rate) of clean, loaded PZ at 60 oC
increases by a factor of 4 with the addition of only 0.05 mM Fe+3. In these experiments the rate is
given as a first-order rate constant, k (hr-1) for the disappearance of dissolved oxygen. From the
raw data it can be seen that a reaction time (residence time in the sump) of 1–2 hours would
consume most of the dissolved oxygen in the sump. Since the sump residence time is typically
10–30 minutes and the absorber liquid residence time is even shorter, these measured rates in clean
solvent would not account for much PZ oxidation in a pilot plant.

 21

Figure 16: Oxygen depletion in PZ catalyzed by Fe+3 in 5 m PZ at 0.4 mol CO2/equiv N and
 60 oC
Figure 17 shows the effect of adding more Fe+3 to the clean solvent in the ODBR experiment. The
rate constant increases from clean solvent to 0.05 mM Fe+3. At greater Fe+3 the rate is a very weak
function of Fe+3. This would be easy to attribute to limited solubility of Fe+3 in the solvent, but
these are still clear solutions at short times. However, previous work suggests that at greater Fe+3
the “dissolved” Fe+3 exists in solution as multiatomic clusters of Fe+3nOHm or other similar species
rather than monoatomic Fe+3. These clusters are less catalytically active so the effect of added
Fe+3 on the rate is minimal (Obute, 2026).

 Figure 17: Effect of clustering reduces Fe+3 effectiveness at 0.05 mM Fe3+ in 5 m PZ at
                                60 oC in ODBR (Obute, 2025)

 22

Rates increased by amino acids
In this project we have demonstrated that the amino acid degradation products increase the
solubility and effectiveness of the dissolved Fe+3. Table 6 shows that the oxygen consumption rate
constant measured in the ODBR is significantly enhanced by the addition of specific amino acid
degradation products and other amino acid chelating agents. These conditions primarily reflect
the ability of each complexing agent in stabilizing Fe³⁺ in monomeric, catalytically active forms.
EDTA was the most effective (k = 24 h⁻¹), followed by PZAAPZ⁺ (12 h⁻¹) and NTA (9.2 h⁻¹).
This ranking is consistent with established stability constants, where multidentate ligands with
higher denticity and greater thermodynamic stability more effectively prevent Fe³⁺ hydrolysis and
clustering (Martell and Smith, 1989).
 Table 6: Summary of DO consumption in 5 m PZ (𝛼 = 0.4) with 1 mM Fe3+ and amino
 acids/ligands at 60 ℃ (Obute, 2026)

                                                 k (h-1)                       k (h-1)
 Amino acid/ligand
                                             +1 mM Fe3+                  +0.025 mM Fe3+

             1 mM EDTA                               24                         1.0

         20 mM PZAAPZ+                               12                         0.37

            15 mM NTA                                9.2                        0.13

           15 mM AEG+                                4.0                        0.13

           15 mM EDDA                                2.0                        0.76

            15 mM GLY                                1.5                        0.09

            15 mM IDA                                1.4                        0.25

              PZ-COO-                                0.8                        0.27

HTOR experiments with cycling from 50 to 150 oC demonstrate a smaller effect than the ODBR
experiments of amino acid degradation products on the rate of PZ oxidation (Figure 18). The
addition of 30 mM piperazineacetic acid (PZAA) increases the oxidation rate by a factor of 2. 30
mM NTA (a commercial chelator) increases the oxidation rate by a factor of 3. The results provide
one explanation for the increase in the oxidation rate of PZ with the cumulative degradation of the
solvent, as the amino acids accumulate and then solubilize and activate the available Fe+3.

 23

 Figure 18: Effect of amino acids added to HTOR with excess Fe3+ (Plantz, 2025)
Mitigation
The effect of dissolved iron on the rate of amine oxidation may be mitigated by thermal reclaiming
or by carbon treating. The next section will show that simple thermal reclaiming tested at NCCC
and TCM was effective at reasonable cost at removing dissolved iron and the amino acids that
solubilize and activate it. The use of carbon treating NCCC was not conclusive.
Thermal Reclaiming
Simple thermal reclaiming was tested in 2 events at TCM, each 200–300 hrs long (Figure 19). The
reclaiming configuration at TCM is shown in Figure 20. Solvent residue was recirculated from
the semibatch reclaimer vessel through a steam heater. Lean solvent was fed at about 1
inventory/day to the reclaimer vessel for three days with the level maintained by adjusting the
steam rate. The vapor containing PZ, water, and CO2 was returned directly to the bottom of the
stripper. After three days the solvent feed was stopped and NaOH was fed to the reclaimer for a
period to neutralize the residual CO2 and convert carboxylic acids, amino acids, and their amides
to carboxylic ions. The water was added to the reclaimer for 3 to 6 days to provide stripping steam
to remove the residual PZ.

 24

 Figure 19: Two reclaiming events during 2,650 hrs at TCM

 Figure 20: Reclaiming at TCM had 2 distinct periods: Solvent Feed and Water Feed
 (Boiloff)
Table 7 gives the fraction removal of solvent impurities in each reclaiming event. The first event
removed 89–96% of the less volatile impurities; the second event removed 84–88% of most of the
impurities but only 44% of the total formate. Ethylenediamine removal decreased from 49% in
event 1 to 21% in event 2. It seems probable that there was not enough NaOH added in the second
event. The second event also started with the residual inventory in the reclaimer from the first
event.

Figure 21 shows the operating tradeoff at TCM of PZ left in the reclaimer residue with the heat
required to vaporize additional water to facilitate PZ stripping. The small amount of PZ left in the
residue decreased from 2 to <0.01 mmol/kg as the reclaimer heat duty increased from 0.023 to
0.04 GJ/MT CO2 removed.

 Figure 21: Trade-off at TCM reclaimer of PZ in residue with the cumulative heat duty at
 TCM

In the NCCC 2023 campaign, reclaiming was performed at the end of the campaign in a proprietary
batch distillation column operated at 150 oC. It was effective at removing dissolved iron from the
solvent (Figure 22). Attachment 1 provides more details on this testing.

 26

 Figure 22: Dissolved iron at NCCC 2023 was removed by thermal reclaiming
A technoeconomic analysis was performed for application of thermal reclaiming to an application
of the PZAS on 942 MW NGCC based on the results of the testing at TCM and NCCC (Attachment
3). The estimated annualized cost of reclaiming was 0.76 $/MT CO2 (Table 8).
 Table 8: Incremental cost of reclaiming for 942 MW NGCC

Why Thermal Reclaiming
Reclaiming reduces emissions of ammonia, HAPs, and nitrosamine and should be triggered if
those emissions approach unacceptable levels. It removes amino acids and other degradation
products that oxidize to produce ammonia and HAPs. It also removes metals and degradation
products that enhance metal solubility and increase the rate of oxidation. The reclaimer removes
and decomposes the less volatile nitrosamine.
Reclaiming intensity should be optimized to trade off the cost of amine makeup and waste disposal.
More intense reclaiming reduces amine loss by oxidation, but it also increases heat duty and the
amine loss in waste.

 27

Dissolved Oxygen
Dissolved oxygen is necessary for amine oxidation. It enters the mechanism of Figure 5 by
reacting with the PZ radical (PZ.) to produce PZ peroxide radical (PZOO.). If it is removed by
nitrogen sparging before it is consumed, it will not react with the amine.
Previous work by Nielsen (2018) showed that the rate of PZ oxidation could be reduced in the
HTOR by sparging the solvent with nitrogen before it was heated after the air-sparged reactor
(Figure 23). In less degraded 5 m PZ, ammonia production decreased from 0.4–0.6 mmol/kg-h to
0.15 mmol/kg-h when N2 was added to the air-sparging vessel before the high temperature hold
time. After substantial degradation to a level of 0.5 mM dissolved Fe, nitrogen sparging reduced
the ammonia production by the same amount, about 0.35 mmol/kg-hr.

Figure 23: Effect of nitrogen sparging on ammonia production from 5 m PZ in the HTOR
                                      (Nielsen, 2018)
DO in pilot plant testing
Nitrogen sparging was tested as a mitigation method at NCCC 2023 with the flow configuration
in Figure 24. Nitrogen was sparged through a porous metal tube in the sump of the absorber.
Dissolved oxygen was continuously measured in either the rich line leaving the rich storage tank
or in the recycle line to the pump-around intercooler.

 28

 Figure 24: Nitrogen sparging at NCCC 2023
Without nitrogen sparging the DO in the rich solvent leaving the sump was about 4 mg/l as
measured by the DO sensor in the intercooling loop (Figure 25). In period 1(gray) with N2 sparging
on the DO left after the rich storage tank was 0.1 to 0.8 mg/L. Without N2 sparging in period 2 the
DO increased to 1.7 mg/L.

 Figure 25: Dissolved oxygen in rich solvent at NCCC 2023
Mitigation by N2 Stripping
Figure 26 shows that a lower rate of N2 sparging is more efficient at removing oxygen. With lower
gas flow the sparging element produces smaller bubbles that are more effective at stripping oxygen.
0.5 scfm nitrogen provided 95% removal of DO.

 29

 Figure 26: Effect of N2 sparging rate on residual dissolved oxygen after rich storage tank
A techno-economic analysis for nitrogen sparging (Table 9) for application at a 942 MW NGCC
plant with thermal reclaiming shows that it could save $0.35/t CO2 (Attachment 4). Nitrogen
provided by onsite pressure swing adsorption (PSA) is the major cost (0.89 MM$/yr). The benefits
are reduction of PZ makeup (1.18 MM$/yr) and reduced steam for the reclaimer (0.63 MM$/yr).

Effect of Stripper time/T
The rate of PZ degradation increased by a factor of 2.5 from 0.4 to 1.1 mmol/kg-hr when the
bottom stripper temperature was increased from 125 to 155 oC in pilot plant testing of 8 m PZ at
CSIRO Tarong (Figure 27). This campaign also averaged 1.3 ppm NO2. Since PZ is very resistant
to thermal degradation even at 155 oC, it is probable that this is an unknown effect of temperature
on PZ oxidation.

Figure 27: Effect of stripper temperature on degradation of 8 m PZ in pilot plant testing at
 Tarong (Nielsen, 2018)
New experiments in the HTOR (Figure 28) during this project further confirm the effect of stripper
T on the generation of ammonia by oxidation of PZ.

 31

 Figure 28: Ammonia generation as a function of stripper temperature in the HTOR with
 5 m PZ
Table 10 summarizes the available data on this effect. All the data suggest an activation energy of
30 to 39 kJ/mol. Closmann (2011) first observed greater oxidation in a bench-scale system with T
cycling and attributed it to the greater consumption of the available dissolved oxygen. However,
the extent of oxidation with PZ cycling may not be explained by the small amount of available
DO. Voice (2013) suggested that other oxygen carriers, “including hydroperoxides and oxidized
metal ions may contribute to oxidation at high temperature. … In particular, high temperature
cycling may convert dissolved metal ions to their more reduced state, where they can react rapidly
with hydroperoxides in the absorber.”

 32

Emissions and ater ash performance
Amine emissions with variable wash configurations
PZ emissions at NCCC 2023 depended on the number of water washes. Figure 29 gives emissions
measured by PTR-TOF-MS during the last 2,000 hrs of the campaign when numerous changes
were made in the water wash configurations. With a single stage of wash PZ emissions were 100–
200 ppb. After thermal reclaiming the PZ concentration in the in solvent decreased from 5 to 3 m,
resulting in much lower PZ emissions. With two stages of wash, whether the additional stage was
a water wash, an acid wash, or a trickle bed, the PZ emissions approached the detection limit of
0.1 ppb. When the trickle bed was added to the single stage wash at 3,800 hrs, the system took
more than 150 hrs to approach a steady state below 0.2 ppb.

.
 Figure 29: PZ emissions measured by PTR-TOF-MS at NCCC 2023
Aerosol-mediated emissions with RFCC flue gas
Figure 30 presents the variation in PZ emissions (in ppb) with various flue gas configurations over
the entire TCM campaign. Operations started with MHP flue gas for the first 34 hrs, after which
the flue gas was switched to RFCC (green background), causing a sharp increase in PZ emissions
spanning four orders of magnitude. This increase was driven by aerosol present in RFCC flue gas
(8.4 x 104 particles/cm3 of size range 0.06 and 0.3 μm), which provided surfaces for condensation
of solvent and degradation product vapors. Even with growth in the absorber, the aerosol was
small enough to bypass the demisters in the absorber and water wash sections and was
subsequently emitted and detected by the PTR-TOF-MS.
At TCM, the daily average amine emission limit is set at 10 ppm, so during the campaign
conservative operational measures were typically triggered when emissions exceeded 1 ppm.
These measures included increasing the number of water wash stages, raising trickle bed flow, and
adjusting lean solvent and intercooler temperatures, among other process modifications. While
such measures caused frequent fluctuations in PZ emissions during the RFCC periods, they

 33

remained consistently elevated, typically ranging from 1–5 ppm, reflecting the strong influence of
aerosol-mediated emissions.

 Figure 30: PZ (in ppb) emissions over the entire TCM campaign. Background colors
represent various flue gas configurations, the two vertical lines mark the start of reclaiming
 events, and the dotted red line denotes the 10-ppm emission limit for PZ.
Effect of water wash on PZ emissions at TCM
Figure 31 shows a detailed view of the first 225 hrs from Figure 30, with background colors
indicating different flue gas and water wash configurations. TCM was initially operated with MHP
flue gas (pink background) for the first 35 minutes before shifting to RFCC flue gas (denoted by
multiple shades of green). White bands reflect "transition” periods between changes in operating
conditions.
The first operation of RFCC flue gas operation began at 35 hrs with a 2-stage water wash
configuration (both top and bottom stages active). When the bottom stage was deactivated at
66 hrs, creating a 1-stage configuration, PZ emissions increased to approximately 7 ppm. To
reduce these emissions, the bottom water wash was turned on at 85 hrs, decreasing PZ to 2–3 ppm.
However, PZ remained higher than the initial 2-stage period (below 1 ppm) due to a simultaneous
increase in inlet gas flowrate. At 160 hrs, the trickle bed was activated, creating a 3-stage water
wash configuration that further reduced PZ to 1–2 ppm. At 200 hrs, turning off the trickle bed
returned the system to a 2-stage configuration, which again increased PZ emissions.

 34

Figure 31: PZ emissions for the first 225 hrs of the campaign. Background colors represent
 varying flue gas and water wash configurations.
Ammonia emissions
In both pilot plant campaigns ammonia emissions were a direct result of the oxidation conditions.
In general, ammonia was not removed by the various water wash configurations. However, a
single stage of acid wash provided greater than 99% ammonia removal at both NCCC and TCM.
Figure 11 showed the effects of oxidation conditions on the ammonia emissions at NCCC. Without
acid wash the ammonia concentration measured by FTIR in the flue gas after the water wash varied
from 1 to 20 ppm. After reclaiming, ammonia emissions were reduced to less 2 ppm.
Figure 32 shows the ammonia emissions measured by the PTR-TOF-MS in the last third of the
NCCC campaign. A 200-hr test of acid wash at 4,100 hrs reduced the ammonia emissions from
2000 ppb to 10 ppb.

 35

Figure 32: Ammonia emissions measured by PTR-TOF-MS during NCCC 2023 (Drewry et
 al., 2025)
During the TCM campaign, NO2 varied strongly with the flue gas source, driving differences in
oxidative degradation. RFCC flue gas had relatively low NO2 (0.2–1.5 ppm), while MHP flue gas
had much higher levels (15.5 ± 2.3 ppm). Dilution reduced NO2 to intermediate values of 6 ppm
for diluted MHP and 0.3–0.8 ppm for diluted RFCC.
Figure 33 demonstrates the direct link between NO2 and NH3 formation. At 660 hrs, the step
change in NO2 (green dots) was immediately followed by a corresponding step change in NH3
(blue dots). Throughout MHP operation, elevated NO2 (~16 ppm) corresponded to significantly
higher NH3 emissions compared to RFCC periods, supporting the role of NO2 in promoting
stepwise oxidative degradation of PZ and its derivatives, with NH3 as the final degradation product.
This effect was not observed during the second half of the campaign because acid wash operation
was implemented starting at 1,830 hrs, which proved highly effective in controlling NH3 emissions.
Acid wash reduced NH3 by more than 99%, decreasing its level by two orders of magnitude
compared to normal water wash operation (gray dots in Figure 33).
NH3 emissions also decreased following the two solvent reclaiming events, suggesting that
removal of accumulated degradation products, particularly amino acids, reduced the pool of
compounds available for conversion to NH3. This also indicates that NH3 formation depends on
the concentrations of both NO2 and degradation intermediates in the liquid phase.

 36

  campaign. The grey dots are NH3 during acid wash periods, and the two vertical lines
 mark the start of solvent reclaiming events.
Hazardous Air Pollutants
Five hazardous air pollutants (HAPs) were quantified by the PTR-TOF-MS in the cleaned flue gas
at NCCC. Figure 34 shows the variation of the total HAPs and the acetaldehyde in the 1,400 hrs
before reclaiming. The total HAPs varied from 50 to 300 ppb. Acetaldehyde was the most
significant; it varied from 20 ppb to 120 ppb. The HAPS emissions were generally correlated with
the ammonia emissions. The single-stage water wash removed about 50% of the HAPs.

 Figure 34: Emissions of listed Hazardous Air Pollutants (HAPs) during NCCC 2023
                                      (Drewry, 2025)

 37

Four hazardous air pollutants (HAPs), acetaldehyde, acetonitrile, formaldehyde, and methanol,
were consistently detected by the PTR-TOF-MS throughout the TCM campaign. Figure highlights
the two dominant HAPs, acetaldehyde and acetonitrile, along with total HAP concentrations,
which ranged from 20 to 600 ppb. The other two HAPs, formaldehyde and methanol, remained
below 5 ppb throughout the campaign.
Acetonitrile emissions increased immediately when MHP flue gas was introduced at 660 hrs,
indicating direct formation from NO2-promoted oxidation reactions. Acetonitrile briefly decreased
when RFCC flue gas was introduced at 924 hrs but rose again during diluted RFCC operation,
potentially due to increased dissolved oxygen from air dilution enhancing oxidation rates. Both
acetaldehyde and acetonitrile decreased following the first solvent reclaiming event, consistent
with their formation from the primary oxidation products of PZ, which are removed during
reclaiming. Their concentrations gradually increased afterward until the second reclaiming event,
which produced a weaker reduction than the first.

 Acetaldehyde

 Acetonitrile

  Figure 35: Acetaldehyde (brown) and acetonitrile (purple) at the TCM campaign. The
 shaded gray background represents total HAPs (ppb), two vertical lines mark the start of
  solvent reclaiming events, and the dotted black lines show the range of observed HAPs.
Mononitrosopiperazine (MNPZ) emissions were measured by PTR-TOF-MS in the last third of
the NCCC campaign. All the mitigation methods were on during this period, including
prescrubbing with sulfite solution to reduce inlet NO2 from 2.5 ppm to about 0.5 ppm. Figure 36
shows the effect of water wash configurations. With the two-stage wash the concentrations were
near the PTR-TOF-MS detection limit and appeared to decrease from 0.05 to 0.02 ppb with the
use of the acid wash. The largest MNPZ emission (0.3 ppb) occurred with the single-stage water
wash. Alternating measurement of the flue gas before and after the single-stage water wash
suggests that the water wash removes about 95% of the MNPZ.

 38

Figure 36: Mononitrosopiperazine concentration during operation of acid wash, two-stage,
 and single-stage water washes at NCCC 2023.
Mononitrosopiperazine (MNPZ) was the dominant nitrosamine observed during the TCM
campaign, accounting for more than 85% of the total nitrosamines and nitramines (Figure 37).
MNPZ emissions were higher during RFCC periods (5–15 ppb) compared to MHP periods (< 1.5
ppb), and about half as high during diluted RFCC conditions (2.5–7.5 ppb). MNPZ tracked PZ
suggesting that MNPZ also accumulates in the aerosol and is also removed by acid wash. The
highest MNPZ (35–55 ppb) occurred using MHP with recycled CO2, when elevated NO2 combined
with aerosol formation created conditions favorable for nitrosamine accumulation in the solvent
and in the emissions as aerosol.

 39

 PZ

 MNPZ

  Figure 37: Emissions of PZ (black) and MNPZ (orange) at TCM. The two vertical lines
 mark the start of reclaiming periods, and the dotted red line denotes the 10-ppm emission
 limit for PZ.

Ener Use and Reliabilit
Heat duty
Heat Duty at NCCC 2023
The optimized heat duty at NCCC 2023 was 2.4 GJ/tonne with 4% CO2 in the flue gas (Figure 38).
The system used the advanced stripper configuration with pump-around intercooling in the
absorber using 40 ft of solvent packing. Around March 5, 2023 the absorber was switched to
pump-around intercooling with all the solvent extracted from the sump of the absorber for both
the intercooling loops. This enhanced the rich loading in the solvent. This increase was
accompanied by a reduction in cold rich bypass that was required to minimize heat duty.
Ultimately, 8% cold rich bypass and 64% warm rich bypass minimized the heat duty to 2.4
GJ/tonne.

 40

 Figure 38: Measured heat duty during the evolutionary optimization of bypass flowrates
 for PA IC (Suresh Babu, 2023)
Heat Duty at TCM
The heat duty at TCM was evaluated with several U-Curve tests performed at specified flue gas
conditions and CO2 capture. These tests were run as a complement to other testing: mitigation
measures for solvent degradation, reclaiming, emissions reduction with water wash and acid wash.
Each test was performed with several lean solvent flowrates to generate a U-Curve. At each test
point the steam to the reboiler was adjusted to give the desired CO2 capture. The tests were
performed at a high flue gas rate that was constrained by the available flue gas supply or the steam
to the stripper. TCM has two flue gas sources shown in Table 11.
 Table 11: TCM flue gas sources
 Component RFCC MHP
                CO2 (%)                           15.7               9.9
                O2 (%)                            2.5-3.5            3.8
                H2O (%)                           5.5-7.5            6.2
                NO2 (%)                           0.5-1.5            15.7

The equipment design and operating conditions at TCM were not representative of expected
commercial practice with PZ and were expected to produce a greater heat duty. The equipment
was mostly operated as a simple stripper with no additional features for heat recovery; it did not
allow for the advanced stripper configuration that was used at NCCC. The cross-exchanger was
small and generated a large hot end approach temperature of about 15 oC, compared to 3 to 5 oC
at NCCC. The RFCC stripper was too large when operated at the elevated pressure of the PZ
solvent. It also has a poorly designed top liquid distribution, so it probably had poor mass transfer
efficiency.

 41

Most tests required the larger RFCC stripper. For 4% CO2, both the CHP and RFCC strippers were
tested. RFCC flue gas with 15% CO2 was diluted to 4% CO2 typical of NGCC conditions. The
flue gas flowrate was 36,000 Sm3/hr, and the lean solvent rate was varied to get the desired L/G.
Each data point represents a steady state average. At 96% CO2 removal, the optimum heat duty
was 3.59 GJ/t CO2 removed (Figure 39). At 99% removal (Figure 40) the optimum heat duty
increased to 4.19 GJ/t.

 conditions

 Figure 40: U-Curve test of RFCC flue gas diluted with air to 4% CO2 to represent NGCC
 conditions and run at 99% CO2 removal
U-Curve tests were also used to show that solvent performance did not change with reclaiming.
Figure 41 shows U-Curves performed before and after reclaiming, and performance remained
similar.

The CHP and RFCC strippers have similar designs and elevations except the CHP stripper has a
smaller diameter. The CHP stripper cross-sectional area is 33% of the cross-sectional area of the
RFCC stripper. The diluted MHP test was run with 38% more flue gas, so the CHP stripper ran at
more than 4 times the CO2 and flue gas feed rate per unit cross-sectional area compared to the
RFCC stripper runs using the same 4% CO2. Despite the much larger flow per unit area, the
reduced cross-sectional area of the CHP stripper provided a lower SRD, 3.2 versus 3.6 GJ/t CO2
for the RFCC stripper shown in Figure 39.
The CHP stripper had a large set of working thermocouples to assess process performance. The
results of the U-curve testing are shown in Figure 44.

 44

 Figure 44: Measured and modeled temperatures for CHP stripper
The measured values shown in Figure 44 are an average of the multiple thermocouples (typically
4 thermocouples per level) that are included in the TCM CHP stripper. A detailed rate-based model
is used for modeling performance. The rate-based model that has been validated at TCM allows
modeling advanced configurations that typically lower the SRD by 20 to 30% compared to the
simple stripper at TCM.
Reliability
Operation without plugging
PZ that is concentrated or has a low lean loading of CO2 has an elevated freeze point. A model of
freezing point as a function of amine concentration and lean loading was used to evaluate all
operating conditions. There is a large window of operability. TCM had online calculation of amine
concentration and CO2 loading that were used to make sure operations stayed at safe conditions
away from freeze points. The lean solvent line was not heat traced or insulated. TCM depends on
the Mongstad refinery to provide flue gas, steam, and power. A cold shutdown with dynamic
restart was simulated during the winter. The temperature in the lean solvent line was monitored,
and solvent was circulated every eight hours to prevent freezing. High rates of seawater cooling
in exchangers can also cause freezing, especially if solvent circulation is slow or stopped. This
occurred while simulating the cold shutdown. The TCM staff were able to use hot condensate to
clear out the plugged line.
Piperazine makeup methods
TCM and Honeywell worked together to develop a protocol for unloading concentrated amine to
supply the unit. The method that TCM proposed was to prefill the tank with water so that amine
could be stored at an intermediate concentration to allow a greater amount of solvent to be stored
at reasonable temperatures. The solvent tank had an external steam heater and pump to circulate
stored solvent, which worked well. Uninsulated transfer lines were heated with condensate before
use, either to unload the truck or to send solvent from the solvent storage tank to the system. After
solvent was transferred, the lines were flushed with condensate.

 45

Conclusions
Oxidation mechanisms
NO2 catalyzes the oxidation of PZ with the loss of 1.6–8 mols PZ/mol NO2. This mechanism was
dominant in the TCM and NCCC pilot plant data with 0.5 to 15 ppm NO2 in the flue gas.
In bench-scale experiments in the absence of NO2, the catalysis of amine oxidation by Fe3+/2+
increases with the degradation of the solvent. The rate of amine oxidation is increased by the
accumulation of amino acids that complex, solubilize, and activate Fe3+/2+.
In bench-scale experiments with temperature cycling from absorber to stripper conditions, the
oxidation rate of PZ increases with the stripper temperature.
Emissions
Without aerosol, PZ emissions are reduced to < 200 ppb with a single stage wash and to < 1 ppb
with a 2-stage wash.
Emissions of NH3, mononitrosopiperazine (MNPZ), and HAPs are manageable. With no aerosol
and low NO2, MNPZ < 1 ppb. With aerosol or high NO2, MNPZ is usually < 10 ppb. Ammonia
emissions can be managed to < 2 ppm by acid wash or thermal reclaiming. Total hazardous air
pollutants (HAPs) were < 600 ppb even with 16 ppm NO2. However, at TCM the combination of
inlet aerosol and high NO2 resulted in 35–55 ppb MNPZ.
Inlet aerosol greatly increases amine and nitrosamine emissions. At TCM the Brownian diffusion
unit (BDU) left 105 particles/cm3 at 0.06 to 0.3 m. With this aerosol, PZ emissions were reduced
from 10 ppm to 0.5–2 ppm by a three-stage water wash and increased solvent temperature.
Operations
At NGCC conditions (4% CO2) the advanced stripper at NCCC provided a heat duty of 2.45 GJ/t
CO2 removed with 96% CO2 removal.
At the less optimal design and operating conditions of TCM the optimum heat duty varied from
3.3 to 3.6 GJ/t at 96% CO2 from flue gas containing 4 to 16% CO2. With 99% removal from 4%
CO2, the heat duty was 4.2 GJ/t.
The precipitation of PZ was not a significant problem in the operations at NCCC or TCM.
At TCM the makeup of molten 60 wt % PZ was demonstrated with no significant precipitation or
plugging.

Recommended miti ation methods
Thermal reclaiming economically reduces emissions and oxidation. It removes degradation
products that would otherwise oxidize to ammonia. Reclaiming removes dissolved metals and
amino acids that solubilize and activate metals. Simple single stage batch reclaiming with PZ
operated reliably & economically at 1–2 bar. It requires 0.06 gJ/t CO2 and 0.07 kg NaOH/t CO2
with a total annualized cost of $0.78/t CO2 removed.
Nitrogen stripping readily removes dissolved oxygen and reduces oxidation with < 0.5 ppm NO2.
It is ineffective at reducing oxidation with > 0.5 ppm NO2. Nitrogen stripping requires 0.0015 t
 46

N2/t CO2 in a single pass absorber. The total annualized cost is $0.34/t CO2 with a net benefit of
$0.35/t CO2 when added to a system using thermal reclaiming. Nitrogen stripping also eliminates
O2 in the CO2 product.

 i

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document is a summary report for a pilot campaign conducted under Cooperative Agreement
DE-FE0031861, “Safeguarding Amines from Oxidation by Enabling Technologies”, during
Budget Period 3 (BP3). The University of Texas at Austin (UT) conducted a pilot campaign to
test oxidation mitigation strategies with 5 m piperazine (PZ) at the National Carbon Capture Center
(NCCC) during BP3 under Milestone 12. Modifications were made on the Pilot Solvent Testing
Unit (PSTU) at the NCCC to accommodate that campaign. Data from the campaign are
summarized in this report. Supplemental review and analyses of the data from the campaign are
included in UT graduate research assistants (GRA) quarterly reports and Ph.D. dissertations and
and Texas Carbon Management Program (TxCMP) quarterly reports.

Summary of Findings
Significant findings from the 2022–2023 NCCC pilot campaign include the following:

 • Prescrubbing with sulfite lowered the NO2 concentration in the flue gas entering the
 absorber from ~3.5 ppm to < 0.5 ppm; ~50% of the remaining NO2 was absorbed into the
 solvent in the absorber throughout the campaign.
 • Prescrubbing NO2 lowered oxidation of the solvent as evidenced by lower concentrations
 of degradation compounds in the liquid phase and NH3 in the water wash outlet.
 • The carbon bed prevented the accumulation of iron whenever it was brought online; spikes
 in the iron concentration were measured immediately after the carbon bed was brought
 online with fresh carbon.
 • Early measurements (< 1,000 hours) with the dissolved oxygen (DO) probe demonstrated
 that > 50% consumption of DO occurred between the absorber and DO probe location
 downstream of the rich amine tank, carbon tank, and rich amine pump.
 • The greatest number of liquid continuous phase mass transfer units (NTU) and DO removal
 were both measured when the N2 gas sparge rate in the absorber sump was < 0.05% of total
 gas traffic through the absorber column.
 • N2 sparging in the absorber sump for DO removal reduced the accumulation rate of N-
 containing PZ degradation species by 30 to 75%.
 • A possible corrosion event occurred at ~2,300 hours as evidenced by the heat stable salts
 (HSS) and dissolved metal ion data.
 • A tipping point was reached ~4,300 hours into the campaign at which time the NH3 in the
 water wash outlet underwent a steady rise; this tipping point was reflected in several other
 liquid phase parameters.
 • The total amino acids concentration was ~85 mmol/kg at the point of peak degradation
 (~4,600 hours); the amino acids were removed by > 90% through the single-stage thermal
 reclaiming event.
 • Aldehydes were measured at a total concentration corresponding to ~10% of the total
 amino acids at 4,600 hours, indicating they are reactive and will be readily oxidized to
 amino acids.
 • The NH3 formation rate accelerated during the corrosion event at 2,300 hours and at 4,300
 hours; when all mitigation methods were online, the NH3 reached 25 ppm.

 ii

• NH3 in the water wash outlet correlated to the liquid phase, with an increase in both at
 ~2,300 hours; this correspondence likely indicates that the iron catalyzed further solvent
 oxidation leading to higher NH3 concentrations.
• Reclaiming reduced NH3 from > 25 ppm to < 3 ppm in the water wash outlet.
• The acid wash was effective at reducing the NH3 concentration in the water wash outlet to
 ~10 ppb.
• PZ was reduced to ~0.1 ppb in the water wash outlet with a two-stage water wash
 configuration.
• The hazardous air pollutants (HAPs) were generally not removed by two-stage water wash
 configurations, but were lowered through thermal reclaiming of the solvent.
• Corrosion coupons generally demonstrated a weight gain in the warm rich bypass line due
 to deposition of dissolved materials originating from other areas of the plant; erosion
 corrosion was observed in an elbow in the pumparound intercooling line as evidenced by
 coupon weight gain.
• Heat loss measurements led to the following observations:
 o Heat loss from the stripper column was greatest at higher steam rates due to an
 overall hotter column;
 o Heat loss from bare metal surfaces was dominant in the regeneration system;
 o Convection-controlled heat loss dominated due to Tsurf-Tamb > Tfluid-Tsurf;
 o The average fraction of heat lost relative to steam heat rate was ~35.4%;
 o A heat duty of 2.4 GJ/tonne CO2 captured was measured at a cold rich bypass split
 fraction of 8% and a warm rich bypass split fraction of 64% while achieving up to
 98% CO2 removal;
 o Energy performance improved when the total rich bypass temperature was raised
 above the bubble point at the stripper operating pressure, giving rise to vapor and
 two-phase flow.

 iii

INTRODUCTION
The overall objective of this project is to define and evaluate strategies to minimize amine
oxidation. Bench-scale experiments and pilot activities were performed to quantify the
effectiveness of technologies to minimize oxidation of piperazine (PZ) and other second-
generation (2G) amine solvents. The technologies are being developed to support capture from
coal-fired power plants but are also relevant at conditions of gas-fired turbines. The subject work
uses the PiperaZine with the Advanced Stripper (PZAS) technology for CO2 scrubbing as the
baseline technology. The mitigation technologies being developed will reduce the cost and
environmental risk of solvent-based CO2 capture systems.
This topical report provides a review of data generated during a pilot campaign conducted with
5 m PZ at the National Carbon Capture Center (NCCC) under Cooperative Agreement DE-
FE0031861, “Safeguarding Amines from Oxidation by Enabling Technologies”. The pilot
campaign was conducted to test oxidation mitigation methods at a ~1.0 MWe scale (flue gas rate
~2,000 SCFM or ~8,000 lb/hr). NCCC is hosted by Alabama Power’s Plant Gaston, a power plant
burning both coal and natural gas in Wilsonville, Alabama.
PZAS uses 30 wt % PZ, a 2G amine for CO2 absorption. The objective of the campaign was to
test solvent oxidation mitigation methods as part of an overall effort to lower the risk of carbon
capture with amine solvents. Oxidation mitigation methods tested in the campaign included N2
gas sparging in the absorber sump for dissolved oxygen (DO) stripping, sulfite scrubbing in the
prescrubber train for NO2 removal, and carbon adsorption for removal of dissolved metal ion
complexes. On September 18, 2023, after 5,150 hours of operating time, reclaiming of the solvent
was implemented using batch vacuum distillation. A total of three solvent inventories were
processed in the reclaimer (~4,190 gallons). This document summarizes important results from
the campaign.
NCCC plant
The PZAS process flowsheet is included in this summary as Figure 1.

conditions utilized at the Separations Research Program (SRP) campaign conducted in 2021–2022
for comparison purposes.

NCCC pilot plant modifications
In preparation for the NCCC pilot campaign, modifications were made to the plant to generate a
flue gas at conditions similar to a natural gas combined cycle (NGCC) unit. The modifications
were also made to enable testing of the most promising oxidation mitigation strategies as
determined during previous pilot campaigns and laboratory testing. These modifications were
completed by November 15, 2022 and are listed in Table 2.

Kick-off of the campaign occurred on December 20, 2022. The campaign was completed on
October 20, 2023 after 5,900 hours of operations.
Oxidation mitigation methods
The campaign was designed to study solvent oxidation mitigation technologies, solvent
reclaiming, and volatile emissions management through various water wash configurations.
Specific plant modifications were made at NCCC to test oxidation mitigation methods that address
three important sources of amine oxidation. These sources include oxygen (8 to 12%) and NO2
(0.5 to 5 ppm) in the flue gas, and dissolved metal ions (Fe and Cr) that are present in the solvent
from equipment corrosion. The respective methods to address these sources include stripping of
DO in the absorber sump with N2 gas, pre-scrubbing of NO2 with sulfite upstream of the absorber,
and adsorption of dissolved metal ion complexes in the solvent with granular activated carbon
(GAC). Thermal reclaiming was performed towards the end of the NCCC campaign (5,150 h) to
remove degradation products and other impurities that accumulated in the PZ solvent.
Pilot campaign conditions
Solvent formulation
The PZ solvent inventory utilized in the campaign was created by combining the following:

 • Approximately 500 gallons of degraded PZ solvent from a previous NCCC pilot campaign;
 • Six drums at 460 lb/drum of fresh 99 wt % PZ purchased from DOW Chemical on July 7,
 2022, shipped to site, and dissolved into the system December 14–21, 2022;
 • 440 gallons of ~30 wt % PZ solvent shipped in two totes from UT from a previous pilot
 campaign and combined with water and the main inventory PZ December 14–21, 2022;
 • Six drums at 460 lb/drum of fresh 99 wt % PZ purchased from DOW Chemical on January
 5, 2023, shipped to site, and dissolved into the system February 27–28, 2023; and
 • Five bags at 50 kg/bag fresh PZ flakes (95 wt % ) dissolved into the solvent inventory as
 make-up for inadvertent losses on February 23, 2024.
Schedule and timeline of operations
Throughout the 2022–2023 NCCC campaign, step changes in process conditions were
implemented to test the efficacy of different oxidation mitigation strategies. Table 3 lists the major
changes made during the pilot campaign including the transfer of solvent into and out of the
 11

system, and step changes to oxidation mitigation methods. All plots of data collected during the
pilot campaign reflect when these oxidation mitigations methods were brought online and taken
offline. Those conditions are reflected through plotting with colored background for each
condition.

As listed in Table 3, the transfer of solvent into the PSTU occurred on December 12, 2022, with
solvent circulation started on December 14. Oxidation mitigation methods were brought online
by December 21, 2022, but by December 23, freezing weather forced NCCC operations staff to
shut the plant down. A combination of factors led to the decision by NCCC staff to transfer the
solvent to totes for temporary storage. Those factors included damage to the plant that occurred
during the freeze event, and a planned outage for security and controls system software upgrades
that was to occur later in January and end in February 2023. On February 20, 2023, the solvent
was transferred back to the PSTU, and by February 21, circulation was restarted with all oxidation
mitigation methods brought online. At that time, the estimated total operating time was only 234
hours, which was a short time period for oxidation to have occurred.
Oxidation mitigation methods were taken offline one by one in a stepwise fashion with extended
intervals between each change in order to allow sufficient time and data collection to observe
changes in oxidation of the solvent. As noted, all mitigation methods were brought online during
the pilot restart on February 21, 2023. On April 5, 2023, the N2 gas sparging for DO stripping in
the absorber sump was taken offline. By that time, the campaign had been in steady state operation
for 1,260 hours. On May 9, 2023, the NO2 prescrubber was taken offline with a total of 2,038
hours of steady state operations. On June 15, 2023, the carbon bed was taken offline with a total
of 2,890 hours of steady state operations. After another 832 hours for a total of 3,722 hours of
steady state runtime, all three mitigation methods were brought back online and remained online
through the end of the campaign. The total number of steady state operating hours estimated for
the campaign was 5,901. Reclaiming was implemented from September 18–21, 2023 as listed in
Table 3.
The steady state operation of the mitigation methods was interrupted as discussed below, with one
major interruption occurring with the carbon bed operation. On March 23, 2023 with 950 hours
of steady state operations having passed, the carbon bed vessel lost circulation, resulting in an
immediate shutdown. On March 23, the carbon bed was brought back online with fresh carbon
canisters. This downtime in the carbon bed led to approximately 170 hours of lost operating time
for carbon adsorption of degradation products and dissolved metal ions. This period of time is not
reflected in Table 3. However, the total number of steady state hours for the carbon bed operation
do reflect this temporary outage.
The plant experienced a freeze event which shut down operations on December 23, 2022. On
December 27, NCCC staff started circulating solvent again to determine the general condition of
the plant. While doing so, ~315 gallons of solvent was inadvertently released from the plant
 12

through the flue gas heater inlet flue gas drain and sent to a storm drain. A portion of the released
PZ solvent was recovered with stormwater in a more dilute form; that material was pumped from
the sump into four totes, two of which contained solvent at 5.95 and 7.13 wt % PZ, respectively.
Solvent contained in these two totes was deemed sufficiently concentrated for reincorporation into
the overall solvent inventory once plant operations were resumed in mid-February 2023. As of
January 9, 2023, 1,380 gallons of solvent at 24.4 wt % were held in TK20401. Because this amount
was short of that needed to complete the campaign, an additional six drums of 68 wt % PZ were
ordered and delivered to the site on January 17, 2023 for dissolution into the campaign solvent
inventory from February 27 to 28, 2023. Prior to that (February 23, 2023), five bags of PZ had
been dissolved into the inventory.

Sample collection
Throughout the pilot campaign, lean and rich amine samples were collected daily Monday through
Friday, with samples typically collected between 5 and 9 am. Samples were not collected on
weekends unless specifically requested.
Analytical methods
UT analyzed all collected lean and rich amine liquid samples using cation and anion
chromatography (IC) with a Dionex ICS 6000 DP instrument, high performance liquid
chromatography (HPLC) using a Dionex UltiMate 3000 instrument, IC with a high performance
amperometric detector (HPAE-PAD) on a Dionex ICS 6000 SP, inductively coupled plasma (ICP),
and UV-Vis absorbance on a Thermo NanoDrop. An FTIR (Gasmet), a direct NO2 measurement
instrument (American Ecotech), a 2010 Ionicon proton transfer reaction time-of-flight mass
spectrometer 8000 (PTR-TOF-MS), and an AMI trace oxygen analyzer were utilized for real-time
analyses of gas phase streams throughout the campaign. Table 4 lists the key analytical parameters
and associated methods.

Campaign results
This section provides a review of the campaign data. All NCCC data from both the lean and rich
amine sample analyses will be uploaded to the DOE website excel spreadsheets separate from this
 14

report. Because the solvent inventory was created from different sources including a previous
pilot campaign at the SRP, it was partially oxidized at the start. As noted above, fresh PZ was
utilized to make up for solvent losses associated with the winter freeze event (December 2022).
As such, the aggregate operating time considered when evaluating and plotting all of the newly
generated data assumed a start date of December 12, 2022 with zero hours of oxidation time on
the solvent inventory. However, the plotted data indicate that some oxidation of the inventory had
occurred before December 12, 2022; some residual degradation products were present from the
portion of the solvent transferred from the 2021–2022 SRP campaign.
Liquid sample analyses
For the next several data plots, a standard method of denoting the oxidation mitigation methods is
used. A colored background indicates which mitigation methods were online during each period.
The plots show an initial shakedown period with a light green background. During this period, the
mitigation methods were being tested and brought online at steady state. During the period roughly
corresponding to February 21 through April 5, 2023 (~1,030 hours) (grey shaded background), all
three oxidation mitigation methods were online (NO2 prescrubbing, N2 sparging, and carbon
treating). During the period that extended to May 9, 2023 (salmon colored background), N2
sparging for DO removal was offline; this period covered ~780 hours. The only mitigation method
online during the next period, which covered ~850 hours up to June 15, 2023 (yellow background)
was the carbon bed. No mitigation method was online from June 15 to July 20, 2023 (white
background), but at the end of that period, all three mitigation methods were brought back online
and remained online through the end of the campaign. This period is noted in the figures with a
grey shaded background.
LiOH was added to the solvent used during the 2021–2022 SRP campaign to serve as a tracer.
The 440 gallons of degraded solvent from SRP in Austin, Texas had sufficient lithium to create an
NCCC solvent inventory concentration of ~2.3 mmol/kg at the start of the campaign (December
13, 2022) (Figure 2). This was the concentration before any shutdowns and solvent losses
occurred. On March 1, 2023, the concentration was 1.3 mmol/kg. This reduced lithium
concentration 2½ months later was due to the addition of fresh solvent to make up for losses in
solvent inventory associated with the winter freeze event in December 2022. However, as was
found following the SRP campaign, inconsistencies in the tracer concentration data generated by
the inductively coupled plasma (ICP) method prevented their use for inventory estimation and/or
data normalization from the NCCC campaign. The concentration of other metal ions were
determined using ICP analyses.

 15

 Figure 2: Li and K measured by ICP
The concentration of PZ was generally maintained at 30 wt % throughout the campaign, but
underwent short-term fluctuations due to PZ losses from emissions, spillage, and water balance
issues. The PZ concentration was measured using four methods: online titration, offline titration,
gas chromatography (GC) (onsite), and cation chromatography (IC). Figure 3 is a parity plot of
the online titration, offline titration, and GC data plotted against the IC measurements throughout
the campaign. The plot depicts a general bias upward when PZ is measured by titration and GC
compared to IC. The offline titration data match the IC data best. The online titration and GC
data were the primary data sets utilized by the on-site operators in monitoring the overall
concentration of the solvent.

 16

  Figure 3: PZ measured by online tritration, offline titratiom, and gas chromatography
A step change loss of PZ was observed when reclaiming was implemented late in the campaign.
Figure 4 is a plot of the total mass of PZ in the system estimated throughout the campaign from
the liquid inventory volume of the pilot system and measurements of PZ concentration made with
IC. The plot reflects losses of PZ mass throughout the campaign due to emissions, degradation,
and plant upsets resulting in liquid releases; the PZ concentration was generally maintained at 28
to 30 wt % throughout the campaign until reclaiming. No fresh PZ was added to the inventory
after February 2023. During reclaiming that took place ~5,150 hours into the campaign, > 30% of
the remaining PZ inventory at that time was lost in the reclaiming process through insufficient
boiloff from the reclaimer loop, and operational issues related to power loss. Immediately after
the reclaiming event was completed, the plant operated with a dilute form of the solvent, at ~17
wt % PZ instead of the typical 28 to 30 wt %. This was due to loss of PZ during the reclaiming
process and the decision not to add fresh PZ. In order to maintain sufficient volume of solvent in
the absorber and stripper sumps while also pumping solvent through the piping and packing, the
solvent was maintained in dilute form (17 wt %). During the period immediately following
reclaiming through the end of the campaign, the lower PZ concentration resulted in a lower CO 2
removal at a given L/G.

 17

 Figure 4: Total PZ mass calculated from IC measurements and inventory volume
Data analysis
UV-Vis
UV measurements at 320 nm, reported as absorbance units (AU), were made with two instruments.
UT loaned a Nandrop UV-Vis instrument to NCCC staff to use in on-site measurements. Samples
were typically batched for measurements to be made on the Nanodrop instrument at the end of
each week, but generally within five days of collection. Samples were also submitted to UT Austin
for off-site measurements to be made with a Cary instrument on the UT main campus, typically
four to six weeks after sample collection. Figure 5 is a plot of those data.
The plot reflects the general trend of increasing UV absorbance at 320 nm as the oxidation
mitigation methods were taken offline. Previous work at UT has determined that absorbance at
320 nm corresponds to dissolved metal ions in solution in addition to organic degradation products.
During the period with carbon treating online, the UV absorbance underwent an accelerated
increase at ~2,300 hours and trended rapidly upward. Data discussed later in this report indicate
that a possible corrosion event occurred around this time that resulted in a higher rate of oxidation.
The absorbance was reduced to < 2 AU with reclaiming. The rapid increase in absorbance at
~4,100 hours is consistent with the data presented in subsequent plots in this report. The data in
the plot also demonstrate the immediate reduction in the UV-Vis absorbance upon reclaiming at
~5,100 hours into the campaign. This reduction is consistent with the removal of dissolved metal
ions as measured by ICP (Figure 12).
