Formate and total formate
The formate (green), total formate (red), and N-formyl PZ (NFPZ) data for the rich and lean amine
samples are plotted in Figure 6. Formate was quantified by anion IC while total formate was
quantified by base treating the samples with NaOH, storing them for a minimum of 24 hours, then
performing anion IC analyses. NFPZ was quantified by cation IC.
As noted earlier, all mitigation methods were online until ~1,250 hours, so the NO2 was typically
scrubbed from 2.5 ppm at the natural gas boiler header to < 0.5 ppm. In general, the concentration
of total formate was ~2X that of formate, indicating that formyl amides were present at equivalent
concentrations throughout the campaign. The greatest concentration of total formate reached
during the campaign was ~85 mmols/kg immediately before reclaiming was implemented (~5,100
hours). At that time, the concentration of NFPZ was ~65 mmols/kg, which is about 75% of the
total formate, indicating that other formyl amides comprised ~20 mmols/kg in solution.
Reclaiming was expected to remove > 90% of the total formate and all other heat stable salts (HSS)
as they have a much lower volatility than PZ. The thermal reclaimer was designed to recover the
PZ with water in the distillate phase for return to the PSTU.

 19

 Figure 6: Formate, total formate, and NFPZ
HSS including formate measured in both the rich and lean samples are presented in Figure 7. The
lean sample data are plotted as circles and the rich data as squares. The plot starts at 400 hours
into the campaign and is on a log-linear basis. The HSS include formate, acetate, glycolate,
oxalate, and propionate. Nitrate is also plotted in Figure 7. With the exception of glycolate, the
overall trend for the HSS was an increase throughout the first 3,700 hours of the campaign,
followed by a leveling off when all three oxidation mitigation methods were online (grey shading).
A rapid increase in HSS formation rate was observed ~4,300 hours into the campaign. The only
species that did not exhibit the same rapid upward increase was nitrate, which is consistent with it
not being a direct product of amine oxidation. All HSS species plus nitrate were reduced by > 90%
with reclaiming, which is a reflection of their having very low volatility and being easily removed.
A rapid increase in formation of all HSS ~2,300 hours into the campaign was not clearly observed;
it was expected that their concentrations would mirror those observed for NFPZ and UV-Vis.
Finally, most of the HSS components exhibited a small difference in concentration between the
rich and lean samples, with the lean samples exhibiting the slightly higher concentration. The one
exception is formate, which exhibited a systematic bias between the two sets of data, with the lean
samples exhibiting 30 to 50% higher concentration than the rich throughout a large portion of the
campaign. Two explanations exist for the lean formate samples being higher than the rich. One
is the removal of CO2 in the stripper resulting in a 6% concentrating effect when going from rich
(0.4 mols CO2/mol alk) to lean (0.18 mols CO2/mol alk). Another possible reason that the lean
sample formate concentration is higher than the rich is that the equilibrium between free formate
and formate bound as amides (NFPZ) is shifted away from amides and towards the formation of
free formate after passing through the stripper (lean circuit).

 20

 Figure 7: All heat stable salts (HSS)
Figure 8 shows the major degradation products of PZ measured using cation IC with their
concentrations normalized to PZ also measured by IC. The plot includes ethylenediamine (EDA),
NFPZ, and oxopiperazine (OPZ). The data demonstrate the general increase in concentration of
these PZ oxidation products for the first 3,000 hours of the campaign. The accelerated increase in
concentrations ~2,300 hours into the campaign corresponds to the same increase observed in the
UV-Vis data (Figure 5). The possible corrosion event would have released a greater concentration
of dissolved metal ions into solution, catalysing solvent oxidation and accelerating the formation
of these degradation species. Those dissolved metal ion data are presented in a separate plot.
One exception to the overall trend exists in the EDA data, which exhibited a reversal and
subsequent decrease approximately 3,200 hours into the campaign. This was a period of the
campaign when no mitigation methods were online (white background). This reversal in
concentration reflects the degradation of EDA itself; in past pilot campaigns as well as in the lab,
UT has demonstrated that EDA will eventually oxidize as fast or faster than it is being formed,
and thus decrease in concentration in solvent samples. When all mitigation methods were brought
back online (~3,600 hours) (grey background), all species exhibited a leveling off in concentration,
with the OPZ continuously decreasing in concentration starting ~200 hours after the mitigation
methods were brought online through the end of the campaign. This trend is consistent with the
amino acid data presented later in this report (Figure 9). Finally, EDA and NFPZ were also
removed through thermal reclaiming, although EDA was already undergoing a decrease before
reclaiming was implemented. EDA volatility is similar to that of PZ, so its removal through single
stage thermal reclaiming was not expected to be effective. In a follow-up campaign at Technology

 21

Centre Mongstad (TCM), EDA was removed at 20 to 50% while NFPZ with its lower volatility
was removed 75 to 90%.

 Figure 8: EDA, NFPZ, and OPZ measured using cation IC and normalized to PZ
The amino acids that were consistently identified and quantified in pilot samples of PZ include
aminoethyl glycine (AEG), oxopiperazine amino acid (3OPZA), glycine (GLY), and piperazin-1-
yl-amino acid (PZAA) (Figure 9). These were measured as the free amino acids in unhydrolyzed
samples. The data demonstrate that the amino acids started forming very early in the campaign
including when all mitigation methods were online up to 1,200 hours into the campaign. OPZA
and PZAA were present at the greatest concentrations among all amino acids, with GLY present
at the lowest. When no oxidation mitigation methods were online (white background), AEG and
GLY experienced a decrease, likely as a result of being oxidized in a similar fashion to PZ and
EDA. The total of all amino acids in Figure 9 is ~85 mmol/kg at peak concentration (4,600 hours).
As discussed above in reference to Figure 8, OPZ and AEG are present in solution at equilibrium
with each other because the OPZ will hydrolyze to AEG. When either species degrades or is
reduced, both should exhibit an overall reduction. This phenomenon is evident in Figures 8, 9,
and 10. Approximately 700 hours after all mitigation methods were brought back online (~3,650
hours), the concentration of AEG underwent a decrease up through the time of reclaiming, but
finally started to increase afterwards. The AEG decrease was followed by an increase in the GLY
concentration. Because their volatility is lower than PZ, all amino acids were removed by > 90%
through the thermal reclaimer late in the campaign (~5,100 hours).
AEG will be partially hydrolyzed in solution, reaching an equilibrium with OPZ. As one or other
of these two species is oxidized, both will decrease in concentration. The downward trend in OPZ
during the period when all oxidation mitigation methods were online is likely an indication that
 22

the methods are limiting the formation of and/or removing both OPZ and AEG at rates greater than
they are being formed (Figure 10). Immediately on reclaiming, the OPZ was removed to below
detection as measured by cation IC. Although the reclaimer at NCCC did not include caustic
treatment, OPZ has a lower volatility than PZ and was easily removed through the single stage
thermal reclaiming process (Plantz, 2025). While the formation of total AEG in solution resumed
after reclaiming, the data in Figure 10 indicate that OPZ did not. These two results are inconsistent.
This inconsistency may be tied to the overall lower PZ concentration (~17%) required for
operations after reclaiming.

 Figure 9: Free amino acids analyzed using HPAE-PAD instrument at UT

 23

 Figure 10: AEG, OPZ, and AEG + OPZ by HPAE-PAD
Figure 11 is a plot of formaldehyde measured in the rich samples and acetaldehyde measured in
the lean samples starting 400 hours into the campaign. Both species undergo a steady increase in
concentration, with a slight increase in the formation rate at ~2,300 hours. This uptick in formation
rate at ~2,300 hours was observed in much of the previous data (Figures 5, 8, and 11) and has been
attributed to a corrosion event. When no mitigation methods were online (white background), the
rate of formaldehyde formation is mostly steady with outlier data points indicating a possible
decrease. However, acetaldehyde exhibits a slight uptick in its formation rate at ~2,900 hours,
indicating the mitigation methods were likely effective at inhibiting its formation. Both aldehydes
are expected to have lower volatility than PZ and were, therefore, easily removed by the reclaimer.
At 4,600 hours, which corresponds to the time where the greatest total amino acid concentration
occurred in the NCCC 2023 campaign, the total concentration of formaldehyde and acetaldehyde
was ~9.4 mmol/kg. This is approximately 10% of the total amino acid concentration at this point
in time. With the aldehydes likely a step in the pathway of amino acid degradation or formation
(acetaldehyde), this lower total aldehyde concentration indicates that they are fairly reactive and,
therefore, more short lived in oxidized amine solvents.

 24

 Figure 11: Aldehydes by HPLC in NCCC samples
Dissolved metal ions
Log-linear plots of dissolved metal ions including iron, nickel, chromium, and manganese (Figure
12) and cadmium, cobalt, copper, vanadium, and zinc (Figure 13), all measured by ICP in the rich
and lean samples are included below. The plots start at 400 hours and continue through the end of
the campaign. Iron, nickel, and manganese were present at the greatest concentrations among all
dissolved metal ions. An upward shift in the concentration of all dissolved metal ions was
observed at ~2,300 hours. The same upward shift was observed in several of the other liquid phase
analyses (Figures 5, 6, 8, and 11) and is likely indicative of a corrosion event that resulted in a loss
of the process equipment passivating layer. The root cause of this corrosion event is unknown,
but may have been the result of a shift in the equilibrium between the liquid phase dissolved metal
ion concentrations and the solid phase (process equipment and precipitates already present).

 25

 Figure 12: Dissolved metal ions (Cr, Fe, Mn, and Ni) by ICP in NCCC samples

 Figure 13: Dissolved metal ions (Cd, Co, Cu, V, and Zn) by ICP in NCCC samples
Gas phase analyses
Figure 14 presents the absorber and water wash process flowsheets utilized throughout the
campaign. The upper left diagram depicts the single-stage water wash, which consisted of the
 26

separate water wash vessel with 10 feet of structured packing (Mellapak 252Y), with the water
wash loop draining to the rich amine storage tank. During operations with this configuration a
slight trickle bed effect was observed through solvent drainage through the top bed and Bed 3; the
liquid was primarily water, which condensed in the upper beds as the flue gas passed up through
the column. The bottom left diagram is the two-stage configuration using the separate water wash
column and Bed 3 with 20 feet of structured packing (Mellapak 252Y). In this configuration, the
water wash loop drained from the separate water wash column to a tank in the Bed 3 circuit. The
Bed 3 circuit consisted of storage, pumping, and cooling systems associated with the Direct
Contact Cooler (DCC), which was not in operation during the campaign. The upper right diagram
depicts the use of the separate water wash column and the trickle bed with 10 feet of gauze packing
(Sulzer BXPlus). In this configuration, water drained from the water wash column to the trickle
bed. The lower right diagram depicts the operation where the separate water wash column was
converted to an acid wash. The liquid inventory in this circuit was converted to an acid solution
(~1% H2SO4), and a closed loop was used to prevent commingling of the acid solution with the
amine solvent. Bed 3 of the column was operated as a single-stage water wash to manage amine
emissions and maintain overall system water balance.

 Figure 14: Absorber water wash configurations at NCCC
The concentration of gas phase constituents was continuously measured with a Fourier-transform
infrared (FTIR) spectrometer downstream of the absorber water wash outlet. Figure 15 is a plot
 27

of NH3 in the water wash outlet for the entire campaign. The data reflect the same patterns
observed in the liquid phase data where oxidation to NH3 increases over time, then accelerates at
~2,300 hours, possibly as a result of a corrosion event. During the period with no oxidation
mitigation methods online (white background), the NH3 concentration increased at a higher rate
than observed during the previous period. When all mitigation methods were brought back online
at ~3,600 hours, NH3 decreased. At ~4,300 hours, NH3 increased at a higher rate than during all
previous periods despite all oxidation mitigation methods being online, reaching almost 25 ppm
in the water wash outlet gas stream. The same effect is reflected in the NFPZ (Figure 6), formate
and acetate (Figure 7), EDA (Figure 8), GLY (Figure 9), and dissolved metal ion data (Figures 12
and 13). The only species to demonstrate an opposite effect were AEG and PZAA, both of which
trended downward around 4,300 hours, indicating that these species likely oxidized with greater
metal ion data in solution (Figures 12 and 13). Reclaiming reduced NH3 in the water wash outlet
to < 3 ppm which was similar to the concentrations measured at the beginning of the campaign.

 Figure 15: NH3 measured by FTIR in the absorber water wash outlet gas
Figure 16 is a co-plot of several measured parameters including NH3, Fe, NFPZ, and MPZ and is
intended to demonstrate the correlation between the NH3 concentration in the water wash outlet
and concentration of degradation and corrosion species in the solvent. The corrosion event at
~2,300 hours discussed above is evident in both the NH3 and the iron data, but not reflected in the
NFPZ and MPZ data. The significance of this observation is that the iron increased in
concentration and likely catalyzed the oxidation of degradation intermediates to NH3, but PZ
derivatives were less susceptible to this catalytic behavior; neither NFPZ nor MPZ dropped or
increased immediately following the corrosion event.

 28

 Figure 16: Co-plot of NH3, Fe, NFPZ, and MPZ in NCCC samples
Proton transfer time-of-flight mass spectrometer (PTR-TOF-MS)
A proton transfer time-of-flight mass spectrometer (PTR-TOF-MS) was installed at two different
locations starting at approximately 3,700 hours. This instrument is generally considered to provide
more accurate measurement of NH3 than the FTIR due to overlapping spectra in the FTIR. Its
limit of detection for most species of interest for this work is < 10 ppb. Figure 17 is a log-linear
plot of NH3 (ppb) measured by PTR-TOF-MS starting 3,700 hours into the campaign, soon after
the PTR-TOF-MS was brought online. The PTR-TOF-MS was moved from the water wash outlet
to the water wash inlet at times throughout the campaign. In Figure 16, the inlet data appear in
blue while the outlet data appear in black.
The data in the figure demonstrate the effectiveness of the acid wash in reducing NH3 at the water
wash outlet (red background), with a three orders of magnitude reduction from < 10 ppm to < 0.02
ppm. The two-stage water wash (blue background) was generally ineffective at reducing the NH3
concentration in the water wash outlet, as evidenced by little difference in the water wash inlet and
outlet data after 4,300 hours. As also noted in the FTIR data, when thermal reclaiming was
implemented at ~5,100 hours, the NH3 in the water wash outlet was reduced to levels observed
earlier in the campaign (< 2 ppm).

 29

  Figure 17: NH3 measured by PTR-TOF-MS in the absorber water wash inlet/outlet gas
Figure 18 is a log-linear plot of the PZ concentration measured by PTR-TOF-MS at the water wash
inlet and outlet locations. Unlike the NH3 concentration, PZ was reduced by the water wash, with
the two-stage wash configurations reducing PZ to ~0.1 ppb at the water wash outlet. The same
data after 4,400 hours reflect a difference between the inlet and outlet locations, with a two orders
of magnitude reduction when the two-stage configuration was online. After thermal reclaiming
was implemented, the concentration of PZ at the water wash outlet was lower than the earlier part
of the campaign, largely as a consequence of operating with a lower overall PZ concentration in
the liquid phase (~17 wt %). The plot also demonstrates that a single stage trickle bed was effective
at reducing the concentration of PZ at the water wash outlet (3,850 hours).

 30

 Figure 18: PZ measured by PTR-TOF-MS in the absorber water wash inlet/outlet gas
The concentrations of hazardous air pollutants (HAPs) were measured in the water wash inlet and
outlet locations and plotted in Figure 19. The “total HAPs” in the figure are comprised of
formaldehyde, acetaldehyde, acetonitrile, acetamide, and methanol. The figure demonstrates that
the total HAPs and acetaldehyde were minimally removed by the two-stage water wash
configuration when the oxidation mitigation methods were on (tan background). However, the
greatest concentrations of acetaldehyde and total HAPs were both reached before reclaiming
(~5,150 hours) and were ~175 ppb and ~275 ppb, respectively.

 31

 Figure 19: HAPs measured by PTR-TOF-MS in the absorber water wash inlet/outlet gas.
 Total HAPs include acetaldehyde, acetonitrile, acetamide, formaldehyde, and methanol.
Emissions profiles were prepared for four different time-averaged periods, three of which represent

post-reclaiming. The green bar represents the entirety of the acid washing period. Compounds
were grouped as follows: (a) ammonia, (b) combined amines, (c) other nitrogen-containing species
such as slightly acidic nitrosamines, pyrazines, acetamide, and pyrroles, and (d) combined HAPs.
Combined amine species include PZ, MPZ, ethyl piperazine (EPZ), EDA, N-
methylethylenediamine (MEDA), NFPZ, 1-(2-aminoethyl)piperazine (AEP), methylamine,
ethylamine, and other PZ derivatives. Reported emissions for these periods are shown on a log
scale in Figure 20.
For all periods except acid washing the dominant emissions were ammonia, followed by combined

after reclaiming. The other nitrogen-containing species were reduced by an order of magnitude
through thermal reclaiming. These emission groupings are broken down into individual
compounds in Figures 20 through 22.

Corrosion
Corrosion coupons were utilized throughout the plant during NCCC 2023. Data collection
generally started after the commissioning activities with water were concluded, but placement of
corrosion coupons in the plant occurred at the start of the water tests; these coupons were retrieved
at the conclusion of the commissioning, and prior to charging the plant with solvent. New coupons
were then installed in the plant for the solvent testing campaign.
Figure 23 depicts the location and type of corrosion coupons installed before and during the NCCC
pilot campaign. The figure also includes the general process fluid conditions for each coupon.
Square symbols indicate locations with a strip corrosion coupon and round symbols indicate disc
corrosion coupons. The red symbols indicate coupons installed in elbows and blue symbols
indicate coupons installed in straight process lines or inside vertical columns.
Figure 24 provides a pictorial of results, with a picture of each coupon after removal at the end of
the campaign. Locations overlaid with a green + sign indicate locations where the coupons
experienced significant weight gain. The metal associated with the weight gain (um/yr) at each
coupon is assumed to have originated elsewhere in the plant and been deposited onto the coupon.
Only one location (WL12), the elbow in the lean amine return line immediately upstream of the
trim cooler, experienced a measureable weight loss at 130 µm/yr; as explained below, this was
likely a result of erosion corrosion in the elbow as well as this coupon being carbon steel (C1010)
 34

rather than stainless steel. Staff were unable to install/retrieve coupons at a few locations including
WL33 and WL34 due to physical interferences in the plant. Table B-1 provides a list of the coupon
results including location, type, and measured overall weight gain/loss.

 Figure 23: Corrosion coupon locations at NCCC 2022–2023

 Figure 24: Corrosion coupon results at NCCC 2022–2023
 35

Figure 25 is a plot of the weight of 304L and 316SS coupons at WL38 which were installed at the
warm rich bypass outlet location. The weight gain for the two coupons is approximately the same
at ~0.5 gm after 4,000 hours of operation. This location exhibited the highest weight gain among
all coupon locations, possibly a result of corrosion occurring throughout the system and being
redeposited in the warm rich bypass line. This process piping may also have experienced two-
phase flow from flashing as the rich amine is heated. Two-phase flow may accelerate deposition
processes in this line. The estimated corrosion/deposition rate in the 304SS and 316SS lines were
29 µm/year at WL14 (hot rich line), and ~77 µm/year at WL38 (warm rich bypass downstream of
confluence of cold and warm rich bypass lines). These results are in contrast to those measured in
the cold rich bypass and the absorber tower coupon locations.
Figure 26 is a plot of the corrosion coupon weight gain at the stripper sump (WL31). Carbon steel
(C1010) and 304SS coupons were installed at this location. The weight gain for the carbon steel
coupons was ~2X that measured for the 304SS. However, the weight gain for both stainless steel
coupons installed in the warm rich bypass line was ~10X that measured in the carbon steel and
~20X that of the 304SS coupon in the stripper sump, indicating that the deposition process is much
lower in the stripper sump.
Figure 27 is a plot of the corrosion coupon weight gain at the hot lean line (WL19). 304SS and
316SS coupons were installed at this location. The weight gain for the 304SS coupons was slightly
lower than that measured for the 316SS. However, the weight gain for both stainless steel coupons
installed in this line was ~1/2X that measured in the stripper sump and a fraction of that measured
in the hot rich line.
In many locations coupons were installed for the duration of the campaign, but in an adjacent
mount they were replaced at intervals throughout the campaign in order to isolate periods of high
corrosion had they existed. For example, in the warm lean return line (WL20) (Figure 28), the
single carbon steel coupon installed for the duration of the campaign exhibited a difference
compared to coupons replaced at intervals throughout the campaign. The lean amine flows at
comparatively high velocity in this process line. The single coupon installed at the start of the
campaign exhibited essentially no weight gain or loss, whereas the coupons installed and removed
at intervals exhibited weight loss; a slight exception to this observation is the final coupon, which
exhibited almost no weight loss, not unlike the single coupon installed for the duration. In
summary, the majority of the weight loss occurred in the middle three coupons installed from 40
to ~140 days into the corrosion measurements. Overall, the carbon steel exhibited only marginal
performance with a rate of ~1.3 µm/yr.

 36

Figure 25: Warm rich outlet (WL38) process line corrosion coupon weight gain

 Figure 26: Stripper sump corrosion coupon (WL31) weight gain

 37

 Figure 27: Hot lean corrosion coupon (WL19) weight gain

 Figure 28: Hot lean corrosion C1010 coupon (WL20) weight gain
Figure 29 is a plot of the weight loss/gain in the C1010 corrosion coupons installed in an elbow of
the pumparound intercooling loop process line (WL32). The single coupon (plotted in blue) and
the coupons installed/removed at intervals at this location exhibited approximately the same
behavior, with both losing a relatively small amount of mass throughout the campaign. The
coupon mass loss is likely a result of erosion corrosion, which prevails in process line elbows.
 38

During the last interval after ~190 days of operation, both coupons gained a small amount of mass,
indicating that the corrosion environment following thermal reclaiming had changed. Reclaiming
removed dissolved metal ions, HSSs, amino acids, and other organic degradation products. Some
of those constituents would likely have complexed with the dissolved metals at the surface of the
process piping in the pumparound intercooling loop, accelerating their removal. With those
constituents removed, the corrosion coupon weight loss would have slowed or reversed as
demonstrated by the data in Figure 29.

 Figure 29: Pumparound intercooling loop C1010 coupon (WL32) weight gain
Figure 30 is a plot of the weight loss/gain in the C1010 coupons removed from the absorber at the
tray above the lowest section of absorber packing and where the absorber intercooling line returns
to the column. The data for the coupon installed for the duration of the campaign are plotted in
blue and the data for the coupons installed/removed at intervals are plotted in orange. In both
cases, the overall coupon weight loss is minimal, and after ~100 hours, the coupons gained mass.
Following the thermal reclaiming event, the continuously installed coupon gained mass whereas
the coupon installed for that time interval lost mass. No clear explanation exists for this difference.

 39

 Figure 30: Absorber C1010 coupon (WL2) weight gain
The disc coupon installed at WL5 in the water wash column exhibited a corrosion rate of 25 µm/yr,
which was relatively high compared to elsewhere on the absorber side of the overall process. This
is likely due to the presence of water vapor and O2 in the vapor phase in the water wash column.
In summary, the corrosion data indicate that:

 • Erosion corrosion is a factor at elbows, with the highest coupon weight loss observed in a
 lean amine elbow at 130 µm/yr; the pumparound intercooler elbow also exhibited a loss in
 weight due to erosion corrosion;
 • The highest corrosion coupon weight gain was observed in the warm rich bypass outlet
 where two-phase flow is likely, with corrosion rates of 29 to 77 µm/yr measured;
 • The hot lean line corrosion weight gain was less than that measured in the stripper sump
 and a fraction of the rate measured in the hot rich line;
 • The corrosion coupon weight loss/gain was minimal throughout the absorber column, with
 corrosion rates typically in the 0.03 to 0.2 µm/yr range.
Energy performance
The energy performance of the PZAS process was measured throughout the campaign at NCCC.
Initial efforts focused on estimating heat loss from the process equipment at NCCC 2023 with
comparisons made to previous campaigns (NCCC 2018, NCCC 2019, and SRP 2021–22).
Heat loss measurements
Surface temperature measurements were made during NCCC 2023 to complement those made in
the SRP 2021–22 campaign across several locations on the hot side of the process. This was done
to study mechanisms of heat loss. This section also provides details on the measured energy

 40

performance of PZAS in the ongoing campaign and model validation for heat duty, lean loading,
and stripper temperature profile.
In the NGCC campaign at NCCC in 2023, heat loss was measured using five steady state water
recirculation runs. The energy balance boundary used for heat loss calculations included the
stripper, all heat exchangers, steam heater, and condenser. The boundary began downstream of
the rich solvent pump and ended downstream of the condenser.
Stripper overhead water vapor flowrate was measured by observing the change in the level of the
S-602 tank where the water vapor condenses. The level indicator LI20211 was used to measure
the water level in this tank, and the total height measured by L120211 was 99.4 in. A linear fit
through the data revealed the slope, which can be interpreted as the change in tank level every
minute. Using this slope, the density of water, and the cross-sectional area of the tank, a mass
flowrate of water was estimated in lb/hr. The mass balance in the heat loss boundary closed with
an average difference of about 0.5%. The heat loss measurement methodology is provided in detail
by Suresh Babu and Rochelle (2022).
Heat loss measurements were made at different steam flowrates. The amount of steam provided
to the system influences the stripper temperature. At low steam rate, the sump temperature may
be affected more than the column temperatures due to sub-saturation of water in the sump. The
rest of the stripper is cold in this case. Heat loss may, therefore, only occur from the lower part of
the stripper and the solvent heat exchangers.
At higher steam rates, the water vapor rises up the column, increasing the upper column
temperature measurements as well, but the sump temperature will remain relatively constant at the
boiling point of water and the pressure of the stripper. In this case, the exposed surface area for
heat loss increases. At sufficiently high steam rates, the entire stripper will attain equilibrium at
the boiling point of water at its pressure. Heat loss in this case will increase due to an overall
temperature increase of the stripping system. These effects are shown in Figure 31.

 41

Figure 31: Stripper sump and overhead gas temperature as a function of steam flowrate at
 NCCC; figure combines data from five campaigns at NCCC between 2018 and 2023.
The trends with water differ from typical observations made during solvent testing at the pilot
plant. With loaded PZ, there will usually be a temperature gradient in the column with a finite
height of packing. Heat loss modeled using water data is applicable to higher temperatures in the
upper sections of the column. However, the modeled heat loss with solvent will not match the
water-based measured heat loss data.
Surface temperature measurements were made using an FLIR Infrared Thermal Imaging Gun at
several high temperature locations. Figures 32 and 33 show results from one such group of
measurements. Figure 32 compares the temperature driving force for convective heat loss from
the surface of bare metal, insulation, and insertions at different locations. Heat loss from bare
metal appears to be dominant in all locations; driving force for heat loss from insulation and
insertion points was similar in almost all cases but was negligible compared to that from bare
metal.

 42

 Figure 32: Temperature driving force for heat loss from surface to ambient
The surface temperature measurement locations were positioned physically close to process
temperature measurement locations where temperature sensors were already in place. The
proximity of the two measurements allows the analysis of heat loss to be made in the form of a
ratio of temperature driving forces at different locations. The ratio of the convection-side driving
force (Tsurface – Tambient)) to fluid-side driving force (Tfluid – Tsurface) indicates the relative resistance
to heat transfer in heat loss. A higher driving force ratio indicates a higher resistance to heat loss
on the ambient side, indicating a more convection-controlled heat loss. Assumptions made in this
analysis include: (1) a constant and identical surface area for heat loss both on the fluid side and
ambient side, and (2) an equal heat flux on both sides.
Figure 33 shows the driving force ratio for the same locations shown in Figure 32. Seven out of
eleven locations analyzed showed a ratio greater than 1, and almost all these locations had a ratio
of 2 or above. This indicates that in these locations, heat loss is at least 60% controlled by natural
convection. In other locations, the ratio was ≤ 1, which could have been caused by fluctuations in
ambient conditions during measurements, fluid-side disturbances, or interference in temperature
measurement through the local cooling of surface by wind.

 43

Figure 33: Ratio between convective temperature driving force and fluid-side temperature
 driving force for heat loss
Modeling heat loss
For the modeling of heat loss, the thermodynamics and rate behavior of PZAS were calculated
using the Independence™ model developed in Aspen Plus®, which incorporates CO2 solubility,
kinetics, specific heat capacity, and amine volatility regressed in the e-NRTL framework (Frailie,
2014). The mass transfer model was developed by Song (2017) and contains the interfacial area
(ae), liquid-phase mass transfer coefficient (kL), and gas-phase mass transfer coefficient (kG) for
random packing. A correction factor of 0.16 was used in the effective area model to bridge the
deviation between modeled and measured diffusivity of PZ at high temperature (Lin, 2016).
Reactions are assumed to be at equilibrium in the stripper. The bypass flowrates, stripper sump
temperature and pressure, and heat exchanger terminal temperatures were specified to be the
measured values at NCCC. The main model outputs are heat rate, lean loading, and stripper
overhead CO2 flow rate.
Combined Heat Loss Data

Figure 34 combines the measured heat loss data from SRP and NCCC as a function of the
temperature driving force between the stripper sump and the ambient conditions. The SRP pilot
plant has a lower heat loss value than the NCCC pilot plant due to its smaller scale and steam
flowrate. There is a correlation of the measured heat loss with the temperature driving force to
ambient from the sump, which is the hottest point in the stripper. Figure 34 also shows the ranges
of sump temperature, stripper overhead temperature, and wind speed for the different water tests.
Sump temperature generally indicates the magnitude of steam flowrate. Stripper overhead
temperature is indicative of rise of steam through the stripper column, exposing the upper sections
to heat loss in addition to the sump. Wind speed shows the influence of forced convection on heat

 44

loss at different pilot plants. The data in Figure 34 can generally be explained in terms of these
three variables.
The high heat loss at the NCCC pilot plant can be primarily explained by the higher steam rates
(high sump temperature), high stripper overhead gas temperature, and seasonal changes. The red
outlier (triangle) in the NGCC 2023 campaign is explained by the high stripper overhead vapor
temperature and high wind speed compared to other data points in this campaign. The NCCC coal
campaign heat loss measurements were made in the winter. The two green triangle outliers were
a consequence of near-freezing ambient temperature in these runs. The green and red NCCC points
have a higher heat loss than the blue points primarily due to the consistently higher stripper
overhead vapor temperature. The NCCC campaign in 2019 operated at higher ambient
temperature (max 96 ℉) than the 2018 (max 60 ℉) and 2023 (max 78 ℉) campaigns at NCCC
and the SRP coal campaign in 2015 (max 83 ℉).

Figure 34: Measured heat loss as a function of temperature driving force from the stripper
  sump to ambient for five pilot plant campaigns; SRP 2015 data taken from Lin (2016).
The effect of steam rate in the heat loss analysis has been removed by normalizing heat loss with
steam flowrate. This removes the dependence of heat loss on the stripper sump temperature.
Normalized heat loss is inversely proportional to ambient temperature, as shown in Figure 35; heat
loss becomes less important at high ambient temperature primarily due to a lower driving force for
heat loss. Heat loss generally has a greater influence at NGCC conditions. The average fraction
of heat lost relative to heat rate of steam across all the campaigns is about 35.4%.
 45

 Figure 35: Heat loss normalized by steam flow removes its dependence on sump
  temperature; heat loss per unit steam flow depends inversely on ambient temperature.
Heat loss could only be weakly correlated with the variables mentioned above and therefore an
average value of heat loss was used to correct the measured gross heat rate of steam in every
campaign. For the NCCC NGCC 2023 campaign, the average value of heat loss was calculated to
be 146,245 ± 43,789 BTU/hr.
The vortex flowmeter used to measure steam flowrate at NCCC was replaced for the 2023
campaign by a V-Cone flowmeter that corrected the flowrate of the steam for temperature and
pressure. To accurately estimate the steam flowrate in this campaign, ten condensate tote
measurements were conducted during water and solvent tests, wherein the steam condensate was
allowed to accumulate in a tote and the change in mass of the tote was measured at set time
intervals. The condensate flowrate was measured using a flowmeter. Figure 36 compares the
condensate and steam flowmeter measurements with the tote measurement. Steam flowrate was
consistently 15% lower than the condensate measurement while the condensate flowrate was 10%
higher than the tote measurement. The deviation in steam flowrate may be a result of steam
conditions deviating slightly from saturation. This deviation could result in the production of
liquid water in the steam line. Ultimately, the steam flowrate measurement was used for heat loss
measurements and for the solvent runs with a constant adjustment factor of 1.154 applied to match
the condensate measurements.

 46

 Figure 36: Deviation of condensate and steam flow measurement from tote measurement
Measured Net Heat Duty

Heat duty of the stripper was calculated using an adjusted steam flowrate based on condensate
measurements. Heat duty based on the steam rate was a result of two thermodynamic effects: (1)
the latent heat of steam, and (2) the sensible heat due to a significant change in temperature from
steam side to condensate side in the steam heater. The absorber was operated with both in-and-
out (I/O) intercooling and pumparound (PA) intercooling. The operational periods for these
strategies are shown in Figure 37. Minimization of heat duty of the stripper was done through
real-time optimization. Step changes in cold and warm rich bypass flowrates were made to observe
their effect on heat duty. The direction of change that reduced the heat duty every time was pursued
until heat duty reached a minimum. During the time when the I/O intercooling configuration was
utilized, heat duty initially was 3.1 GJ/tonne at a cold rich bypass split fraction of 20% and warm
rich bypass split fraction of 40% as shown in Figure 37. From that point onward, bypass flowrates
were adjusted until the heat duty reached about 2.7 GJ/tonne at a cold rich bypass split fraction of
10% and warm rich bypass split fraction of 50%. All reported heat duty values were adjusted for
an average measured heat loss value from water tests done in this campaign.

 47

 Figure 37: Measured heat duty during evolutionary optimization of bypass flowrates
After a plant shutdown, the stripper started back up on February 22 but with PA intercooling
employed in the absorber. However, one of the PA loops did not extract solvent from the sump of
the absorber, but instead from the middle of one of the packing sections, leading to ineffective
performance compared to regular PA intercooling. The cold rich bypass at the beginning was 10%
of the total rich solvent, and the warm rich bypass was about 40%. Heat duty was about 3.6
GJ/tonne. A step change in cold rich bypass split fraction from 10% to 20% was made, which
reduced the heat duty to between 2.6 and 2.8 GJ/tonne. On March 5, the cooling in the absorber
was switched to PA intercooling with all the solvent extracted from the bottom of the packing
section for both the intercooling loops. This enhanced the rich loading in the solvent. Ultimately,
a cold rich bypass split fraction of 8% and a warm rich bypass split fraction of 64% minimized the
heat duty to 2.4 GJ/tonne. The stripper operated without issues at a bypass rate of 72% with good
energy performance in the CO2 removal range of 66–98% with most data around 97%. The sump
temperature was maintained at about 302 ℉ at a stripper pressure of 66 psig.
Heat duty variation in the campaign was a function of solvent loading. High heat duty during
startup was also a result of lower rich loading and higher lean loading, leading to a low cyclic
capacity of the solvent (Figure 38). A combination of bypass control and maximization of delta
loading lowered the heat duty of the process. In Figure 38, at about 0.2 lean loading and 0.4 rich
loading, the heat duty was between 2.4 and 2.7 GJ/tonne. The data points at 2.4 GJ/tonne were
primarily impacted by the high total rich bypass operation.

 48

 Figure 38: Measured heat duty as a function of solvent loading
A decrease in heat duty was tied to an increase in total rich bypass split fraction, as reported in
modeling studies by Suresh Babu and Rochelle (2022). This decrease in heat duty is linked to a
hotter warm rich bypass (hot rich bypass), which leads to a higher total rich bypass temperature
and a flashing total rich bypass. These effects are shown in Figure 39; a high bypass split fraction
makes the total rich bypass temperature hotter than the stripper overhead gas temperature (orange
data points). Low heat duty is associated with a negative temperature difference in the overhead
of the stripper (between -5 and -10 ℉). Enhancement of energy performance can occur if the
temperature of the total rich bypass is raised above its bubble point at the stripper pressure, giving
rise to significant vapor content in this line.

 49

 Figure 39: Effect of total rich bypass split fraction on measured heat duty
Figure 40 shows that the high total rich bypass split fractions have a higher vapor fraction in the
total rich bypass line. This is also associated with an increase in total rich bypass temperature
above the stripper overhead temperature. In contrast, a low total rich bypass split fraction brings
the total rich bypass to its bubble point (0 vapor fraction). Energy performance at low lean loading
and removal under NGCC conditions will benefit from having a total rich bypass above its bubble
point, resulting in significant vapor content.

  Figure 40: Effect of total rich bypass split fraction on vapor fraction in total rich bypass

 50

Model Predictions of Heat Duty and Lean Loading

Model prediction of heat duty is shown in Figure 41. The rich loadings obtained from either
density-alkalinity analyses in the lab or online titration performed at NCCC were used to specify
rich solvent properties in the rate-based model. In the absence of online titration data, the density-
alkalinity values were used. The figure shows that the rate-based model can accurately predict the
heat duty under the three different intercooling strategies that were used in this campaign. The
average ratio of the predicted heat duty to the measured heat duty was ~1.

 Figure 41: Model predictions and measured values of heat duty
Figure 42 compares the solvent CO2 loading measured using online titration at NCCC, density
combined with alkalinity measurements at UT Austin, and lean loading predictions made by the
rate-based model. The stripper sump temperature and pressure were specified in the rate-based
model based on time-averaged measurements made at the pilot plant at steady state. The
thermodynamic model used these data to calculate a lean loading, which was then compared to
measured lean loading values from UT Austin and NCCC. The online titration-based CO2 loading
measurements were calibrated to match those of density-viscosity, as proposed by Gao (2021).
The online titration-based CO2 measurements acted as proxies for the density-viscosity
measurements of CO2 after applying a constant 1.15 correction factor. Figure 42 demonstrates
that there is agreement between the lean loading predicted by the model and the online titration at
NCCC, with an average ratio of prediction to measurement of 1.

 51

 Figure 42: Comparison of loading values from density-alkalinity, online titration, and
 thermodynamic model.
Stripper Temperature Profile

The stripper temperature profile predicted by the rate-based model and temperature measurements
taken from six locations in the stripper is plotted in Figure 43. There is general agreement between
temperatures measured and predicted by the model. The sump temperature measurements
(triangles in upper right corner of plot) agree more with the vapor temperature predictions than
with the liquid temperature predictions. The rest of the measurements in the column also agree
more with the liquid temperature predictions. A new temperature sensor (TI40513) was added at
the pilot plant to measure the stripper overhead gas temperature at the mouth of the stripper. Data
from this temperature sensor agree more with the liquid temperatures predicted by the model and
are close to the prediction of the temperature of the total rich bypass solvent. The data indicate
that the vapor and liquid temperatures at the top of the stripper are close to each other and indicate
the presence of a mass transfer pinch.

 52

 Figure 43: Validation of stripper temperature profile for a pumparound intercooling case
Overview of oxidation mitigation methods
This section reviews the oxidation mitigation methods including NO2 prescrubbing with sulfite
and thiosulfate, N2 sparging for DO removal in the absorber sump, and carbon treating for
dissolved metal ion and degradation products removal. Table 5 lists the time periods during which
each oxidation mitigation method was online.
 Table 5: NCCC 2023 oxidation test plan

 Dec Feb 21– Apr 5– May 11– Jun 15– Jul 20– Sep Sep Sep 21–
12–23 Apr 5 May 11              Jun 15      Jul 20      Sep 11      11–18        18–21 Oct 20
Carbon Treating
 No
 NO2 Scrubbing Mitigation
  N2 Sparging
 Reclaiming

NO2 prescrubbing
NO2 prescrubbing to reduce amine oxidation was implemented during extended periods
throughout the campaign. Post-combustion coal flue gas typically has 0.5–5 ppm NO2 and 10–
100 ppm NO. NO2 is absorbed by reaction with amines to form amine radicals and nitrite (NO2-)
in the absorber. 5 m PZ absorbed ~50% of the inlet NO2 and produced nitrosamines as measured
during the 2022 SRP pilot plant campaign (Chen, 2022). The absorption product (nitrite) reacts
with PZ carbamate and produces MNPZ. MNPZ itself will thermally degrade in the stripper,
limiting its accumulation (Fine, 2015). The pilot plant results from campaigns at UT SRP, CSIRO
Tarong, and Pilot Plant 2 (PP2) demonstrated that MNPZ degraded in the high-temperature
stripper (Nielson, 2018).
 53

Prescrubbing NO2 from the flue gas was tested as a method for mitigating oxidation and controlling
MNPZ generation. NO2 prescrubbing by sulfite and thiosulfate was successfully applied in two
PZ pilot plant campaigns at NCCC in 2016 (Selinger, 2018), and 2018–19 (Suresh Babu, 2019).
The reactions are summarized in Figure 44. SO2 in the flue gas reacts with hydroxides to form
sulfite. Sulfite removes NO2 and generates nitrite and sulfite radicals. The radical propagation by
DO results in fast sulfite consumption. The addition of thiosulfate slows down the sulfite
consumption rate by competing with sulfite to react with SO5- •.
In the 2016 and 2018–19 campaigns, 90% NO2 removal required 25 mM sulfite, which was
maintained with 50 mM thiosulfate. The previous two campaigns confirmed that the NO2 removal
efficiency depended on the pH and the prescrubber sump level. A more alkaline environment (pH
> 8) suppressed the sulfite oxidation and increased the NO2 removal efficiency because of the
lower HSO3-/SO32- ratio; SO32- absorbs NO2 faster than HSO3-. The pH of the prescrubber changes
with the absorption of NO2 and CO2.
Because there was no SO2 in the flue gas in the 2023 NCCC campaign, sulfite was added to the
prescrubber to test its efficiency and to maintain the NO2 concentration entering the absorber < 0.5
ppm. The prescrubber operation included the daily addition of one-half bag (125 lb/bag) and the
weekly addition of a full bag of sodium sulfite, the addition of sodium thiosulfate as needed, and
the addition of NaOH to maintain pH at 8.5–9. The sodium sulfate concentration was maintained
< 2 M by solution removal or water addition to dilute and avoid formation of sodium precipitates;
pH and sodium concentration in the tank were prioritized over level control.

 Figure 44: NO2 prescrubbing reactions
Sulfite and thiosulfate concentrations were measured by anion IC and shown in Figure 45.
Cumulative sulfur is the summation of sulfite, thiosulfate, and sulfate. Sulfite and thiosulfate will
eventually degrade into sulfate and N-S compounds. Sulfite concentration decreases rapidly due
 54

to the absorption of NO2 and radical oxidation. In Figure 45, the sulfite degradation rate during
operation was approximately 0.8 mol/hr, equivalent to continuous consumption of 6 ppm SO2 in
the flue gas (G = 8,000 lb/hr). It was unclear whether thiosulfate was effective in reducing sulfite
degradation because the amount of thiosulfate was relatively constant. Sulfite concentration
gradually dropped to zero during the shutdown, while there was almost no change in thiosulfate
(Figure 46). The most likely sulfite oxidant was DO.
The correlation between NO2 removal and sulfite concentration is plotted in Figure 47. 90% NO2
removal required 65 mmol/kg sulfite; the data from the previous two campaigns indicated that 25
mmol/kg sulfite was sufficient to remove 90% NO2. No clear explanation exists for why more
sulfite was needed to achieve 90% removal in the current campaign.

To evaluate the effectiveness of NO2 prescrubbing, the NH3 FTIR data from the absorber outlet
were reviewed (Figures 48 and 49). NH3 generation decreased when the NO2 prescrubber was
brought online (Figure 48), confirming that removing NO2 from the flue gas before entering the
absorber reduced amine oxidation. Figure 49 demonstrates a correlation between NH3 generation
and NO2 removal efficiency, with NH3 generation increasing when the NO2 removal efficiency
was lower.

Figure 49: NH3 concentration in the absorber outlet gas during the NCCC 2023 campaign
MNPZ forms from the reaction of PZ and nitrite, which is the stoichiometric product of the NO2
absorption by PZ. MNPZ forms instantly but is not thermally stable. Both the formation and
degradation rates of MNPZ are sensitive to temperature with positive activation energies. Nitrite
will be completely converted into MNPZ when the solvent passes through the high temperature
part of a CO2 capture plant including the stripper sump. The first-order degradation reaction of
MNPZ becomes faster as the MNPZ accumulates in the system. Eventually, MNPZ will reach a
steady-state concentration determined by the NO2 concentration in the flue gas, absorption
efficiency of NO2 by PZ, flue gas flowrate, and the stripper sump temperature and volume, as
described in Equation 1. The kinetic expression for the decomposition of MNPZ is Equation 2.

 57

Equation 1 can be used to estimate the steady-state MNPZ concentration and back-calculate the
amount of NO2 absorbed by PZ.
 [𝑁𝑂2 ] × 𝜂 × 𝐺 = [𝑀𝑁𝑃𝑍]𝑠𝑠 × 𝑘𝑑𝑒𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑝 × 𝑉𝑠𝑢𝑚𝑝 (1)
 −94 𝑘𝐽 1 1
         𝑘𝑑𝑒𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 (𝑇) = 10.4 × 10−6 exp(                                       (𝑇 − 408))                                             (2)
 𝑅

where:
 [𝑁𝑂2 ]: NO2 concentration in the flue gas;
 𝜂: absorption efficiency of NO2 by PZ;
 𝐺: flue gas flowrate;
 [𝑀𝑁𝑃𝑍]𝑠𝑠 : MNPZ steady-state concentration;
 𝑘𝑑𝑒𝑐𝑜𝑚𝑝 : MNPZ decomposition rate constant;
 𝑉𝑠𝑢𝑚𝑝 : stripper sump temperature.

Figure 50 shows the MNPZ concentrations measured by HPLC. Before the pilot plant was
restarted in February 2023, fresh PZ was added to the unit resulting in dilution of the MNPZ
concentration to 1.5 mmol/kg. With the NO2 prescrubber online, NO2 entering the absorber was
controlled to ~0.5 ppm and the MNPZ concentration reached a steady-state concentration of
2 mmol/kg. Through back calculation, the 2 mmol/kg steady-state MNPZ concentration implies
that PZ absorbed 0.25 ppm NO2 in the flue gas. This corresponded to ~50% NO2 absorption as
was observed during the 2022 SRP pilot campaign with PZ (43% NO2 absorption). Figure 51 is a
plot of NH3 in the water wash outlet and of MNPZ in both the rich and lean samples throughout
the campaign. The MNPZ concentration reached steady state at the end of the first period of NO2
prescrubbing, but increased quickly after stopping NO2 prescrubbing at 2,034 hours. At 3,181
hours, the stripper sump was increased from 30% to 65% to increase the residence time at high
temperature and increase MNPZ thermal decomposition. The MNPZ concentration decreased then
reached steady state at the end of the test phase with no oxidation mitigation methods online. After
the NO2 prescrubber was brought back online at 3,735 hours, MNPZ decreased. NH3 generation
was low when all the mitigation methods were online, and increased fivefold after NO2
prescrubbing was stopped (beige background in Figure 51).

Carbon bed operations
The operation of the carbon bed as a PZ oxidation mitigation method spanned 5,000 h during the
NCCC 2023 campaign. Carbon treating was operated without other mitigation methods for 835 h
before being taken offline for 840 h. All mitigation methods were brought back online for 1,272 h.
Carbon treating was then taken offline for ~240 h so that the solvent in the carbon vessel could be
transferred to the rich amine tank, ensuring that sufficient inventory existed to perform thermal
reclaiming. The final test period was operated with all mitigation methods online for 932 h. The
activated carbon was replaced three times after 1,108 h, 3,710 h, and 5,226 h of operations.
As depicted in Figure 52, solvent traveled from the absorber to the rich storage tank (TK401). It
was then pumped at 35 gpm from TK401 through the carbon bed, where it drained by gravity back
to the storage tank. The solvent flow path is depicted in Figure 53. The upper section of the carbon
vessel had 14 poles, each of which could hold two activated charcoal canisters. For the first part
of the campaign, 11 of the poles were covered and only six canisters on three poles were used.
The 35 gpm flowrate was required to fully submerge the six canisters in solvent. Solvent flowed
radially through the perforated canisters to contact the carbon.
The carbon media was similar to that used during NCCC 2019 except both original Nowata and
third party, off-brand canisters were used in the NCCC 2023 campaign (Wu, 2022). The Nowata
11NC22 charcoal canisters contain 31 lb of 8x30 mesh lignite-based granular activated carbon
(Clear Solutions). Inside, the carbon is contained in cotton cloth liners to prevent the charcoal
fines from migrating from the carbon bed. The third-party replicas have differences, including a
smaller particle diameter and a different method for sealing the cloth sacks.

 59

 Lean Sample

 C Bed Sample
 Rich Sample

Figure 52: Process flow diagram for PZAS at NCCC. Circles mark the main sampling
 points: O Lean, O rich, and O carbon bed outlet.

 60

 Figure 53: NCCC 2023 carbon bed
On-site analyses to monitor for carbon breakthrough included a UV-Vis spectrometer (320 nm),
gas chromatography (GC) for PZ concentration, and total inorganic carbon analysis (TIC) to
determine CO2 loading. Liquid samples were analyzed at UT for Fe, Cr, Mn, Ni, Cu, Li, and K
using ICP-MS (see Figures 12 and 13). Other analyses at UT included IC to quantify anionic and
cationic degradation species (Figures 6, 7, and 8). A Cary 5000 UV-Vis NIR spectrometer was
used to measure UV-Vis spectra for all samples at a path length of 10 mm (Figure 54).
Previous pilot campaigns and bench-scale experiments demonstrated that as PZ degrades, its UV
absorbance at 320 nm and visible absorbance at 538 nm increase (Wu, 2022). The replacement of
the carbon canisters at 3,710 h coincided with a 0.5X reduction in the UV absorbance. Prior to
replacement, the carbon bed was offline for 173 h and there was no change in the UV absorbance.
During the period with carbon treating and NO2 scrubbing online, the UV increase was 4.5 mAu/h.
Figure 54 shows an increase in the on-site UV measurements when NO2 scrubbing was terminated.
It was hypothesized that when carbon treating was terminated, there would be a change in UV
accumulation. This effect was not observed in the UV data, indicating that either breakthrough
did not occur or UV should not be used to determine bed breakthrough on-site. With no mitigation
methods online, the rate of accumulation of UV-absorbing species was 49.8 mAu/h according to
the on-site measurement, while the UT measurements exhibited no change. Both the UT-measured
and on-site-measured data show a step decrease when the carbon was replaced. When all
mitigation methods were brought back online, the accumulation rate was 32.3 mAu/h until 4,400 h,
when there was an increase to 163.9 mAu/h. This increase in accumulation may have been
 61

indicative of bed breakthrough, but this cannot be determined based on UV data alone. When
thermal reclaiming was used to treat three solvent inventories (4,187 gallons), the UV absorbance
was reduced to the level initially exhibited by the solvent.

 Figure 54: UV absorbance at 320 nm for lean (●), rich (■), and carbon bed outlet (▲)
 samples measured at UT and at NCCC
Figure 55 demonstrates that the iron concentration doubled after replacement of carbon canisters
at 1,108 h. It is has been hypothesized that the carbon initially leaches Fe and/or Fe-complexing
agents. During steady-state operation of the NCCC 2023 campaign with all oxidation mitigation
methods online, Fe levels decreased by 0.04 µmol/kg/h. When NO2 scrubbing was taken offline,
the iron concentration increased to as much as 125 µmol/kg in a relatively short time. This spike
was possibly due to NO2 changing the oxidative environment, resulting in a change in the Fe2+/Fe3+
ratio and the resulting solubility of Fe. When carbon treating was terminated, iron started to
accumulate at a rate of 0.1 µmol/kg/h. These results indicate that carbon prevents Fe from
accumulating in solution and potentially catalyzing oxidation.

 62

 Figure 55: Dissolved metal ions (Cr, Fe, Mn, and Ni) by ICP in NCCC samples
Nitrogen sparging
The efficacy of oxidation mitigation through N2 sparging in the absorber sump for DO removal
was tested at the SRP and NCCC 2023 (Closmann, 2024). Data were collected over a range of N2
gas sparging conditions to facilitate full-scale design of the DO stripping approach. In both
campaigns, the N2 gas sparge volumetric flowrate was adjusted while measuring the DO activity
downstream to understand the rates needed at larger scale.
Besagni et al. (2018) provided a review of literature on two-phase bubble columns, with an in-
depth review of flow regimes, flow regime transitions, fluid dynamics patterns, and mass transfer
phenomena. The mono-dispersed homogeneous bubble regime and the pseudo-homogeneous
bubble regime are characterized by limited interaction between sparge bubbles themselves, leading
to very few bubbles coalescing into larger bubbles. Schumpe and Grund reported that at higher
gas velocity (UG), the heterogeneous flow regime will prevail, which is characterized by high
bubble coalescence and breakage rates with a large variety of bubble sizes. The selection of
sparging devices will influence the stability of the dispersed homogeneous flow regime over a
range of gas velocities (UG), with gas sparge hole diameters (do) of < 1 mm providing stable mono-
dispersed bubble flow regimes, even at higher UG. In summary, a more stable, dispersed bubble
regime will be produced through generation of smaller bubbles at lower gas exit velocities from
sparge devices with small sparge hole diameters (< 1 mm). Mott Corporation recommends that
superficial gas exit velocities of 5–10 fpm with a maximum of 25 fpm be used in their Media
Grade 2 metal sparge pipes for in-tank applications with no agitation.
A Mott Media Grade 2 316 stainless steel pipe (2316-G16[12]-12-GAS-AB) with a sparge cross-
sectional area of 0.26 ft2 was procured and installed in the NCCC absorber sump for the 2023
campaign. Figure 56 shows the sparge pipe used for DO stripping with N2 gas in the absorber
sump during the NCCC 2023 campaign. The pipe was specified to produce maximum sparge
cross-sectional area, resulting in minimum N2 gas exit velocity in the absorber sump for efficient
DO stripping.

 63

 Figure 56: Mott sparge pipe used at NCCC 2023
A Memosens DO probe was utilized in the NCCC 2023 campaign for measurements of oxygen
activity at two locations depicted in Figure 57; the membrane probe is used to make equilibrium
measurements of the partial pressure of oxygen, but DO is reported in units of concentration (ppm
of mg/L) without correcting for changes in O2 solubility between water and CO2-loaded amine.
One probe location was in the pumparound intercooling (IC) loop of absorber Bed 1 while the
second location was in the rich amine process pipe downstream of the rich amine tank and pump.
With a pumparound IC rate of 36.5 gpm and a nominal liquid rate of 17 gpm, the total liquid rate
through the absorber sump was ~54 gpm; this is in contrast to the liquid rate (L) passing through
the absorber packing above Bed 1. The Lsolv/GN2 in the absorber sump liquid at the tested N2 gas
rates of 0.5, 1, 2, and 4 scfm ranged from ~1,735 to 13,860 lb/lb in reverse order (Table 6). These
absorber sump Lsolv/GN2 values are one to two orders of magnitude greater than those utilized at
SRP (Table 6), resulting in a far lower N2 consumption rate for DO stripping.

 Figure 57: DO probe locations used during the NCCC 2023 pilot campaign
Figure 58 is a plot of the DO concentration measured during the NCCC 2023 campaign. Early
measurements (April 2023) demonstrated that steady-state DO in the IC loop location,
representative of the absorber sump, was about 4 mg/L when N2 sparging was offline. At the same
time, DO at the downstream location was ~1.8 mg/L, indicating that DO was being partially
consumed as readily oxidizable degradation products such as aldehydes were being generated over
the course of the campaign. The residence time of the solvent in the rich amine tank and carbon
vessel, both located between the absorber and the downstream DO probe location, was ~100
 64

minutes, which was sufficient to allow > 50% DO consumption. The data in the same plot
demonstrate that when the N2 sparging was brought online, DO at the IC loop location was reduced
by 90% to ~0.5 mg/L. These tests were conducted with 2 scfm N2.

 Figure 58: DO measurements collected during the NCCC 2023 pilot campaign
A short-term test entailing the adjustment (doubling/halving) of the N2 sparge volumetric rate was
implemented to observe DO removal. The data plotted in Figure 59 and included in Table 6
demonstrate that better stripping performance occurred at lower rates of N2 sparging; all rates
tested at NCCC 2023 resulted in gas exit velocities from the sparge pipe of < 25 fpm. At the lowest
N2 sparge rate and gas exit velocity (1.9 fpm), the Lsolv/GN2 was 13,860 with 2.8 NTU (NTUstr =
ln(DOo/DOi)). This is in contrast to ≤ 1.7 NTU measured at N2 sparge rates of 10 and 20 scfm and
Lsolv/GN2 of 51 and 25 at the SRP, respectively. A fundamental difference between the operating
modes for stripping DO in the sump at SRP and NCCC is that the liquid made a single pass through
the absorber sump at SRP whereas pumparound intercooling at NCCC resulted in back-mixing in
the sump and Bed 1. As such, on average, the solvent passed through the absorber sump
approximately three times before being pumped downstream to the stripper at NCCC. Because
the solvent rates entering Bed 1 and exiting the absorber to the rich amine tank were the same at
~17 gpm, the residence time was unaltered by the intercooling loop.
One other distinction between the NCCC and SRP results was the N2 fraction of total gas traffic
in the absorber column. At an N2 sparge rate of 0.5 scfm at NCCC, the gas exit velocity was 1.9
fpm and the N2 gas was < 0.05% of the total gas traffic through the absorber column. This N2 rate
resulted in the greatest estimate for NTU of 2.8. The minimum Lsolv/GN2 necessary to meet the
stated goal for removing DO (≥ 90%) was exceeded at 0.5 scfm N2. In contrast, the N2 gas was
1 to 3.3% of the total gas traffic at SRP and the NTU was lower.

 65

  Figure 59: Short-term test conducted over a range of N2 gas sparge rates (NCCC 2023)

Tangible evidence of the benefits of N2 sparging for DO stripping in the absorber sump at NCCC
2023 include the FTIR NH3 data continuously measured at the absorber outlet. During NCCC
2023, the oxidation mitigation methods were all brought online at the beginning of the campaign,
as depicted with grey background in Figure 60. The oxidation mitigation methods were taken
offline one at a time, with the first method removed from service the N2 gas sparging in the
absorber sump at ~1,300 hours; Figure 60 shows an increase from 0.1 mol/hr to 0.15 mol/hr in the
NH3 concentration. This is evidence of increased oxidation of solvent to form NH3. After taking
all oxidation mitigation methods offline 3,000 hours into the campaign (white background), all
methods were brought back online at 3,720 hours (grey background). During that final period with
all mitigation methods online, the NH3 concentration at the absorber outlet dropped and continued
to decrease before passing through a “tipping point” at ~4,300 hours and then undergoing a steady
rise.

 Figure 60: NH3 measured at absorber inlet and outlet during NCCC 2023
The total moles of nitrogen-containing degradation species were quantified in both rich and lean
samples during NCCC 2023 (Figure 61). Those species include NH3, NFPZ, EDA, MPZ, AEP,
AEG, HEG, PZAA, and OPZ. The data were regressed separately for each period of time when
step changes were made to the oxidation mitigation methods; the step change at ~1,300 hours when
the N2 gas sparging was taken offline is of interest. The rate of N-containing degradation
product(s) accumulation increased in the rich samples from 0.1 moles/hr with all mitigation
methods online to 0.4 moles/hr without N2 gas sparging. When the same step change was made
in the lean samples, the rate of N-containing degradation product(s) accumulation increased from
0.2 moles/hr with all mitigation methods online to 0.3 moles/hr without N 2 gas sparging. These
changes in accumulation rates indicate that N2 gas sparging reduced oxidation of the solvent to
these N-containing degradation product(s) by 30 to 75%.
During the latter half of the NCCC 2023 campaign, the PTR-TOF-MS was used to monitor
concentrations of gas phase constituents in the absorber outlet and water wash outlet gas streams.
A test was performed to determine short-term effects of N2 sparging at the water wash outlet gas
location (Figure 62). N2 gas sparging was online, then shut off for five hours, after which it was
brought back online. During this entire period, the PTR-TOF-MS was used to measure NH3 and
hazardous air pollutants (HAPs) at the water wash outlet gas location. NH3 increased from a
steady-state concentration of ~15 ppmv with N2 gas sparging online to a new steady-state
concentration of ~17 ppmv after four hours with N2 gas sparging offline. At the end of the test,
N2 sparging was brought back online and the NH3 concentration decreased, reaching a new steady
state of ~15.5 ppmv. The HAP compound acetaldehyde was also measured by PTR-TOF-MS
during the test and plotted in Figure 62. An increase in its concentration was measured during the

 67

short-term test, but not to the extent measured for NH3. The other PZ degradation product in the
plot, MPZ, did not exhibit a change in response to the N2 sparging being taken offline.

 Figure 61: Moles of N-containing degradation species measured in liquid samples NCCC
 2023
In summary, N2 sparging reduced DO in the rich amine in the absorber sump by >90%. Direct
evidence of the benefits of N2 sparging lies in measurements of degradation data including gas
phase NH3 and liquid phase N-containing species. Combined data demonstrate a reduction in the
rate of PZ degradation of between 30 and 75% over the long term.

 68

  Figure 62: PTR-TOF-MS measurements of PZ and degradation species at water wash
 outlet NCCC 2023

CONCLUSIONS
Top conclusions from the NCCC 2023 campaign include:

 • Prescrubbing with sulfite lowered the NO2 concentration in the flue gas entering the
 absorber from ~3.5 ppm to < 0.5 ppm; ~50% of the remaining NO2 was absorbed into the
 solvent in the absorber throughout the campaign;
 • Prescrubbing NO2 lowered oxidation of the solvent as evidenced by lower concentrations
 of degradation compounds in the liquid phase and NH3 in the water wash outlet;
 • The carbon bed prevented the accumulation of iron whenever it was brought online; spikes
 in the iron concentration were measured immediately after the carbon bed was brought
 online with fresh carbon;
 • Early measurements (< 1,000 hours) with the DO probe demonstrated that > 50%
 consumption of DO occurred between the absorber and DO probe location downstream of
 the rich amine tank, carbon tank, and rich amine pump;
 • The greatest number of liquid continuous phase mass transfer units (NTU) and DO removal
 were measured when the N2 gas sparge rate was < 0.05% of total gas traffic through the
 absorber column;
 • A tipping point was reached at ~4,300 hours when the NH3 in the water wash outlet
 underwent a steady rise; this tipping point was reflected in several other liquid phase
 parameters;
 • N2 sparging in the absorber sump for DO removal reduced the accumulation rate of N-
 containing PZ degradation species by 30 to 75%;

 69

 • A possible corrosion event occurred at ~2,300 hours as evidenced by the HSS and dissolved
 metal ion data;
 • The total amino acids concentration was ~85 mmol/kg at the point of peak degradation
 (~4,600 hours); the amino acids were removed by > 90% through the single stage thermal
 reclaiming event;
 • The aldehydes were measured at a total concentration corresponding to ~10% of the total
 amino acids at 4,600 hours, indicating they are reactive and will be readily oxidized to
 amino acids;
 • The NH3 formation rate accelerated during the corrosion events at 2,300 hours and at 4,300
 hours, with all mitigation methods online reaching 25 ppm;
 • NH3 in the water wash outlet correlated to the liquid phase, with an increase in both at
 ~2,300 hours; this correspondence likely indicates that the iron catalyzed further solvent
 oxidation leading to higher NH3 concentrations;
 • Reclaiming reduced NH3 from > 25 ppm to < 3 ppm in the water wash outlet;
 • The acid wash was effective at reducing the NH3 concentration in the water wash outlet to
 ~10 ppb;
 • PZ was reduced to ~0.1 ppb in the water wash outlet with a two-stage water wash
 configuration;
 • The HAPs were generally not removed by two-stage water wash configurations, but were
 lowered through thermal reclaiming of the solvent;
 • Corrosion coupons generally demonstrated a weight gain in the warm rich bypass line due
 to deposition of dissolved materials evolving from other areas of the plant; erosion
 corrosion was observed in an elbow in the pumparound intercooling line as evidenced by
 coupon weight gain;
 • Heat loss measurements led to the following observations:
 o Heat loss from the stripper column was greatest at higher steam rates due to an
 overall hotter column;
 o Heat loss from bare metal surfaces was dominant in the regeneration system;
 o Convection-controlled heat loss dominated due to Tsurf-Tamb > Tfluid-Tsurf;
 o The average fraction of heat lost relative to steam heat rate was ~35.4%;
 o A heat duty of 2.4 GJ/tonne CO2 captured was measured at a cold rich bypass split
 fraction of 8% and a warm rich bypass split fraction of 64% while achieving up to
 98% CO2 removal;
 o Energy performance improved when the total rich bypass temperature was raised
 above the bubble point at the stripper operating pressure, giving rise to vapor and
 two-phase flow.
