 71

Characterizing Emissions from Technology Centre Mongstad
Abstract
Pilot data from a four-month campaign at the Technology Centre Mongstad (TCM) were analyzed
to evaluate how flue gas composition, water wash configurations, and operating conditions
influence emissions of solvent and degradation products during post-combustion CO2 capture
using a second-generation amine solvent.
TCM operated with two primary flue gases: the RFCC (Residual Fluid Catalytic Cracker) flue gas
containing H2SO4 aerosols that act as condensation nuclei, and MHP (Mongstad Heat Plant) flue
gas with elevated NO2 (~16 ± 1 ppm) that promotes oxidative degradation. Both streams were
manipulated using air dilution and CO2 recycling to simulate flue gas with CO2 concentrations
ranging from 4% to 20%, representing a range of industrial combustion scenarios.
This report demonstrates that flue gas composition is the primary driver of solvent emission levels
and mechanisms. RFCC flue gas produced aerosol-mediated piperazine (PZ) emissions of 1–5
ppm, which increased with inlet aerosol and were controlled by increasing water wash stages and
absorber temperatures. MHP flue gas produced vapor-phase-dominated PZ emissions below
10 ppb that were largely insensitive to water wash configuration. When MHP was combined with
a recycled CO2 stream containing NH3, PZ emissions shifted to an aerosol-mediated mechanism,
increasing by 2–3 orders of magnitude. Major PZ degradation products including
mononitrosopiperazine (MNPZ), N-formyl piperazine (NFPZ), and ethylenediamine (EDA)
followed the same aerosol-mediated mechanisms as PZ.
NO2 in MHP flue gas acted catalytically to promote oxidative degradation. Major amine
degradation products including NH3, dimethylamine (DMA)/ethylamine, and monomethylamine
(MMA), peaked during high NO2 periods, and were managed by acid wash operation. Elevated
levels of hazardous air pollutants, particularly acetaldehyde and acetonitrile, and MNPZ, a known
carcinogen, also tracked with high NO2. Liquid-phase analysis of degradation products revealed
that 1.8 moles of PZ degraded per mole of inlet NO2, with a constant PZ degradation rate of 7.9
mol/h (1.3–1.7 mol PZ per MT CO2 captured) attributable to combined oxidation from dissolved
oxygen and trace metals in the system. These findings demonstrate that flue gas composition,
particularly NO2 and aerosol content, is the primary driver of emission variability, necessitating
flue gas-specific emission control strategies for commercial carbon capture deployment.
Introduction
Post-combustion CO2 capture using amine-based solvents represents the most mature and
commercially viable technology for reducing greenhouse gas emissions from large stationary
sources such as power plants and industrial facilities. This technology utilizes alkaline amine
solutions to selectively absorb CO2 from flue gas streams, followed by thermal regeneration to
release concentrated CO2 for storage or utilization. Despite their technical maturity, amine-based
capture systems face environmental challenges associated with atmospheric emissions of volatile
organic compounds and aerosols containing amine solvents and their degradation products
(Rochelle, 2024).
Emissions from amine-based capture systems arise through multiple pathways: vaporization of
volatile compounds, physical entrainment of liquid droplets, and aerosol-mediated transport. Of
 1

particular concern are degradation products such as nitrosamines and nitramines, many of which
are known carcinogens, as well as hazardous air pollutants (HAPs) including acetaldehyde and
acetonitrile (Nielsen et al., 2012; Da Silva & Booth, 2013; Rochelle, 2024). The magnitude and
speciation of these emissions depend strongly on flue gas composition, with individual flue gas
components driving distinct emission mechanisms.
For example, sulfur trioxide (SO3) in the flue gas forms sulfuric acid (H2SO4) aerosols that act as
condensation nuclei, enabling aerosol-mediated transport of amines and its degradation products
(Buvik et al., 2021). Current emission control strategies such as water wash systems and demisters
can capture larger particles but show limited effectiveness against submicron aerosols, which can
carry solvent and degradation products, and represent a major emission pathway (Fang et al., 2020;
Drewry et al., 2025). Aerosol formation is particularly complex, involving heterogeneous
nucleation, chemical reactions within aerosol particles, and mass transfer processes sensitive to
absorber temperature profiles and lean solvent composition, making aerosol-mediated emissions
difficult to predict and manage under varying operating conditions.
Nitrogen dioxide (NO2) in the flue gas catalytically promotes oxidative degradation of amines,
producing atmospheric emissions of ammonia (NH3), hazardous air pollutants (HAPs), and
carcinogenic nitrosamines (Rochelle, 2024). This pathway is particularly concerning for
secondary amines such as piperazine (PZ), which form nitrosamines directly, while primary and
tertiary amines may degrade into secondary amines that subsequently generate nitrosamines (Dai
and Mitch, 2013). The concentration of nitrosamines can be reduced by thermal decomposition in
a high temperature stripper (Closmann et al., 2024). Acid wash can effectively remove some
oxidation products such as NH3 and simple amines, though its applicability to other degradation
products varies. Recent PZ pilot plant campaigns have reported degradation rates ranging widely
from 1.5 at higher NO2 concentrations to 9 moles PZ/mole NO2 (Closmann et al., 2024; Chen,
2024; Wu, 2024; Nielsen, 2018; Fine, 2015), and the relationship between inlet NO2 concentration,
PZ degradation rates, and resulting emissions remains unquantified. Understanding and controlling
these emissions remains a critical challenge for accurately evaluating the environmental impacts
of large-scale amine-based CO2 capture. While extensive research has examined individual
degradation products, emission mechanisms and control technologies, gaps remain in
understanding how flue gas composition, process conditions, and emission control configurations
interact under industrial operating scenarios (Buvik et al., 2021).
A four-month long (2,650 hours) pilot campaign was conducted at the Technology Centre
Mongstad (TCM), Norway to characterize emissions during post-combustion CO2 capture using a
PZ-based solvent. TCM operated with two primary flue gases: the aerosol-rich Residual Fluid
Catalytic Cracker (RFCC) flue gas, and the NO2-rich Mongstad Heat Plant (MHP) flue gas, known
to promote oxidative degradation. Both flue gas streams were modified through air dilution and
CO2 recycling to simulate a range of CO2 concentrations (4% to 20%) representative of industrial
conditions. The campaign provided a unique opportunity to systematically evaluate how flue gas
composition, emission control configurations, and operating conditions influence emissions of PZ
and its degradation products.
This report examines emissions of PZ and its degradation products across different flue gas
compositions, water wash configurations, and operational scenarios tested at TCM. The analysis
identifies how flue gas properties govern emission mechanisms, quantifies the catalytic role of

 2

NO2 in degradation product formation, and evaluates the effectiveness of water wash and acid
wash systems for controlling different classes of emissions.
Methods
Data Sources and Instrumentation
Operational parameters for the campaign were obtained from the IP.21 historian system managed
by TCM. These included absorber and stripper temperature profiles, lean solvent loading, gas and
solvent flowrates, water wash flowrates, and flue gas composition (e.g., CO2, NO2, O2), among
other process variables. Hourly values of these variables were used to characterize flue gas and
water wash configurations and operational events throughout the campaign.
Inlet flue gas composition was measured using multiple analyzers at TCM. NO2 concentrations
utilized in this work were measured using a Rosemount laser-based analyzer, CO2 concentrations
were measured using a laser-based analyzer on a dry basis, while O2 was measured using gas
chromatography (GC).
A proton-transfer-reaction time-of-flight mass spectrometer (PTR-TOF-MS) located at the
absorber outlet downstream of the water wash system was used to measure gas-phase emissions
(Figure 1). The instrument provided real-time measurements of PZ, selected degradation products,
NH3, and other volatile species. Impurities including NH3 in the CO2 product stream were
measured using Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy (FTIR) at the stripper outlet.
Lean solvent samples were collected periodically and shipped to the University of Texas at Austin
for off-site laboratory analysis. Samples were analyzed using ion chromatography (IC) to quantify
amines and heat-stable salts, and Direct Amino Acid Analysis (AAA) to measure amino acid
species. MNPZ in lean solvent, however, was analyzed on-site using liquid chromatography-mass
spectrometry (LC-MS). Table 1 provides a summary of the primary data sources and analytical
methods utilized for this work.

Water wash configurations
To control emissions, TCM employed three sequential water wash stages with three demisters
positioned above the two absorber beds (Figure 1). When all three water washes are operating,
the treated flue gas, containing solvent and degradation vapors, first passes through a “trickle bed”
via a demister, where countercurrent contact with wash water provides initial cooling and
absorption of volatile amine compounds and degradation products. The treated gas then passes
through the “bottom water wash” via a demister, where further gas-liquid contact occurs to remove
remaining gaseous amines and provide additional cooling. Finally, the treated flue gas passes
through the final water wash stage, the “top water wash” where final polishing occurs to capture
residual volatile species and any remaining aerosol particles. After this stage, any remaining
aerosol particles are collected in the final demister before exiting the system.

 Figure 1: Simplified flowsheet of absorber and water wash system tested at TCM
Both the top and bottom water wash stages have dedicated water tanks and circulation systems.
Flow to the trickle bed is supplied via bleeds from the outlets of the top and/or bottom water
washes. Each water wash stage provides an opportunity for cooling, aerosol growth, and scrubbing
of the treated flue gas stream. Increasing the number of water washes generally enhances removal
efficiency for both vapor- and aerosol-phase components (Drewry & Rochelle, 2024a). The three
demisters are positioned after each water wash stage and serve to physically remove aerosol
 4

particles from the treated gas through inertial impaction and coalescence mechanisms. The top
water wash, at times, was operated at low pH (typically < 3.0), i.e., acid wash conditions to
neutralize volatile basic compounds such as NH3 and other basic amines through acid-base
reactions that convert volatile amines into non-volatile salts (Drewry & Rochelle, 2024b)
In this work, the operational status of each water wash was determined by the water flow to the
water washes. Each water wash stage had a dedicated flowmeter located at the inlet of that water
wash, which continuously measured the flowrate along with the temperature. Hourly flowrates
during the campaign were extracted from the IP.21 historian system to assess the activity of each
stage over time. Using these time series, water washes were classified as active, inactive, or in
transition (e.g., during water wash switching, startup, shutdown, or disturbances). In addition to
flowrates, pH readings from a sensor located just before the top water wash inlet were used to
identify acid wash activity. An acid wash condition was defined by pH values falling below 3.0.
Table 2 summarizes the flowrate criteria used to define each water wash as 'Active,' along with
typical flowrates and the total number of active hours for each water wash.

Flue gas configurations
Flue gas was classified based on NO2 concentration (in ppm) measured at the flue gas inlet using
a Rosemount sensor. RFCC flue gas was identified by NO2 concentration below 1.6 ppm, while
MHP flue gas was identified by NO2 concentration above 10.7 ppm, and diluted MHP (Dil-MHP)
by NO2 concentration of 3–8 ppm. RFCC flue gas was diluted with air at ratios of 3.8 and 1.5,
forming two diluted RFCC streams (Dil-RFCC (1), and Dil-RFCC (2)), which were identified
from corresponding reductions in inlet CO2 concentration. Dilution ratios were defined as the ratio
of CO2 concentration in the original flue gas to that in the diluted stream.
Table 3 summarizes the classification criteria, average NO2, CO2, O2, aerosol number
concentrations (where available), and active hours for each flue gas.

Operational Timeline
Figure 2 presents the operational timeline of flue gas streams tested at TCM during the 4-month
campaign comprising 2,650 operating hours. A total of 253 hours of shutdown were excluded
from the calendar time. The vertical black lines mark the start and end of the two reclaiming
events. Each reclaiming event started when solvent flow to the reclaimer was initiated and ended
when vapor flow (distillate) from the reclaimer to the stripper stopped. The gray region shows the
period of dynamic testing during which stripper pressure varied between 0 and 2.5 bar gauge
(barg). Table 3 provides the corresponding event log, including start times (calendar date and
operating hours) and durations of major operational transitions and shutdowns.

 Figure 2: Operational timeline of flue gases tested at TCM campaign
Key operational transitions included testing of the Brownian Demister Unit (BDU) upstream of
the absorber, pressure reduction in the stripper, solvent reclaiming events, and switching between
the two stripper units available at TCM. BDU testing was conducted to evaluate aerosol particle
number and size distribution in the RFCC flue gas. Two strippers were used at TCM and switched
several times during the campaign. Stripper pressure was adjusted during system ramp-up and
during reclaiming operations. For reclaiming operations, the stripper pressure was systematically
adjusted: in event 1, pressure was decreased from 2.5 to 0.2 barg; in event 2, pressure was
decreased to 0.2 barg and then returned to 2.5 barg. These adjustments are not listed in Table 4 as
they were considered part of the reclaiming operation.

 6

Other transitions such as changes in lean solvent flow and temperature, inlet flue gas flow and
temperature, intercooler settings, and lean loading were implemented more dynamically
throughout the campaign and are therefore not included in Table 4. These parameters, however,
had a significant impact on emissions and are discussed in the Results section.

 7

Operating periods with various flue gases were further categorized based on the number of active
water wash stages: 1-stage, 1-stage with trickle bed, 2-stage, and 3-stage, and the presence or
absence of acid wash. This systematic grouping allowed for evaluation of different emission
control strategies. Preliminary assumptions included the expectation that top and bottom water
washes perform similarly, and that flow magnitude has limited impact.

Emission Measurement
Gas-phase emissions were measured at the outlet of the absorber after the water washes (Figure 1)
using a PTR-TOF-MS. This is the same instrument used during the 2023 National Carbon Capture
Center (NCCC) campaign in Alabama to measure gas phase emissions and is described in Drewry
et al. (2025). The instrument provided real-time measurements of PZ and its degradation products
including MNPZ, ethylenediamine (EDA), N-formyl piperazine (NFPZ), ammonia, and HAPs
(including acetaldehyde and acetonitrile).

Sustained emissions
Emissions measured by PTR-TOF-MS were available at a 10-minute resolution and were averaged
to hourly values. Each hourly data point was then attributed to a specific water wash and flue gas
configuration based on the operational conditions at that time.
In addition to changes in flue gas and water wash configurations, several events listed in Table 4,
such as bypassing the BDU, adjusting stripper pressure, and dynamic changes in lean solvent
temperature and loading, among other scenarios, caused temporary emission spikes. These spikes
did not reflect the typical conditions of the water wash and flue gas configuration but were instead
driven by transient operational changes.
To ensure meaningful comparisons across configurations, periods of steady operation were
identified to estimate “sustained emissions,” representing typical emissions under stable
conditions. For each flue gas and water wash configuration, the total operating time was
determined, and a stability criterion was applied to detect steady-state periods. A period was
considered stable/steady state if it lasted at least three consecutive hours (or 10% of the total
configuration duration, whichever was longer) and if the emissions of a given species remained
within ±10% of that period’s mean.
For each stable period, mean concentrations were calculated for all species of interest. The
sustained minimum was defined as the lowest of these stable-period means, the sustained
maximum as the highest, and the sustained average as the mean of all stable-period means. The
sustained average provides a robust estimate of typical emissions during steady-state operation
under each configuration.

 8

Results
PZ emissions under different flue gas configurations

Aerosol-mediated emissions with RFCC flue gas
Figure 3 presents the variation in PZ emissions (in ppb) with various flue gas configurations over
the entire campaign. Operations at TCM started with MHP flue gas for the first 34 hours, after
which the flue gas was switched to RFCC (green background), causing a sharp increase in PZ
emissions spanning four orders of magnitude. This increase was driven by aerosols present in
RFCC flue gas (8.4 x 104 particles/cm3 of size range 0.06 and 0.3 μm), which provided surfaces
for condensation of solvent and degradation product vapors. These aerosols were small enough to
bypass the demisters in the absorber and water wash sections and were subsequently emitted into
the atmosphere where they were detected by the PTR-TOF-MS.

 Figure 3: Piperazine (PZ, in ppb) over the entire TCM campaign. Background colors
represent various flue gas configurations, the two vertical lines mark the start of reclaiming
 events, and the dotted red line denotes the 10-ppm emission limit for PZ.
At TCM, the daily average amine emission limit is set at 10 ppm, so during the campaign
conservative operational measures were typically triggered when emissions exceeded 1 ppm.
These measures included increasing the number of water wash stages, raising trickle bed flow, and
adjusting lean solvent and intercooler temperatures, among other process modifications. While
such measures caused frequent fluctuations in PZ emissions during the RFCC periods, they
remained consistently elevated, typically ranging from 1–5 ppm, reflecting the strong influence of
aerosol-mediated emissions.

 9

To reduce aerosol-based amine emissions from the absorber, a Brownian Demister Unit (BDU) is
located upstream of the absorber at TCM to remove fine particulate matter and aerosols present in
the RFCC flue gas. The BDU vessel consists of a high-efficiency demister followed by twenty-
one candle filters filled with polypropylene fibers (Lombardo et al., 2018). The demister removes
larger particles (> 1 μm) primarily through impaction, while the candle filters capture ultrafine
particles (10–500 nm) through Brownian diffusion, achieving > 95% overall aerosol removal
efficiency.
ELPI+ (Electrical Low-Pressure Impactor) measurements were conducted by Laborelec (third
party) at 320 and 369 hours (November 11 and 13, 2024) at the outlet of the BDU for 7 hours each
day. The objectives were: (i) to test particle removal efficiency after recent replacement of the
filter candles in the BDU, and (ii) to assess particle levels when gradually increasing the BDU
bypass, to correlate particle number concentrations in the flue gas with aerosol-based PZ emissions
at the absorber outlet. Laborelec had also previously conducted similar measurements for
monoethanolamine (MEA) testing at TCM (Faramarzi et al., 2017).
Real-time measurements of particle size distribution and number concentration (of size range 6 nm
to 10 μm) downstream of the BDU showed total particle numbers in filtered RFCC flue gas ranging
from 7.9 × 104 to 9.5 × 104 cm-3, with an average of 8.4 × 104 cm-3. More than 99% of these
particles were between 0.06 and 0.3 μm, with microscopy analyses confirming that particles
smaller than 0.5 μm were predominantly H2SO4 aerosols.
At 369 hours, a stepwise opening test of the bypass valve was conducted to gradually increase
aerosol concentrations in the flue gas sent to the absorber. Table 5 shows particle counts measured
downstream of the BDU for different bypass openings. During this test, the absorber operated
with all three water wash stages, a lean solvent temperature of 49.8 °C, and a loading maintained
0.19 mol CO2/mol N. Other absorber operating conditions are listed in Table 6.

 10

PZ concentrations measured by PTR-TOF-MS at 10-minute resolution were averaged for each
bypass opening period to assess the impact of aerosol concentrations on PZ emissions. Figure 4
demonstrates that PZ emissions increased with increasing particle numbers (i.e., greater BDU
bypass opening). The increase in PZ emissions was steep initially but then leveled off as the
number of particles increased. This behavior likely occurs because, as particle numbers increase,
more surface area becomes available for PZ vapor condensation, leading to increased emissions.
However, at very high particle concentrations, the available PZ vapor in the gas phase becomes
depleted due to competition among numerous aerosol droplets for the limited amine vapor, limiting
further growth and emissions (Svendsen et al., 2025).

 11

At 924 hours, RFCC flue gas was reintroduced after an extended period of MHP operation,
resulting in elevated PZ emissions comparable to the initial RFCC period. At 990 hours (after 66
hours of RFCC operation), the flue gas was diluted with air to achieve ~4% CO2 (compared to the
typical 14–15%). Shortly afterward, at 998 hours, solvent reclaiming began.
The diluted RFCC period showed substantially lower PZ emissions than undiluted RFCC
operation. Dilution reduced both the number of aerosol nuclei and the CO2 driving force for amine
absorption. With fewer aerosol nuclei available for vapor condensation and a reduced absorption
driving force, aerosol-mediated PZ emissions decreased by approximately 3–5 times compared to
undiluted RFCC. The conditions after solvent reclaiming might have affected the emissions too,
but the effects of dilution and reclaiming could not be separated during this period.
A second dilution of RFCC began at 1,346 hours, producing RFCC flue gas with ~10% CO2 (1.5×
dilution compared to undiluted RFCC at 14–15% CO2). Unlike the first diluted RFCC period
(~4% CO2, 3.8× dilution), this more modest dilution did not sustain lower PZ emissions. PZ
emissions were lower at the beginning of this period, but they gradually increased, reaching ≥ 1
ppm, as during other RFCC periods. This limited effectiveness likely resulted from the smaller
reduction in aerosol nuclei concentration and CO2 driving force at 1.5× dilution compared to the
3.8× dilution achieved in the first diluted RFCC period.
The remaining two RFCC periods, starting at 1,496 hours (182 hours duration) and 2,178 hours
(307 hours duration), also showed initial PZ above 1 ppm. However, increasing absorber
temperatures (lean solvent and intercooler) during these periods reduced emissions by
approximately 50%, as discussed in detail later in this report.

Vapor-phase emissions with MHP flue gas
The first MHP operation began at 660 hours, when the flue gas transitioned from RFCC to MHP,
and PZ emissions decreased drastically from ~3,000 ppb to below 100 ppb. Unlike RFCC flue
gas, MHP contained minimal aerosol, so emissions arose predominantly from vapor-phase
transport rather than aerosol-mediated mechanisms.
PZ continued decreasing for approximately 330 hours without reaching steady state, and this trend
was observed for all MHP and Dil-MHP periods. This persistent decline suggests ongoing
clearance of residual aerosol from earlier RFCC operations, which likely remained trapped in the
absorber packing, internals, and PTR-TOF-MS sampling line. As these were gradually removed
or evaporated, they continued releasing absorbed PZ, causing emissions to show intermittent
spikes overlaid on an overall declining trend over hundreds of operating hours.
At 1,856 hours, MHP was diluted with air to 4% CO2 (compared to 10% CO2 in undiluted MHP)
to yield Dil-MHP. At the start of this period, PZ showed large fluctuations spanning two orders
of magnitude (1–100 ppb) overlaid on the same decreasing trend. These fluctuations likely
resulted from intermittent release of residual aerosol still present in the system. Despite the
fluctuations, emissions followed an overall decreasing trend, reaching approximately 10 ppb.
When undiluted MHP was reintroduced, concentrations decreased further to approximately 1 ppb.
The final MHP periods at the end of the campaign showed sustained low emissions (1–10 ppb),
maintaining the decreasing trend observed throughout all MHP operations.

 12

Aerosol formation with MHP flue gas with recycled CO2
At 2,026 hours, a unique operational scenario began when the CO2 stream from the stripper was
recycled and blended with MHP flue gas to achieve 20% CO2 at the absorber inlet (referred to as
"MHP with recycled CO2" in this report). This operation lasted approximately 114 hours and
exhibited PZ emissions above 1 ppm, which was unexpected because typical MHP periods showed
emissions below 10 ppb.
The elevated PZ emissions are likely due to a fundamental change in the emission mechanism.
The CO2 product stream contained elevated concentrations of NH3, which was continuously
reintroduced upstream of the absorber during the ‘MHP with recycled CO2’ period. In the presence
of SO2 and NO2 in the MHP flue gas, this recycled NH3 could react in the direct contact cooler
(DCC) (Faramarzi et al., 2017), promoting aerosol formation upstream of the absorber.
These conditions likely transformed the normally aerosol-free MHP flue gas into one resembling
RFCC, where aerosol-mediated emissions dominate. The persistently elevated NH3 levels
observed in the CO2 product stream during this period (shown in purple in Figure 5) support this
mechanism, confirming NH3 was continuously available for aerosol formation.
Table 7 summarizes sustained PZ emissions (identified using the stability criteria described in the
Methods section) across different flue gas compositions, demonstrating how gas composition
fundamentally determines both the mechanisms and magnitudes of PZ emissions. RFCC flue gas
with high aerosol content drives an aerosol-mediated emission mechanism resulting in elevated
PZ (1–5 ppm). MHP flue gas, characterized by high NO2 but minimal aerosol, operates through a
vapor-phase-dominated mechanism with significantly lower emissions (< 10 ppb).

 recycled CO2. The background colors represent varying water wash configurations.

However, when MHP is combined with recycled CO2 containing NH3, the system shifts to an
aerosol-mediated mechanism, resulting in emissions 2–3 orders of magnitude higher than typical
MHP periods. While operational controls such as water wash stages and absorber temperature
adjustments (discussed in detail later) provide measurable emission reductions, the fundamental
emission mechanism and baseline magnitude are controlled by the intrinsic chemical composition
of the flue gas, particularly its aerosol, dilution, and NO2 content.

Mitigation Strategies for PZ Emissions

Effect of water wash on PZ emissions
Figure 6 shows a detailed view of the first 225 hours from Figure 3, with background colors
indicating different flue gas and water wash configurations. TCM was operated with MHP flue
gas (pink background) for the first 35 minutes before shifting to RFCC flue gas (denoted by
multiple shades of green). White bands in between reflect "transition” periods due to changes in
operating conditions.
The first operation of RFCC flue gas operation began at 35 hours with a 2-stage water wash
configuration (both top and bottom stages active). When the bottom stage was deactivated at 66
hours, creating a 1-stage configuration, PZ emissions increased to approximately 7 ppm. To
reduce these emissions, the bottom water wash was turned on at 85 hours, decreasing PZ to 2–3
ppm. However, PZ remained higher than the initial 2-stage period (below 1 ppm) due to a
simultaneous increase in inlet gas flowrate.

 14

 Figure 6: PZ emissions for the first 225 hours of the campaign. Background colors
 represent varying flue gas and water wash configurations.
At 160 hours, the trickle bed was activated, creating a 3-stage water wash configuration that further
reduced PZ to 1–2 ppm. At 200 hours, turning off the trickle bed returned the system to a 2-stage
configuration, which again increased PZ emissions.
Figure 6 and Table 7 demonstrate that increasing the number of water wash stages progressively
reduced PZ emissions during RFCC flue gas operation. Each water wash stage promoted aerosol
droplet growth through water condensation, increasing size until they become large enough to be
captured by demisters or coalesce with the bulk liquid. The transition from 1-stage to 2-stage
water wash showed the most significant reduction, while the change from 2-stage to 3-stage
provided additional but more modest improvements.

 15

Figure 7: PZ emissions during one of the periods with diluted RFCC flue gas. The dashed
  line marks the end of solvent reclaiming, and the background colors represent varying
 water wash configurations.
Figure 7 shows PZ emitted during the first diluted RFCC period from 1,100 to 1,346 hours. The
dashed vertical line at 1,202 hours marks the end of a solvent reclaiming event, after which the
system required several hours to restabilize.
As previously discussed, during diluted RFCC operation, PZ emissions were substantially lower
than observed during RFCC periods. Dilution of RFCC flue gas to approximately 4% CO2
(compared to typical 14–15% in RFCC) reduced both the number of aerosol nuclei available for
vapor condensation and the driving force for amine absorption. This combination decreased
overall aerosol-mediated PZ emissions by approximately 3–5 times compared to RFCC.
During Dil-RFCC operation with a 2-stage water wash, PZ emissions averaged around 500 ppb.
At 1,220 hours, the system transitioned to a 3-stage configuration, reducing PZ emissions by half
to approximately 250 ppb, demonstrating that water wash effectiveness persists under diluted flue
gas conditions. At 1,255 hours, the trickle bed was turned off for 7 hours, returning the system to
2-stage operation and increasing PZ. After reactivating the trickle bed at 1,262 hours, the system
was restored to the 3-stage configuration with PZ stabilizing around 250 ppb.

 16

Figure 8: Sustained PZ emissions (in ppb) during RFCC and dilute RFCC flue gas periods.
 The error bars show the range of sustained emissions, and the duration indicates hours of
 operation.
Figure 8 compares sustained PZ emissions across different water wash configurations for RFCC
and diluted RFCC flue gas conditions. Diluted RFCC periods (lighter shades of green circles)
consistently show lower PZ compared to RFCC conditions (dark green circles) across all water
wash configurations.
For RFCC flue gas, the 1-stage water wash resulted in the highest emissions (~5,000 ppb).
Conventional 2-stage and 3-stage water wash configurations progressively reduced emissions.
The 1-stage water wash with trickle bed, however, showed substantially lower concentrations
(< 1,000 ppb) than expected. This performance occurred at two distinct operational conditions.
The first two periods (47 h and 104 h) operated with lower lean loading, which likely increased
PZ volatility and promoted faster aerosol growth, resulting in larger particles that were easily
collected in the demisters. The remaining periods (13 h, 17 h, and 36 h) operated at elevated
absorber temperatures, as they occurred shortly after a reclaiming event, which likely promoted
aerosol growth and subsequent removal in the demisters. The effect of absorber temperature on
PZ emissions is discussed later in the report.

 17

 Figure 9: PZ emissions during one of the periods with MHP and Dil-MHP. Background
 colors represent varying flue gas and water wash configurations.
Figure 9 shows the effect of water wash configurations on PZ emissions during Dil-MHP and
MHP periods. At 1,815 hours, TCM operated with Dil-MHP flue gas using only the bottom water
wash stage, with PZ around 10 ppb. At 1,833 hours, activating the top water wash at acid wash
conditions (pH ~2) reduced PZ to 1 ppb, because the increased water wash capacity helped to cool
and condense PZ vapors back into the liquid phase.
At 1,859 hours, the system transitioned to MHP flue gas (after a brief transition period shown in
white). MHP showed lower PZ emissions than Dil-MHP, with concentrations continuing to
decrease gradually below 1 ppm. From 1,888 to 1,903 hours, both the top water wash and acid
wash were turned off, but this did not affect PZ concentration. At 1,905 hours, the system resumed
operation with a 2-stage acid wash, with PZ emissions around 0.6 ppb, and minor fluctuations due
to variations in inlet gas flowrate. The limited sensitivity to water wash configuration during MHP
operation confirms that emissions are dominated by vapor-phase mechanisms rather than aerosol
carryover.
Figure 10 compares sustained PZ emissions across different water wash configurations for MHP,
Dil-MHP, and MHP with 20% recycled CO2. For MHP and Dil-MHP flue gas, PZ remained
consistently low across all configurations, typically below 10 ppb. The addition of water wash
stages provided further reductions, though the low baseline emissions limited the observable
benefits (Table 7).
In contrast, MHP with recycled CO2 (yellow circles), like RFCC, exhibited substantially higher
emissions, greater than 1,000 ppb. This is because the recycled CO2 stream contained NH3, which
when combined with SO2 and NO2 in the DCC formed aerosol, shifting the emission mechanism
from vapor-phase-dominated (typical MHP) to aerosol-mediated.

 18

 Figure 10: Sustained PZ emissions (in ppb) during MHP, Dil-MHP, and MHP with
 recycled CO2 flue gas periods. The error bars show the range of sustained emissions, and
 the duration indicates hours of operation.

Effect of absorber temperatures
At TCM, three primary temperature setpoints controlled the thermal profile of the absorber: inlet
flue gas temperature, lean solvent temperature, and intercooler temperature. These setpoints
determined the temperature distribution throughout the absorber, including the water wash
sections.
For aerosol-mediated emissions, the water wash temperatures are particularly important, as they
govern aerosol growth rates and capture efficiency. Higher temperatures promote aerosol growth
through increased gas phase saturation and enhanced absolute mass transfer driving forces for PZ,
making aerosol droplets large enough for effective demister capture (Svendsen et al., 2025).
Figure 11 shows the effect of intercooler temperature on PZ emissions during a 2-stage RFCC
period. At approximately 2,365 hours, the intercooler temperature was raised from ~55 °C to
~70 °C (red line) to maintain water balance during a solvent reclaiming event. This led to a step-
change reduction in PZ, dropping from 2,000–5,000 ppb to 200–400 ppb, the lowest emissions
observed during any RFCC period of the campaign.
The elevated intercooler temperature throughout the absorber and water wash promoted aerosol
growth and subsequent capture in demisters. During this time, the lean solvent temperature
remained stable (52–55 °C, not shown here), while the inlet flue gas temperature (brown) was
gradually reduced. This suggests that the intercooler temperature change was the primary driver
of the emission reduction. The blue line in Figure 11 shows the temperature measured below the
top water wash, indicating the step-change response to intercooler temperature adjustment.

 Figure 11: Effect of intercooler temperature on PZ emissions during RFCC operation.
Black points show PZ emissions (ppb, left axis). Solid lines show temperature profiles (°C,
right axis): intercooler outlet (red), below top water wash (blue), and inlet flue gas (brown).

Emissions of PZ derivatives
In addition to PZ, several PZ-derivatives were monitored throughout the campaign, including N-
formyl piperazine (NFPZ), ethylenediamine (EDA), and MNPZ. These products are formed in the
liquid phase through oxidative and thermal degradation of PZ. Once formed, their emissions are
governed by their volatility and the prevailing emission mechanism. Among these compounds,
MNPZ is also a nitrosamine and is discussed later with HAPs.
Figure 12 and Figure 13 show emissions of PZ with NFPZ, EDA, and MNPZ throughout the
campaign. All three compounds closely tracked PZ, with strong positive correlations across all
flue gas configurations: MNPZ (Spearman ρ = 0.84), EDA (ρ = 0.82), and NFPZ (ρ = 0.78). These
correlations indicate that emissions of MNPZ, NFPZ, and EDA are governed by the same
fundamental mechanisms controlling PZ emissions.

vertical lines mark the start of reclaiming events, and the dotted red line denotes the 10-

 limit for PZ.

 21

Figure 14 further demonstrates that emissions of MNPZ, NFPZ, and EDA increased with inlet
aerosol particle number concentration, following the same aerosol-mediated emission mechanism
observed for PZ during the BDU bypass testing. During RFCC periods with high aerosol loading,
emissions of these degradation products were elevated and responded like PZ to water wash
configuration changes.
During MHP periods with minimal aerosol content, emissions of MNPZ, NFPZ, and EDA
remained low regardless of water wash configurations. However, during the MHP period with
20% recycled CO2, all three products showed substantially elevated concentrations comparable to
RFCC periods, confirming that these species partition into aerosol and follow aerosol-mediated
emission pathways when aerosol nuclei are present.

Table 8, Table 9, and Table 11 summarize sustained emissions of NFPZ, MNPZ, and EDA,
respectively, across different flue gas and water wash configurations. Emissions of all three
compounds decreased with increasing water wash stages. EDA emissions also decreased with
elevated absorber temperatures, mirroring PZ behavior. MNPZ and NFPZ, however, did not show
this behavior. This difference likely reflects altered molecular properties introduced by the nitroso
(–NO) and formyl (–CHO) functional groups, which affect vapor pressure and solution-phase
chemistry differently than the parent diamine structure.
Lower lean solvent loading also reduced emissions of all degradation products. The lower CO2
loading likely increased the volatility of these species and promoted faster aerosol growth and
subsequent removal in the demister and water washes.

Emissions of NH3
During the TCM campaign, NO2 varied strongly with the flue gas source, driving differences in
oxidative degradation. RFCC flue gas had relatively low NO2 (0.2–1.5 ppm), while MHP flue gas
had much higher levels (15.5 ± 2.3 ppm). Dilution reduced NO2 to intermediate values of 6 ppm
for diluted MHP and 0.3–0.8 ppm for diluted RFCC.
Figure 15 demonstrates the direct link between NO2 and NH3 formation. At 660 hours, the step
change in NO2 (green dots) was immediately followed by a corresponding step change in NH3
(blue dots). Throughout MHP operation, elevated NO2 (~16 ppm) corresponded to significantly
higher NH3 emissions compared to RFCC periods, supporting the role of NO2 in promoting
stepwise oxidative degradation of PZ and its derivatives, with NH3 as the final degradation product.
This effect was not observed during the second half of the campaign because acid wash operation
was implemented starting at 1,830 hours, which proved highly effective in controlling NH3
emissions. Acid wash reduced NH3 by more than 99%, decreasing its level by two orders of
magnitude compared to normal water wash operation (gray dots in Figure 15).
NH3 emissions also decreased following the two solvent reclaiming events, suggesting that
removal of accumulated degradation products, particularly amino acids, reduced the pool of
compounds available for conversion to NH3. This also indicates that NH3 formation depends on
the concentrations of both NO2 and degradation intermediates in the liquid phase.

 NH3

 NO2

Figure 15: Ammonia (blue) and NO2 (green) at the TCM campaign. The grey dots are NH3
 during acid wash periods, and the two vertical lines mark the start of solvent reclaiming
 events.
Table 10 shows that MHP flue gas operation consistently produced NH3 an order of magnitude
higher than RFCC. Acid wash reduced these emissions by 99%, bringing them down from

 26

> 10,000 ppb to 100–200 ppb for diluted MHP, and from > 26,000 ppb to ~200 ppb during the
MHP periods.

Emissions of nitrosamines and Hazardous Air Pollutants (HAPs)
MNPZ was the dominant nitrosamine observed during the TCM campaign, accounting for more
than 85% of the total nitrosamines. Other nitrosamines detected at very low concentrations were:
dinitroso-PZ (< 0.5 ppb, average 0.2 ppb), nitro nitroso-PZ (< 0.7 ppb, average 0.2 ppb), and the
nitrosamine of EDA (~0.2 ppb).
MNPZ emissions were higher during RFCC periods (5–15 ppb) than MHP periods (< 1.5 ppb),
and about half as high during diluted RFCC conditions (2.5–7.5 ppb). The highest MNPZ (35–55
ppb) occurred using MHP with recycled CO2, when elevated NO2 combined with aerosol
formation created conditions favorable for nitrosamine formation and transport (Figure 13 and
Table 9). This suggests that aerosol-mediated transport rather than NO2 alone drove the peak
nitrosamine emissions, as high NO2 during standard MHP operation produced minimal MNPZ
emissions. Given the carcinogenic nature of nitrosamines, these results highlight the need for
anticipating and managing potential aerosol formation.
Four hazardous air pollutants (HAPs), acetaldehyde, acetonitrile, formaldehyde, and methanol,
were also consistently detected throughout the campaign (Figure 16 and Table 10). Figure 16
highlights the two dominant HAPs, acetaldehyde and acetonitrile, along with total HAP
concentrations, which ranged between 20 and 600 ppb. The other two HAPs, formaldehyde and
methanol, remained below 5 ppb throughout the campaign.

 Acetaldehyde

 Acetonitrile

  Figure 16: Acetaldehyde (brown) and acetonitrile (purple) at the TCM campaign. The
 shaded gray background represents total HAPs (ppb), two vertical lines mark the start of
  solvent reclaiming events, and the dotted black lines show the range of observed HAPs.

 27

Acetonitrile emissions increased immediately when MHP flue gas was introduced at 660 hours,
indicating direct formation from NO2-promoted oxidation reactions. Acetonitrile briefly
decreased when RFCC flue gas was introduced at 924 hours but rose again during diluted RFCC
operation, potentially due to increased dissolved oxygen from air dilution enhancing oxidation
rates. Both acetaldehyde and acetonitrile decreased following the first solvent reclaiming event,
consistent with their formation from the primary oxidation products of PZ, which are removed
during reclaiming. Their concentrations gradually increased afterward until the second reclaiming
event, which produced a weaker reduction than the first.
Table 10 summarizes sustained HAPs across different flue gas and water wash configurations.
Unlike PZ and its degradation products, HAPs were not significantly affected by water wash or
acid wash. The strong correlation between NO2 concentrations and increased HAP formation
suggests that NO2 promotes oxidative degradation.

Other Gas Phase Emissions
A wide range of other nitrogen-containing and oxygenated compounds were detected by PTR-
TOF-MS during the campaign. These species included solvent degradation products, secondary
reaction products, and trace impurities, with emissions governed by a combination of formation
pathways, volatility, solubility, and system configurations. Detected compound classes include
simple amines, ketones, pyrazines, pyrroles, and imidazoles, all of which are discussed below.

Simple Amines
Methylamine (MMA) and dimethylamine (DMA)/ethylamine (Figure 17 and Figure 18) exhibited
similar emission patterns to NH3 during MHP periods, having 3–5 times higher emissions than
RFCC periods. Both species were highly correlated with each other (Spearman ρ = 0.83) and were
effectively removed by acid wash (> 95% reduction).
Unlike NH3, MMA and DMA/ethylamine emissions increased rapidly following reclaiming
events, indicating formation through direct PZ degradation pathways independent of NH3. The
frequent fluctuations in their concentrations suggest that these species were reactive intermediates
being formed from PZ and subsequently consumed in secondary reactions that generated other
degradation products.
Table 11 summarizes sustained emissions of MMA and DMA/ethylamine across flue gas and
water wash configurations. Neither MMA nor DMA/ethylamine were significantly reduced by
standard water wash stages, consistent with their moderate volatility and relatively low aqueous
solubility (Drewry et al., 2025). However, the high effectiveness of the acid wash demonstrates
that pH control is critical for managing emissions of basic volatile amines.

  are NH3 during acid wash periods, and the two vertical lines mark the start of solvent
 reclaiming events.

Ketones
Figure 19 shows hourly emissions of acetone and butanal/butanone measured over the entire
campaign. Acetone and butanone emissions tracked closely throughout the campaign (Spearman
ρ = 0.88), confirming shared formation pathways.
Emissions of both species were consistently higher during MHP periods compared to RFCC
periods, indicating more oxidizing conditions with higher NO2. The highest ketones emissions
(> 60 ppb for acetone, and > 10 ppb for butanal/butanone) were observed during the MHP with
recycled CO2 period. This period also produced PZ emissions up to 2 ppm, likely due to aerosol
formation. Emissions of other oxidation products such as acetaldehyde, pyrazines, pyrroles, and
imidazoles were also elevated during this period. This suggests that in addition to aerosol
formation, the chemical environment likely promoted enhanced oxidative conditions. The specific
mechanisms driving these conditions remain unknown but may involve interactions between
aerosol-phase chemistry and high NO2. Perhaps high NO2 results in oxidation within the aerosol
causing greater emissions of the ketone products as aerosol and gas.
Ketones were reduced following solvent reclaiming, suggesting that these compounds are likely
formed through secondary oxidation of PZ degradation products. Removal of their precursor
species during reclaiming limited subsequent ketone formation and accumulation. Additionally, a
step increase in ketone emissions at 183 hours coincided with increased stripper pressure (Table
4), demonstrating sensitivity to stripper operations.
Table 11 summarizes sustained emissions of ketones across flue gas and water wash
configurations. Emissions during MHP periods were up to an order of magnitude higher than
RFCC periods. Additional water wash or acid washing did not significantly reduce ketones,
consistent with their high volatility and low aqueous solubility (Drewry et al., 2025).

Pyrazines
Pyrazines are heterocyclic nitrogen-containing compounds characterized by a six-membered
aromatic ring with two nitrogen atoms positioned opposite each other (Figure 20). In PZ-based
solvent systems, pyrazines are not primary degradation products but form through secondary
reactions involving PZ degradation intermediates.

 Figure 20: Molecular structures of pyrazines, pyrroles, and imidazoles

 Pyrazine

 Acetyl pyrazine

 Figure 21: Pyrazine (brown) and acetyl pyrazine (pink) at the TCM campaign.
Background colors indicate various flue gas configurations, and the two vertical lines mark
 the start of reclaiming events.
 32

Figure 21 shows hourly emissions of pyrazine and acetyl pyrazine during the campaign. Pyrazine
emissions were consistently higher than acetyl pyrazine across all conditions. Like HAPs and
ketones, both these compounds exhibited elevated emissions during high NO2 periods.
In addition to pyrazine and acetyl pyrazine, C5H6N2 was observed at relatively high concentrations
and is suspected to correspond to a methyl-substituted pyrazine (methyl pyrazine or C1-pyrazine).
Figure 22 shows hourly emissions of this compound during the campaign. Like acetyl pyrazine,
C5H6N2 emissions were higher during MHP periods than RFCC periods.

 C5H6N2

  Figure 22: C5H6N2 at the TCM campaign. Background colors indicate various flue gas
 configurations, and the two vertical lines mark the start of reclaiming events.
Acetyl pyrazine and C5H6N2 were strongly correlated (Spearman ρ = 0.83) and tracked well with
both ketones (ρ = 0.76-0.82; Table 12). In contrast, unsubstituted pyrazine showed relatively weak
correlations with acetyl pyrazine (ρ = 0.53) and C5H6N2 (ρ = 0.50), though correlation remained
moderate with ketones (ρ = 0.61-0.71; Table 11). These correlations suggest that substituted
pyrazines and ketones share similar oxidative formation pathways, while unsubstituted pyrazine
exhibits greater stability or follows different formation kinetics.
Following solvent reclaiming events, concentrations of all pyrazine species decreased, indicating
that their formation depends on PZ degradation intermediates present in the solvent.
Table 13 summarizes sustained pyrazine emissions across flue gas and water wash configurations.
Like ketones, additional water wash stages or acid washing did not significantly reduce pyrazine,
likely due to its volatility and limited removal efficiency through aqueous scrubbing.

 34

Pyrroles
Pyrroles are five-membered aromatic heterocyclic compounds containing one nitrogen atom
(Figure 20), formed through secondary reactions involving ring-opening and recombination of PZ
degradation intermediates.
Figure 23 shows that hourly emissions of pyrrole and methyl pyrrole (C1-pyrrole) were below
1 ppb during RFCC periods and between 1 and 10 ppb during MHP periods. Pyrrole and C1-
pyrrole tracked very closely with each other (Spearman ρ = 0.90, Pearson r = 0.91) and exhibited
strong correlations with other oxidation products including C2-imidazoles, pyrazines, and ketones
(ρ = 0.76-0.87, Table 12). C1-pyrrole showed slightly weaker correlations with these compounds
compared to unsubstituted pyrrole, possibly reflecting differences in formation kinetics or stability
associated with methyl substitution.
Both compounds exhibited similar emission patterns, with emissions increasing progressively
during extended MHP periods, suggesting continued formation from accumulating degradation
intermediates. Following solvent reclaiming events, emissions of both species decreased sharply,
confirming dependence on PZ degradation intermediates. The subsequent gradual recovery
indicates continued pyrrole formation as degradation products re-accumulate in the system.
Table 13 summarizes sustained pyrrole emissions across flue gas and water wash configurations.
C1-pyrrole appeared to decrease slightly during acid wash periods; however, the low emission
levels (~1 ppb) make quantitative assessment difficult.

 Pyrrole

 C1-Pyrrole

 Figure 23: Pyrrole (light blue) and C1-pyrrole (orange) at the TCM campaign.
Background colors indicate various flue gas configurations, and the two vertical lines mark
 the start of reclaiming events.

Imidazoles
Imidazoles are five-membered aromatic heterocycles containing two nitrogen atoms (Figure 20).
Two imidazole species were detected at TCM: methyl imidazole or C1-imidazole (C4H6N2, also
identified as potentially dihydropyrazine) and ethyl-imidazole (C2-imidazole). These compounds
exhibited distinctly different emission behaviors, suggesting different origins within the system.
Figure 24 shows hourly emissions of C1-imidazole for the campaign. Unlike other degradation
products, C1-imidazole emissions were an order of magnitude higher during RFCC periods than
MHP periods, mirroring the emission pattern of PZ itself. These emissions were also strongly
correlated with PZ emissions (Spearman ρ = 0.7), suggesting a shared aerosol-mediated emission
mechanism that dominates during RFCC operation.

 C1-imidazole/
 Dihdropyrazine

 Figure 24: C1-imidazole/dihydropyrazine emissions during the TCM campaign.
Background colors indicate various flue gas configurations, and the two vertical lines mark
 the start of reclaiming events.
Although C1-imidazole varied throughout the campaign, it maintained a persistent background
concentration whenever aerosol formation occurred. Table 13 summarizes sustained imidazole
emissions across flue gas and water wash configurations. The PZ-like emission pattern with MHP
and RFCC flue gas, combined with its lack of response to reclaiming and water washing, suggests
C1-imidazole is possibly a trace impurity in the PZ solvent rather than a degradation product.
In contrast, C2-imidazole exhibited emission patterns consistent with secondary oxidative
degradation (Figure 25). C2-imidazole emissions were higher during MHP periods and showed
very strong correlations with C1-pyrazine (Spearman ρ = 0.94), pyrrole (ρ = 0.87), acetyl pyrazine,
and other oxidation products (Table 12), indicating formation through shared oxidative pathways.
C2-imidazole emissions decreased following reclaiming events and subsequently increased during
operation, confirming formation from PZ degradation intermediates that accumulate over time.
This behavior is consistent with other heterocyclic degradation products (pyrroles, pyrazines).
 36

 C2-imidazoles

 Figure 25: C2-imidazole during the TCM campaign. Background colors indicate various
  flue gas configurations, and the two vertical lines mark the start of reclaiming events.

Other Compounds
Table 14 reports sustained emissions of additional compounds detected by PTR-TOF-MS that do
not fit into the previously discussed classes.
C4H6O2 exhibited the highest emissions among unclassified compounds (Figure 26), with
emissions elevated during the MHP + recycled CO2 period consistent with the enhanced oxidative
environment observed for other degradation products. This compound had strong linear
relationships with NFPZ, piperazin-2-one, and MNPZ (Pearson, r = 0.82), indicating formation
through oxidative degradation of PZ derivatives.
C7H8N2 was strongly correlated with pyrrole (Spearman ρ = 0.84) and exhibited similar emission
behavior, with higher concentrations during MHP periods than RFCC periods. However,
emissions remained below 3 ppb across all conditions.
C8H10N2 showed aerosol-mediated emission behavior like methylpyrazine, with which it was
moderately correlated (ρ = 0.75), though the structural relationship remains unclear. Emissions
remained below 3 ppb but followed PZ-like patterns, with higher concentrations during RFCC
periods when aerosol formation dominates.
Emissions of C8H8N2 and C6H9NO emissions remained below 1 ppb throughout the campaign,
near the detection limit of PTR-TOF-MS, preventing detailed characterization.

Comparison of emissions reported at NCCC 2023 and TCM 2025
Emissions measured using PTR-TOF-MS at TCM were compared with those reported from an
800-hour pilot campaign at the National Carbon Capture Center (NCCC), Alabama in 2023
(Drewry et al., 2025).
Figure 28 to Figure 32 compare the emission ranges of HAPs, ketones, simple amines,
nitrosamines, nitramines, pyrazines, pyrroles, imidazoles, piperazines, and other compounds from
both campaigns. This comparison provides an overall emission profile across different operating
conditions rather than a direct configuration-by-configuration comparison, as the campaigns
differed in operational configuration, flue gas composition, and emission control strategies.
Both campaigns used aqueous PZ solvent but the flue gas composition differed substantially.
NCCC utilized flue gas from a gas-fired boiler diluted with air to give 4.3% CO2 with initial NO2
of ~2.5 ppm, reduced to ~0.5 ppm through N₂ sparging, with no aerosol content. TCM operated
with two distinct flue gases: RFCC flue gas (NO2 ~0.5 ppm, high aerosol ~9×10⁴ particles/cm³)
and MHP flue gas (NO2 ~16 ppm, no aerosol). Both flue gas streams were modified through air
dilution and CO2 recycling to simulate a range of CO2 concentrations (4% to 20%), causing NO2
to vary between 0.5 and 16 ppm (Table 4).
While emission profiles depend on multiple process parameters (temperature, pressure, absorber
configuration, wash stages), flue gas composition, particularly NO2 concentration and aerosol
content, represents a major mechanistic driver of the observed emissions at TCM.
Acetaldehyde and acetonitrile emission ranges (Figure 27) were substantially higher at TCM than
NCCC. The lower minimum emissions reflect the effectiveness of thermal reclaiming in removing
their degradation intermediates precursors, while the higher maximum values result from enhanced
oxidative degradation due to high NO2. Methanol was higher at NCCC due to its use in the online
CO2 titrator.
Among simple amines, EDA (Figure 28) demonstrated the impact of aerosol-mediated transport,
with concentrations approximately two orders of magnitude higher at TCM than at NCCC.
Ketones and dimethylamine/ethylamine also showed substantially elevated emissions at TCM,
reflecting enhanced oxidative degradation under high NO2 conditions.
MNPZ showed the most dramatic difference (Figure 29), with maximum concentrations
approximately 70 times higher at TCM (68 ppb, and sustained emissions between 35 and 55 ppb)
compared to NCCC (~ 1 ppb). The highest MNPZ (35–55 ppb) occurred during MHP operation
with recycled CO2, when elevated NO2 combined with aerosol formation created conditions
favorable for nitrosamine formation and transport. All other nitrosamines and nitramines also
showed elevated emissions at TCM; however, they remained below 1 ppb at both campaigns.
Pyrazine, acetyl pyrazine, and pyrroles all showed 2–8× higher maximum concentrations at TCM,
consistent with formation through enhanced oxidative degradation and accumulation of
intermediates (Figure 30). Imidazoles had the highest impact, showing the largest relative increase
among heterocycles, with C2-imidazole approximately 60 times higher at TCM.
Among PZ derivatives, NFPZ and 1-(2-aminoethyl) piperazine showed substantially elevated
emissions at TCM (Figure 31), which was attributed to aerosol-mediated transport during RFCC
operation.

 41

This comparison demonstrates that flue gas composition, particularly NO2 and aerosol content,
represents a dominant mechanistic driver of emission profiles, requiring flue gas-specific emission
control strategies. High NO2 flue gases require NO2 reduction measures (N2 sparging, selective
catalytic reduction) to minimize nitrosamine formation and oxidative degradation products. High-
aerosol flue gases require enhanced aerosol management (pre-treatment, Brownian demisters) to
control partitioning-driven emissions. Optimization of process and operational configurations
remain important across all flue gas types for managing baseline degradation and emissions.

 campaigns at NCCC, Alabama (2023) and TCM, Norway (2025)

 Figure 28: Emission range of ketones and simple amines emitted during pilot plant
 campaigns at NCCC, Alabama (2023) and TCM, Norway (2025)

                       Alabama (2023) and TCM, Norway (2025)

 campaigns at NCCC, Alabama (2023) and TCM, Norway (2025)

                      Alabama (2023) and TCM, Norway (2025)

Mechanistic insights from oxidation data
To investigate the role of NO2 in oxidative degradation, nonlinear regression was performed on
the accumulation of nitrogen-containing degradation products measured during the TCM
campaign (Table 1). Total degradation was expressed as the sum of all quantifiable nitrogen-
containing species measured in the lean solvent, normalized on a 2N basis where one mole of
degraded PZ corresponds to two moles of degraded nitrogen. Species containing only one nitrogen
atom (ammonia and glycine) were divided by two for consistency.
The quantified species included:
 • MNPZ, free 3-oxopiperazin-1-acetic acid (3-OPZA), and EDA
 • Amino acids, including total N-(2-aminoethyl) glycine (AEG), piperazin-1-yl-acetic acid
 (PZAA), ethylenediamine-diacetic acid (2-EDDA), and glycine (GLY)
 • NH3, quantified by PTR-TOF-MS at the absorber outlet and by FTIR at the stripper outlet
Oxopiperazine (OPZ) and 3-EDDA were excluded due to analytical uncertainty. OPZ was
captured as AEG in samples treated with NaOH to get the total amino acids, and 3-EDDA was
observed to be a minor species compared to its lactam form (3-OPZA).
The total degradation products (2Ndeg) or PZ degraded (PZ deg) were calculated as:
 𝟏 𝟏
 𝟐 𝟐
where:
 NH3, t = total NH3 leaving the system (i.e., from absorber and stripper)
A total of 132 lean solvent samples were collected during the campaign. These samples were
analyzed off-site at UT for amines and amino acids, while MNPZ was quantified on-site at TCM
using LC-MS. Concentrations (mmol/kg) of all quantified species were converted to moles using
the measured solvent inventory at each sampling time. Figure 33 shows the estimated moles of
degradation products or degraded PZ (blue dots) at each sampling time. Degradation products
decreased sharply after both solvent reclaiming events, confirming their removal from the solvent
inventory.
To segregate the effect of reclaiming, the dataset was divided into two periods: Period 1 (hours 6–
987) and Period 2 (hours 1,233–2,161), and each period was regressed separately. PZ degradation
rate was assumed to have a first-order dependence on NO2, with an additional constant rate
representing NO2-independent pathways:
 𝒅(𝐏𝐙𝐝𝐞𝐠 ) 𝒅𝐍𝐎𝟐 𝒄
                                     = 𝒌 + 𝒌𝐍𝐎𝟐                                                (2)
 𝒅𝒕 𝒅𝒕
where:
 k = constant rate of degradation (mol/h).
 k NO2 = rate constant for NO2-dependent rate;
 NO2 𝑐 = cumulative NO2 (moles).
Integrating Equation 2 gave the total moles of degradation products at any time ‘t’ as a function of
cumulative moles of inlet NO2:
 𝐏𝐙𝐝𝐞𝐠 (𝐭) = 𝐂𝐨 + 𝒌 𝐭 + 𝒌𝐍𝐎𝟐 𝐍𝐎𝟐 𝒄 (3)

 45

Equation 3 was then regressed for the two time periods to get the optimum values of C o, k and
k NO2 . The non-linear regression converged as:
                         𝐏𝐙𝐝𝐞𝐠 (𝐭) = 𝐂𝐨 + 𝟕. 𝟗 𝒕 + 𝟏. 𝟖 𝐍𝐎𝟐 𝒄                                  (4)
where:
 Co = initial offset for the two periods (moles).
In Equation 4, 7.9 mol/h represents the constant degradation rate while the NO2-driven term (1.8)
scales with cumulative absorbed inlet NO2. The three major oxidation mechanisms of PZ result
from dissolved oxygen (DO), NO2, and Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺ (Chen, 2024). The constant degradation rate of
7.9 mol/h observed at TCM likely represents the combined contributions of DO and iron-catalyzed
oxidation pathways. During Period 1, approximately 6,647 MT of CO2 were captured over 964
hours (average capture rate of 6.9 MT/h), corresponding to a constant degradation rate of 1.3 mol
PZ per MT CO2 captured. During Period 2, about 4,645 MT of CO2 were captured over 907 hours
(average capture rate of 5.1 MT/h), corresponding to a degradation rate of 1.7 mol PZ per MT CO2
captured.
Figure 33 shows the regressed model using Equation 4 overlaid with the measured moles of
degradation products. The model captured the observed accumulation very well for both periods
(R2 = 0.99). Co for the first period was 1,062 moles, and for the second period it was 3,357 moles.
The higher Co value for the second period indicates that solvent reclaiming reduced degradation
product inventory but did not completely restore the solvent to its initial state. This analysis
indicates that 1 mole of inlet NO2 degraded approximately 1.8 moles of PZ.

  Figure 33: Estimated PZ (in moles) degradation at TCM. Blue dots are the measured
 moles of degradation products (as 2N), dotted orange lines show nonlinear regression fits,
  and Co is the initial degradation product inventory for each regressed curve. The two
 vertical lines mark the start of solvent reclaiming.

 46

Figure 34: Modeled rate of PZ degradation (moles/h) at the TCM campaign (orange curve).
  The black dashed lines represent the contribution of the constant degradation rate (7.9
 mol/h), while the green dashed line shows the contribution of NO2-dependent rate.
Figure 34 shows the instantaneous degradation rate, decomposed into a constant rate (black dashed
line) and a NO2-dependent (green line) contribution. During RFCC periods with low NO2 (0.2–
1.5 ppm), degradation was dominated by the constant rate. On switching to MHP flue gas at 660
hours, the NO2-dependent contribution increased dramatically, becoming approximately 6–7 times
larger than the background rate. Temporary decreases in the rate of degradation (e.g., at 831 hours)
corresponded to reduced gas flowrates, which lowered NO2 molar flux.
For the second period (after reclaiming), diluted RFCC operation again shows degradation
dominated by the constant rate due to low NO2. When diluted MHP was introduced, the NO2-
dependent contribution became 3 times the constant rate and later increased more when undiluted
MHP was introduced, demonstrating that NO2 is the dominant driver of oxidative degradation.
Conclusions
PZ emissions under different flue gas and water wash conditions at TCM
1. RFCC flue gas produced aerosol-mediated PZ emissions of 1–5 ppm. Increasing water wash
 stages from 1-stage to 3-stage progressively reduced emissions.
2. During RFCC flue gas periods, PZ emissions increased with increasing number of particles at
 low particle number. At high particle number, emissions plateaued as PZ in the gas phase
 becomes depleted due to competition among numerous droplets, limiting further growth and
 emissions.
3. Dilution of RFCC flue gas reduced aerosol nuclei and the CO2 driving force, lowering PZ
 emissions 3–5 times compared to undiluted RFCC.

 47

4. Elevated absorber temperatures reduced RFCC emissions by an order of magnitude (from
 ~3,000 ppb to ~300 ppb) by promoting aerosol growth and enhancing demister capture
 efficiency.
5. During MHP and Dil-MHP periods, sustained PZ emissions were always less than 10 ppb,
 dominated by vapor-phase transport. The addition of water wash stages resulted in only
 marginal reductions.
6. MHP flue gas with 20% recycled CO2 produced PZ emissions up to 2 ppm due to NH3 in the
 recycled CO2 stream forming aerosol nuclei with SO2 and NO2 in the flue gas upstream of the
 absorber.
Emissions of degradation products and influence of NO2 on emissions
7. MNPZ, EDA, and NFPZ followed aerosol-mediated mechanisms and correlated strongly with
 PZ across all flue gas configurations.
8. MNPZ accounted for > 85% of nitrosamines. Highest MNPZ concentrations (35–55 ppb)
 occurred during MHP with recycled CO2, when elevated NO2 combined with aerosol formation
 conditions to promote nitrosamine.
9. NH3 emissions ranged from 3 to 30 ppm and were highest during MHP (> 26 ppm) and Dil-
 MHP (> 10 ppm) periods. Acid wash reduced more than 99% of these emissions.
10. Acetaldehyde and acetonitrile dominated among HAPs, which range from 20 to 600 ppb.
 Highest HAPs were seen during high NO2 periods. Water wash configurations did not help in
 HAPs removal.
11. Secondary oxidation products including MMA, DMA/ethylamine, ketones, and imidazoles
 were 3–5x higher during high NO2 periods.
12. Acid wash reduced MMA and DMA/ethylamine emissions by 95%.
13. Ketones, pyrazines, and imidazoles were not affected by water wash configurations or acid
 washing and exhibited highest emissions during MHP operation with recycled CO2, where
 elevated NO2 combined with aerosol formation created enhanced oxidative conditions.
14. Maximum sustained emissions of ketones, nitrosamines, nitramines, pyrazines, pyrroles, and
 imidazoles were 1–2 orders of magnitude higher at TCM than at NCCC 2023, reflecting the
 impact of high NO2 MHP flue gas and aerosol-containing RFCC flue gas.
15. At TCM, the PZ degradation rate can be expressed as a sum of a baseline constant degradation
 (7.9 mol/h, or 1.3–1.6 mol PZ per MT CO2 captured) and NO2-driven degradation rate. About
 1.8 moles of degradation products are formed per mole of inlet NO2.
16. Flue gas composition, particularly NO2 and aerosol content, represents the dominant
 mechanistic driver of emission profiles, requiring flue gas-specific emission control strategies.
