 VII

INTRODUCTION

 The carbon dioxide removal (CDR) technology discussed in this work is amine scrubbing,

specifically Piperazine with the Advanced Stripper (PZAS). Among the advantages of PZAS

compared to other amine scrubbing processes is the use of 5 molal (m) or 30 wt % aqueous

piperazine (PZ). PZ is a second-generation amine solvent known for its fast rate of CO2 absorption,

large CO2 capacity, and high thermal stability compared to other well-studied solvents such as

monoethanolamine (MEA) (Rochelle et al., 2011). In particular, the relatively high thermal

stability of PZ allows for the operation of the stripper at 150 °C and 5.5 bar, decreasing the amount

and cost of compression of the final CO2 product (Freeman, 2011; Freeman and Rochelle, 2012;

Rochelle et al., 2011).

 Even with solvents with relatively low rates of amine loss, amine degradation remains a

critical barrier to amine scrubbing deployment (Rochelle, 2009). Amine solvents degrade by two

primary pathways: thermal degradation and oxidative degradation (Bello and Idem, 2005).

Oxidation is the main pathway for amine degradation, especially with thermally stable solvents

such as PZ (Freeman, 2011; Freeman et al., 2010; Rochelle et al., 2011). NO2, when present in the

inlet flue gas, is the key driver of PZ oxidation (Fine, 2015; Chi, 2024). Dissolved Fe, present

from stainless steel corrosion, is also a prominent PZ oxidation catalyst (Freeman, 2011; Nielsen,

2018; Nielsen and Rochelle, 2017). Unlike other stainless-steel corrosion products, Fe is

solubility-limited, with solubility increasing as degradation progresses (Nielsen, 2018). It is

hypothesized that certain degradation products complex Fe and increase its solubility, allowing it

to further accelerate oxidation. Because the system tends toward the Fe-complex solubility limit,

both Fe and its complexing agents must be addressed to minimize Fe-catalyzed degradation.

 Solvent oxidation produces known and unknown degradation species, which pose largely

unknown environmental and health risks (Thitakamol et al., 2006). In recent years, increasing

 1

attention has been directed toward mitigating emissions of amine degradation products, including

NH3, which can contribute to the formation of PM2.5 in the atmosphere (Buvik et al., 2021a; Plautz,

2018). Amine degradation also decreases CO2 absorption capacity, increases energy costs,

increases corrosion, and causes foaming, fouling, and plugging of equipment (Vevelstad et al.,

2011). Solvent make-up costs associated with oxidative degradation can be up to 10% of the cost

of the capture process (Rao and Rubin, 2002). Oxidation should be mitigated wherever possible

to reduce risk, improve process efficiency, and minimize operating costs.

 Solvent management encompasses the set of operational strategies designed to minimize

amine degradation and to manage or remove the degradation products formed during long-term

operation. Solvent management strategies include adding oxidation inhibitors, replacing degraded

amine with clean amine (bleed and feed), and treating degraded solvent to remove impurities

(amine reclaiming). Bleed and feed removes all impurities but generates large volumes of waste

and incurs high solvent replacement and waste disposal costs, making it economically and

environmentally unsustainable (Cummings et al., 2007). Amine reclaiming can only remove

specific impurities, depending on the technology. Four primary amine reclaiming technologies

exist industrially: thermal or vacuum distillation (thermal reclaiming), activated carbon filtration

(carbon treating), ion exchange, and electrodialysis (Cummings et al., 2007; Elmoudir et al., 2014;

Wang et al., 2015). Thermal reclaiming is investigated in this work.

 Thermal reclaiming of amines is a process by which non-volatile degradation products and

metals are separated from the volatile amine solvent by distillation. It has been implemented

industrially for decades (Elmoudir et al., 2014). The amine solvent is boiled under vacuum or near

atmospheric pressure and the vapor product, which includes the working amine, is returned to the

amine scrubbing cycle (Elmoudir et al., 2014; Wang et al., 2015; Sexton et al., 2016). The

operating temperature depends on the thermal stability of the solvent and the operating pressure of

the reclaimer. Frequently, reclaiming is operated as a semi-batch process, with the eventual

 2

addition of sodium hydroxide to improve solvent recovery (NaOH or caustic) (Wang et al., 2015).

The caustic improves solvent recovery by neutralizing protonated amine that has reacted with heat

stable salts (HSS) and hydrolyzing amide degradation products back to the parent amine (Buvik et

al., 2024; Cummings et al., 2007). Reclaiming is particularly efficient at reducing dissolved

metals, making it useful for reducing Fe-catalyzed degradation. Thermally reclaiming at least

three system inventories has successfully improved PZ solvent health at the National Carbon

Capture Center in 2023 (NCCC 2023) and at Technology Centre Mongstad in 2025 (TCM 2025)

(Plantz et al., 2024). The NCCC 2023 campaign suggested that reclaiming is not only useful for

the purification of the solvent but also as a strategy to slow solvent oxidation.

 Others have suggested that the amine loss to thermal reclaiming would be on the order of

10% of the working solvent, and that amine make-up costs would dominate the process economics

(Elmoudir et al., 2014; Sexton et al., 2016). Sexton et al. performed a techno-economic analysis

of thermal reclaiming that estimated the incremental cost of implementation with PZ would be

$4/MT CO2 (Sexton et al., 2016). However, TCM 2025 proved the assumed amine loss was

grossly overestimated. Furthermore, while waste product has previously been identified as

probably hazardous, the impact of this classification does not impact the cost of reclaiming as

significantly as Sexton et al. and Nielsen estimated (Elmoudir et al., 2014; Nielsen, 2018; Sexton

et al., 2016; Thompson et al., 2017).

 This work presents a techno-economic analysis (TEA) for intermittent operation of a

thermal reclaimer with PZAS at a 942 MWeq greenfield NGCC plant. The analysis is based on the

2022 NETL Cost and Performance Baseline Revision 4a (Schmitt et al., 2022). For the base

design, the incremental cost of installing thermal reclaiming was $0.76/MT CO2, which is 1.2%

of the estimated cost of capture (Schmitt et al., 2022). The annualized steam requirement for the

base reclaimer design is 2.3% of the PZAS reboiler duty (Rochelle et al., 2022).

 3

RECLAIMER DESIGN AND OPERATION

 A techno-economic analysis (TEA) for intermittent, semi-batch thermal reclaiming at a

commercial NGCC powerplant with PZAS was conducted. The aim was to estimate the

incremental cost of building and operating this plant with thermal reclaiming. The basis and

operating conditions for the scaled-up reclaimer design can be found in the 2022 National Energy

Technology Laboratory (NETL) cost baseline of a greenfield NGCC built with post-combustion

carbon capture (Schmitt et al., 2022). Experimental results from NCCC 2023 and TCM 2025

along with cost estimates from a 2021 front-end engineering design (FEED) study of a PZAS

retrofit at a NGCC plant (Mustang FEED) were used to perform the TEA (Rochelle et al., 2022).

Table 1 presents the key results from the TEA.

 4

Table 1: Summary of results from TEA of adding reclaiming to PZAS at 942 MWeq NGCC
 plant.

 Cost of Reclaiming ($/MT CO2) 0.76
 Total Cost of Capture ($/MT CO2) 65
 Annualized Heat Duty (GJ/MT CO2) 0.06
 PZAS Reboiler Duty (GJ/MT CO2) 2.5
 Total Purchased Equipment Cost ($MM) 1.2
 Total Plant Cost for Reclaiming ($MM) 5.8
 Total Plant Cost for CANSOLV ($MM) 588
 Annualized CapEx ($MM) 0.38
 Annualized OpEx ($MM) 1.7
 Total Annual Cost ($MM) 2.1

Basis for Reclaimer Design

 The TEA basis was case B32B.95 from the NETL cost baseline, which evaluated the design

and operation of a greenfield NGCC with 95% CO2 capture (Schmitt et al., 2022). The NGCC

plant consisted of two state-of-the art 2017 H-class turbines, two heat recovery steam generators

(HRSG), and one steam turbine generator (STG) in a 2x2x1 configuration. While the NETL cost

baseline utilized CANSOLV as the CDR, this analysis assumed an equivalent capture rate of

314 MT CO2/h with PZAS instead. This distinction is important because solvent degradation

between technologies is likely to be very different. The rate of degradation, specifically NH3

generation, was used to decide the required frequency of reclaiming. NH3 was chosen as the metric

for reclaiming initiation because maintaining emissions within permitting limits was assumed to

be a primary reason for operators to decide to reclaim.

 PZAS uses 30 wt % aqueous PZ, and the total inventory required was 4,739 m3, as scaled

by capture rate from the Mustang FEED (Rochelle et al., 2022). The NETL baseline employed a

selective catalytic reduction unit (SCR) to minimize NOx (Schmitt et al., 2022). Assuming NO2

was 10% of the NOx remaining after the SCR, the inlet to the CDR contained 0.15 ppm NO2. PZ

oxidation rates are primarily driven by NO2 when present above 1 ppm, and good pilot-scale PZAS

oxidation data has not been collected below 0.5 ppm NO2 (Chen, 2024). Therefore, the reclaimer

 5

was designed for an inlet flue gas with 2.5 ppm NO2, the same as at NCCC 2023 when NO2

prescrubbing was off. Unless otherwise noted, all other process conditions were derived from the

NETL cost baseline and are shown in Table 2.

 Table 2: Comparison of operating conditions for the TEA, NETL cost baseline, Mustang
                         FEED, TCM 2025, and NCCC 2023.

 NETL Cost
 TEA Baseline Mustang FEED TCM 2025 NCCC 2023
 Capture Rate (MT/h)     314           314             188             9.7            0.28
 PZ w/ Simple
 CDR PZAS CANSOLV PZAS Stripper PZAS
 3
    Inventory (m )       4,739      Unknown            2,835            50              8
     NO2 (ppm)            2.5          0.15             0.5           0.5–16         0.2–2.5
  Gas Rate (kmol/h)     170,575      170,575      29,000–98,000       80–490           125

 The general design of the reclaimer was based on the reclaiming unit at TCM 2025, with

some modifications. One constraint of the TCM 2025 design was returning the distillate vapor to

the stripper. This tied the operating pressure of the stripper to the reclaimer. Since TCM 2025

used a simple stripper at 1–2 bara, this was not ideal but still feasible. The PZAS stripper operates

at ~6.3 bara, and it is infeasible to operate the reclaimer at this pressure (Rochelle et al., 2022).

 In the TEA design, hot lean solvent was fed from the stripper sump outlet to the reclaimer

(Figure 1). The distillate was condensed and returned to the hot lean solvent, minimizing

disruption to the capture process while also preventing the precipitation of PZ. It is ideal to reclaim
lean solvent to minimize the amount of NaOH required to neutralize any CO2 not stripped in the

reclaimer. Reclaiming was operated at 2 bara and 150 ºC, irrespective of stripper operating

conditions, since the distillate was not returned as vapor. The base case reclaimer design assumed

intermittent, semi-batch operation. Future work could include a sensitivity analysis on the

economic and energy tradeoffs between intermittent and continuous reclaiming.

 6

 Figure 1: Intermittent reclaimer design at 942 MWeq NGCC with PZAS

Frequency and Length of Operation

 A 5 ppm NH3 emissions target set the frequency of reclaimer operation. NH3 generation

generally increases linearly with time (Chen, 2024). At NCCC 2023, 2.5 ppm NO2 was operated

from 2,869–3,710 h, so this period was used to calculate how quickly the NH3 generation increases

(Figure 2). To compare NH3 generation across systems, emissions were normalized by the gas

rate and total solvent inventory. The rate of increase of NH3 generation for the design case was

2.5 ppt/h. Reclaiming three inventories of solvent at the design conditions would return the NH3

generation to 1 ppm, based on NCCC 2023 reclaiming results (Figure 2). To maintain an average

emissions of 5 ppm NH3, reclaiming needs to be initiated when NH3 emission reaches 9 ppm. This

upper limit should be reached every 3,169 h, meaning 2.8 reclaiming campaigns operated annually,

each treating three inventories or 14,217 m3 of solvent.

 7

Figure 2: NH3 emissions at NCCC 2023. N2 sparging, NO2 scrubbing, carbon treating, and
 thermal reclaiming were tested as oxidation mitigation strategies.

 The length of reclaimer operation greatly influences size and therefore the capital expense

(CapEx) of process equipment. The base design assumed reclaiming would operate 50% of the

time, so each reclaiming campaign was 1,584 h. This length is later optimized. Regardless of

operational length, it was assumed the same amount of solvent and water would need to be

processed by the reclaimer. Therefore, a shorter campaign means larger process equipment would

be required, leading to a higher CapEx. Conversely, a shorter campaign would decrease the

operation and maintenance (O&M) costs, which are a significant portion of the annual operating

expenses (OpEx).

 The annual steam requirement (annualized heat duty) is independent of the length of

reclaiming, as the total amount of fluid that needs to be boiled does not change. However, the

steam rate during reclaiming (instantaneous heat duty) would be larger for shorter reclaiming

campaigns. The instantaneous heat duty cannot be too large a fraction of the stripper duty, though

 8

the specific maximum allowable duty would probably be plant specific. For this work it was

generally assumed anything above 8% would not be feasible.

Dynamic Operating Periods

 There are four distinct phases of operation for intermittent reclaiming: solvent feed, caustic

treatment, boiloff, and waste disposal (Figure 3). The solvent feed is the period when solvent is

being continuously fed to the reclaimer at a fixed rate. After the solvent feed is cut, a period of

caustic treatment is initiated to increase PZ recovery. It was assumed all the caustic required would

be added within 24 h after cutting the solvent feed. The subsequent phase of operation is boiloff,

when water is continually fed to improve the recovery of PZ. The final period, waste disposal,

refers to cleaning out the residue between reclaiming campaigns. This period was not counted in

the total time of reclaiming, as steam is not being supplied during this period. For this analysis, it

was assumed the waste would be fully removed within three-day shifts (cumulatively 24 h). The

waste would be pumped directly into tanker trucks, each with the capacity for 30 MT of waste.

 9

 Figure 3: Dynamic operation of one full reclaiming cycle for base design of reclaiming at
 942 MWeq NGCC with PZAS.

Boiloff Length

 Active reclaiming was 1,584 h, and consisted of the solvent feed, caustic treatment, and

boiloff periods. The length and feed rate during each period was determined by the amount of

water needed to sufficiently strip PZ from the residue. For the base design, it was decided to feed

enough water during boiloff to reach 0.74 wt % PZ in the residue, as this was what was done at

TCM 2025. To estimate the PZ remaining in the residue for this plant, the cumulative water fed

during TCM 2025 Event 1 was normalized by the total amount of solvent treated (Figure 4).

Equation 1 shows the exponential fit used to determine the fraction of PZ in the residue at different

points during the caustic treatment and boiloff periods. To achieve the target of 0.74 wt % PZ in

the residue, 11,080 MT of water needed to be fed in addition to the solvent feed.

                                           [𝑃𝑍] = 0.2051𝑒 −4.11𝑅                                  (1)

where:
 [PZ] = PZ in residue (mass frac);
 R = Cumulative water fed to the reclaimer as a fraction of solvent treated (kg/kg).

 10

 Figure 4: Boiloff required for a given PZ recovery

Caustic Feed

 The reclaimer waste composition was estimated from TCM 2025 results. It was assumed

the solvent composition when reclaiming was initiated at the design conditions would be identical

to the composition at TCM 2025 when the NH3 emissions limit was reached. The upper emissions

limit of NH3, normalized by gas rate and inventory, is 0.324 mol/h/m3 of inventory, which was

reached at about 750 h at TCM 2025. The composition of the solvent at this point, along with the

removals from the TCM 2025 Event 1, were used to estimate the composition of the reclaimer

residue and the amount of caustic addition needed (Table 3).

Table 3: Estimated solvent composition at the start of reclaiming and the expected removal
 of degradation products at TEA conditions
 Total Total Total
 PZAA AEG 2-OPZA 3-OPZA Glycine Formate EDA PZAA AEG Formate
Solvent Before Reclaiming
                             17    17     6.6       13          5.1       13      49     61      45       18
 (mmol/kg)
      Removal (%)            61    76      94       96          95        79      49     86      84       85

 11

 Enough caustic must be added to hydrolyze all CO2-bound amine and amide degradation

products along with charge balancing any HSS. From Table 3, it was assumed all removed species

ended up in the reclaimer. First, sufficient caustic must be added to convert the amides of formate,

PZAA, and AEG to their parent species, with the goal of recovering PZ that may be bound in

amides such as NFPZ. Furthermore, since the reactions are not fully known, enough caustic must

also be added to convert OPZA species to EDDA, even though this hydrolysis does not recover

any PZ. Next, it is assumed that some CO2 will remain bound to amine species in the reclaimer,

although much of the CO2 is expected to be stripped during the solvent feed period. A residual

loading of 0.05 mol CO2/mol alkalinity was assumed. This may be an overestimate, but further

work is required to refine this assumption. Caustic must also be added to charge balance the

resulting carbonate salts and HSS such as formate. Additional caustic will be necessary if fed

simultaneously with the solvent feed because it will continuously neutralize any residual dissolved

CO2 being fed. Equation 2 shows the details of this calculation. For the TEA, it was assumed that

a 20% excess would be required, although additional work is needed to determine the actual

required excess.

 𝑚𝑜𝑙 𝑁 𝑚𝑜𝑙 𝑎𝑚𝑖𝑛𝑒

where:
 mNaOH = Required mass of NaOH(kg);
 mresidue = Mass of reclaimer residue (kg);
 [] = Concentration of a given species (mmol/kg);
 T = “Total” or hydrolyzed HSS, PZAA, or AEG (mmol/kg).

 Based on the estimated residue composition, about 608 kmol of NaOH is required for 1:1

treatment. Assuming a 20% excess of NaOH, 39 m3 of 50 wt % NaOH would be added at 1.62

m3/h. The rest of the feed during this period would be 14.35 m3/h of water. By the end of the

caustic treatment period, it was assumed all CO2 is converted to sodium carbonate. An additional

20.5 MT PZ was freed from weak acid interactions and returned to the process. This analysis

 12

assumed caustic treatment was necessary, even though 20.5 MT PZ is only 1.4% of the original

PZ inventory. Future work could include a sensitivity analysis to determine when the cost of PZ

make-up associated with no caustic treatment is less than the annualized cost associated with

caustic treatment.

Reclaimer Feed Rate

 The volumetric feed to the reclaimer was assumed to be constant during active reclaiming

periods. With this assumption and knowing how much solvent, caustic, and water needed to be

fed throughout the 1,584 h of a single reclaiming campaign, the total reclaimer feed needed to be

maintained at 15.97 m3/h. At this constant feed rate and a set 24 h for caustic treatment, the solvent

feed period was 890 h and the boiloff period was 670 h. To maintain water balance, it is ideal to

use bleed water from the water wash to feed the reclaimer. It was estimated there would be about

40 m3/h of water available from the water wash bleed that would be used for the water feed. This

was estimated from the average bleed rate from TCM 2025 and was scaled to the TEA conditions

by gas rate.

Residue Composition

 The estimated composition of the reclaimer residue at each period of operation can be

found in the corresponding stream table for that period (Tables 4–7). The residue consists of water,

PZ, CO2, and NaOH, with the remaining composition (denoted as “other”) encompassing

degradation products and other impurities. Since the reclaimer operation was dynamic, the

composition of the residue was always changing. The composition in the stream tables reflects

the composition at the end of the listed operational period. The water content at each phase of

operation was maintained at 37 wt % because this was what was maintained at TCM 2025 to ensure

solvent pumpability. The PZ content at each period was determined by Equation 1, with

 13

cumulative water addition being counted from the start of the caustic treatment period. The total

amount of Na in the waste remained constant from the caustic treatment period onward. The CO2

in the residue was set by the assumed loading of amines after the solvent feed period. The total

amount of degradation products in the residue was determined by Table 3. The final reclaimer

waste was 195 MT, or about 185 m3. Disposal would require 7 trucks per event.

Energy Requirements

 The energy requirement for the reclaimer, as normalized by the annual capture rate, was

0.06 GJ/MT CO2. Assuming a PZAS reboiler heat duty of 2.5 GJ/MT CO2, 2.3% of the annual

steam requirements would be from the reclaiming unit (Rochelle et al., 2022). However, the

reclaimer was on only 50% of the time. Therefore, during operation, the reclaimer required 4.8%

of reboiler steam. Any water or solvent boiled by the steam heater must then be condensed. Table

8 gives the estimated distillate flow, the steam heater and condenser duties, the steam and cooling

water requirements, and the solvent circulation rate through the steam heater for each operational

period. The properties of the available steam and cooling water are given in Table 9, along with

the other thermodynamic assumptions made for the energy balance calculations (Schmitt et al.,

2022).

Capital Expenses

 The estimated total purchased equipment cost (TPEC) was $1.2 MM. All cost estimates

are reported as USD, escalated to April 2024 using the Chemical Engineering Plant Cost Index

 17

(CEPCI) (“Economic Indicators,” 2024; “Economic Indicators,” 2021; Sinnott and Towler, 2020).

The most expensive pieces of process equipment were the circulation pump, reclaimer vessel,

steam heater, and condenser at 27, 24, 16, and 14% of the TPEC, respectively (Table 10). Costs

were based on the Mustang FEED or Sinnott & Towler (2020) when a relevant Mustang FEED

estimate was unavailable. Mustang FEED costs were escalated from August 2021. Pump costs

were scaled by brake power, heat exchangers by area, pressure vessels by steel mass, and tanks by

volume, using Sinnott & Towler scaling factors. The caustic tank was costed as epoxy-lined

carbon steel, based on the Mustang FEED caustic storage tank TK-30100. All pumps were scaled

from Mustang FEED centrifugal pump P-50850, except the caustic feed pump, which was scaled

from the Mustang FEED caustic drain pump P-30100, a 316 SS diaphragm pump. Heat exchangers

were scaled from carbon steel U-tube shell-and-tube exchangers in Sinnott & Towler, using a

material factor of 1.3 to escalate costs for 304 SS (Sinnott and Towler, 2020). All equipment not

otherwise specified was assumed to be 304 SS.

 19

 The largest expense was the solvent circulation pump, which is dependent on the solvent

circulation rate and the pump head. Increasing the length of reclaiming and/or reducing the amount

of water fed during the boiloff period would decrease the required solvent circulation rate. It

therefore follows that the steam heater and condenser would be smaller and therefore cheaper. The

pump head, the other factor determining the cost of the circulation pump, was determined by the

pressure required to prevent flashing in the steam heater (4.3 bara), and assuming a 1 bar pressure

drop across the steam heater (Table 10).

 The other largest expenses were the condenser and steam heater, which are sized based on

heat exchange area. Area is estimated by calculating a log mean temperature difference (LMTD)

and assuming an overall heat transfer coefficient (U). The LMTD calculated is dependent on

assumptions that the solvent in the steam heater would be heated to 165 C and that cooling water

return could be 20 C colder than the supply temperature (Table 11). Further, U was roughly

estimated as 500 and 1,000 W/m2/K for the steam heater and condenser, respectively, but this value

could be more rigorously calculated.

 Table 11: Information used for sizing heat exchangers

 Condenser Steam Heater
     LMTD (K)                  U (W/m2K)             LMTD (K)             U (W/m2K)
       94                         1,000                129                   500

 The total plant cost (TPC) for reclaiming was estimated at $5.8 MM. The TPEC was scaled

by a factor of 5 to account for equipment erection, piping, instrumentation, controls, electrical,

civil works, structures, insulation, paint, offsites, design, engineering, and contingency (Sinnott

and Towler, 2020). The CapEx was annualized by assuming a 30-year plant life and an interest

rate of 5%, as assumed in the NETL base scenario. These values give a capital recovery factor

(CRF) of 0.07 and an annualized CapEx of $380,000.

 20

Operating Expenses

 Annual operating expense (OpEx) was estimated at $1.7 MM, making the total annual cost

(TAC) $2.1 MM to implement reclaiming (Table 12). OpEx cost assumptions can be found in

Table 13. OpEx was divided into six categories: O&M, steam, NaOH, PZ make-up, waste

disposal, and electricity. The OpEx reported here is the incremental cost associated with the

addition of reclaiming to PZAS. PZ make-up only accounts for PZ loss to the reclaimer residue

and accounts for only 1.7% of the OpEx in the base design. Similarly to PZ make-up, steam and

electricity costs are only those associated with reclaiming process equipment. Steam costs make

up 38.3% of the OpEx, and as previously discussed the amount of steam required is directly linked

to the decision on how much water to feed during boiloff. Water was not considered in the OpEx,

because it was assumed the water feed would be from the water wash bleed. Waste was assumed

to be hazardous; the cost of waste disposal would be halved if deemed non-hazardous. However,

because not a lot of waste is produced, waste still only makes up 3.7% of the OpEx.

Bleed and Feed Scenario

 To efficiently achieve NH3 emissions targets for the given process conditions, there are

three options: reclaiming, bleed and feed, and acid washing. Acid wash is outside the scope of

this work, but future work could include evaluating when utilizing acid wash to reach emissions

targets is more economically favorable than reclaiming. Bleed and feed operation involves

continuously replacing a set amount of solvent with fresh PZ to maintain a constant NH3 emissions

rate of 5 ppm. Normalizing this target by gas rate and liquid inventory gives aammonia emissions

of 0.18 mol/m3/h. It was assumed the normalized NH3 generation rate would increase linearly

with the accumulation of amine degradation species in the solvent. Therefore, to maintain a

constant NH3 generation, solvent needs to bleed at a rate that maintains a constant concentration

of degradation products. At TCM 2025, NH3 emissions reached 0.18 mol/m3/h when there was

112 mol/kg of amine degradation species.
 To calculate the bleed rate, the rate of accumulation of amine degradation species needs to

be estimated. The accumulation of degradation species was assumed to primarily be a function of

NO2. The accumulation rate, based on scaling the rate at NCCC 2023 when prescrubbing was off

by the total inventory, was 474 mol/h of amine degradation products (Chen, 2024), making the

bleed rate to maintain 5 ppm NH3 at the design conditions 4.2 MT/h. At this rate, the entire solvent

inventory would be replaced 7.4 times annually. It was assumed that the liquid waste from bleed-

and-feed would be non-hazardous. To replace the PZ bled, 1.8 MT/h of 68 wt % PZ needed to be

fed. The annual cost of PZ make-up was the main driver of OpEx, at $79 MM (Table 14). The

 22

CapEx was assumed to only consist of a feed pump. It was assumed that waste would be directly

filled into tanker trucks, and at the rate of bleed a 30 MT truck would be filled every 7 h. The cost

of bleed and feed would be $29.72/MT CO2. This analysis clearly indicates that bleed and feed is

not a viable option for managing NH3 emissions. For a lower NO2 flue gas source, the rate of

degradation may be slow enough to make bleed and feed viable. A bleed rate of 105 kg/h would

yield the same annual cost as the base reclaiming design.

Cost Minimization and Sensitivity Analyses

 The length of a reclaiming campaign and the amount of boiloff were optimized at the base

case cost assumptions to minimize the incremental cost of reclaiming. The optimized cost of

reclaiming was $0.73/MT CO2, with active reclaiming 41% of the time and an annualized heat
duty of 0.047 GJ/MT CO2. The variable costs associated with the cost of capture minimization

are the cost of PZ make-up, steam, and waste disposal. The base case assumed $5,000/MT 68 wt %

PZ, $4.42/MMBTU, and $80/ton hazardous waste. A sensitivity analysis was performed to see

how the minimum cost and the optimum operating conditions changed with these variable costs.

 The minimum cost of reclaiming is most sensitive to fluctuations in natural gas prices

(Figure 5). Over a range of $1–20/MMBTU, the minimum cost ranges from $0.57–1.35/MT CO2.

 23

The reclaimer heat duty is sensitive to both fluctuations in natural gas prices and the cost of PZ

make-up (Figures 5 and 6). As the cost of gas increases, there is an economic incentive to operate

less boiloff so that less steam is required and to therefore lose more PZ to the reclaimer residue.

As PZ costs increase, there is the opposite incentive to operate more boiloff and therefore have a

larger reclaimer heat duty. Figure 7 shows how the incremental cost of reclaiming and the optimal

operating conditions are minimally dependent on the cost of waste disposal. This phenomenon is

due to the small amount of expected waste and the orders of magnitude lower cost of waste disposal

compared to the PZ make-up cost. Across scenarios, the optimal length of the reclaiming period

is about 40% of the plant operating period.

 Figure 5: Sensitivity analysis minimizing incremental cost of reclaiming for a range of
 natural gas prices. Base design assumed $4.42/MMBTU.

 24

Figure 6: Sensitivity analysis minimizing incremental cost of reclaiming for a range of PZ
 make-up costs. Base design assumed $5,000/MT PZ.

 Figure 7: Sensitivity analysis minimizing incremental cost of reclaiming for a range of
 waste disposal costs. Base design assumed $73/MT hazardous waste.

 25

CONCLUSIONS

• A TEA of intermittent thermal reclaiming with PZAS on a 942 MWeq greenfield NGCC

 showed a base-case incremental cost of $0.76/mt CO2, or 1.2% of the total capture cost, based

 on a $5.8 MM CapEx ($0.38 MM annualized) and $1.7 MM annualized OpEx.

 • The solvent circulation pump and reclaimer vessel represented the costliest pieces of

 process equipment, at 27% and 24% of the TPEC, respectively.

 • O&M and steam accounted for 46% and 38% of the total OpEx, respectively.

 • The base design assumed a 1.4% PZ loss and 50% reclaimer uptime, and optimization

 of these parameters reduced the total cost to $0.73/MT CO₂.

• Reclaiming should be operated in four distinct phases: solvent feed, caustic treatment, boiloff,

 and waste disposal. Caustic treatment should not overlap with solvent feed to minimize caustic

 requirement.

• Three inventories of solvent should be treated to restore solvent health. More work is required

 to determine if this may be further reduced.

• Stripper and reclaimer operation should be decoupled for high pressure strippers like PZAS.

 Operating this way necessitates condensing and returning reclaimer distillate to the absorber.

• Use NH3 measured by FTIR or PTR-TOF-MS as a primary indicator for when to reclaim. The

 frequency with which an NH3 limit will be reached will depend primarily on NO2 in the flue

 gas for PZAS. Higher NO2 flue gases will increase the cost of capture and the incremental cost

 of reclaiming.

 • The reclaiming frequency was set at 2.8 times per year, assuming 2.5 ppm NO₂ in the

 inlet gas and an NH₃ emissions target of 5 ppm.

• NH3 emissions can be managed three ways: bleed and feed, reclaiming, or acid washing.

 Maintaining a 5 ppm NH3 emissions target at the TEA conditions through bleed and feed alone

 would require replacing the entire inventory 7 times annually. This is economically and

 26

 practically infeasible, making the use of solvent management strategies like thermal reclaiming

 or implementing emissions mitigation techniques such as an acid wash necessary.

• The annualized heat duty for the base case was estimated at 0.06 GJ/MT CO₂, corresponding

 to approximately 4% of a typical PZAS reboiler duty.

 • The cost of PZ and steam ultimately governed the optimal amount of boiloff, while

 hazardous waste disposal costs were a minor consideration since they are two orders of

 magnitude lower per MT than the cost of PZ make-up. Higher cost of steam

 incentivizes operating less boiloff to decrease reclaimer heat duty, while higher PZ cost

 incentivizes operating more boiloff to increase PZ recovery.

 • Reducing the boiloff by 48% achieved the economic optimum PZ recovery,

 corresponding to an annualized heat duty of 0.047 GJ/MT CO₂.

 • The amount of boiloff can be optimized in real time by the operator depending on the

 current price of PZ and steam. The timing of reclaiming initiation could potentially be

 adjusted so that it operates when natural gas prices are low. Using an acid wash would

 allow for more flexibility with the reclaiming schedule while still meeting emissions

 targets. Alternatively, the reclaimer operation could be spread out over the entire

 reclaiming cycle so that it operates when gas prices are projected to be lowest.

• Over a range of $1–20/MMBTU for the price of natural gas, the minimum cost of reclaiming

    ranges from $0.57–1.35/MT CO2

• Only 195 MT of waste was produced per reclaiming event. Because this volume is small and

 PZ make-up is roughly two orders of magnitude more expensive than hazardous waste

 disposal, the cost of waste disposal had a minimal impact on the TEA.

• Caustic treatment is necessary to maximize PZ recovery, but its economic benefit depends on

 balancing the additional CapEx and OpEx against the cost of PZ make-up associated with

 higher PZ losses if caustic treatment was omitted.

 27

• Future work should include evaluating the cost of variations on reclaimer operation including

 continuous operation and operating without caustic treatment. Furthermore, an analysis of

 when emissions should be managed with acid wash rather than thermal reclaiming should be

 conducted. While reclaiming is necessary for maintaining solvent health as well as managing

 emissions, there is potentially some economic benefit to using an acid wash to help maintain

 emissions within permitting limits. There could be further comparison between thermal

 reclaiming and other strategies for managing solvent health, such as N2 sparging, NO2 removal,

 and carbon treating.

Techno-Economic Analysis of Nitrogen Sparging

 by

 James I. Obute and Gary T. Rochelle

Submitted under Cooperative Agreement No: DE-FE0031861

 SUBMITTED

                        August 15, 2026

 by

 The University of Texas at Austin
 McKetta Department of Chemical Engineering
 200 E Dean Keeton St. Stop C0400
                       Austin, TX 78712

 PRINCIPAL INVESTIGATOR

 Dr. Gary T. Rochelle
 gtr@che.utexas.edu

 SUBMITTED TO

 U.S. Department of Energy
 Office of Fossil Energy
 National Energy Technology Laboratory

 1

 1. Introduction

 A techno-economic analysis (TEA) was conducted to evaluate the feasibility and value of

implementing nitrogen sparging in a commercial natural gas combined cycle (NGCC) power plant

operating with the Piperazine with the Advanced Stripper (PZAS) process. The objective was to

quantify the capital and operating costs associated with adding nitrogen sparging, along with the

resulting economic and environmental benefits at full scale. The design basis followed the 2022

National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL) cost baseline for a greenfield NGCC facility with

post-combustion carbon capture (Schmitt et al., 2022). Experimental findings from the 2023

National Carbon Capture Center (NCCC) campaign established the extent to which nitrogen

sparging stripped dissolved oxygen, forming the performance basis for this analysis. Honeywell

provided cost estimates for supplying 99.5%-purity nitrogen (N2) generated by an on-site pressure

swing adsorption (PSA) unit, enabling a realistic assessment of commercial deployment.

 Table 1 summarizes the principal results of the TEA. N2 sparging produces $1.84 MM/y

in operational savings, primarily through reduced solvent losses, lower steam consumption, and

reduced waste handling. The required capital and operating charges total $0.89 MM/y, yielding a

net annual benefit of $0.95 MM, equivalent to $0.35/t of CO₂ captured, where t denotes a metric

ton (1,000 kg). Although this cost impact is modest relative to the full capture cost of

approximately $55/t CO₂, N2 sparging meaningfully improves solvent quality, long-term

operability, and overall environmental performance. The accompanying 0.06 GJ/t CO₂ reduction

in reclaimer duty reflects less degraded solvent being processed and therefore less waste generated,

reinforcing the environmental incentive for implementation.

  Table 1: Key Techno-Economic Outcomes of Nitrogen Sparging for a 942 MWeq NGCC
 PZAS Facility

 2

 Performance Impacts Reclaimer duty reduction 0.06 GJ/t CO₂

 N₂ sparging capital cost 2.6 $MM

1.1 Operational Context and Insights from NCCC Testing

 Nitrogen sparging was evaluated at pilot scale during the 2023 NCCC campaign, which

was conducted using the PZAS configuration for 5,900 hours under representative NGCC flue-gas

conditions at a CO₂ capture rate of 0.28 t/h (Closmann, 2024). The absorber sump was selected as

the sparging location because oxidative degradation starts at this point, immediately after oxygen

dissolves in the rich solvent.

 A porous stainless steel sparger (Mott Media Grade 2, 316 SS, cross-sectional area 0.26

ft²) was used for gas distribution. The design maximized cross-sectional area to minimize

superficial gas velocity, promoting finer bubble dispersion and higher mass-transfer efficiency.

According to the Mott design guide, in-tank applications without agitation should use gas exit

velocities of 5–10 fpm, with an upper limit of 25 fpm. The guide emphasizes that fine-bubble

 3

propagation is essential because smaller bubbles provide greater interfacial area for mass transfer.

Consistent with these recommendations, nitrogen flow rates of 0.5–4 scfm resulted in gas exit

velocities below 25 fpm, enabling efficient DO stripping in the absorber sump (Figure 1 shows the

sparger used during the campaign).

 Figure 1: Porous stainless steel sparger (Mott Media Grade 2) used for DO stripping
 during the 2023 NCCC campaign

 DO concentrations were monitored at two locations: the pump-around intercooling (IC)

loop of absorber bed 1, which represents absorber-sump conditions, and the rich-amine piping

downstream of the rich-amine tank and carbon bed (Figure 2).

 4

  Figure 2: Schematic of the NCCC PZAS process highlighting the two DO measurement
 locations
 The absorber sump circulated approximately 12.2 m³/h of solvent. Because the intercooler

continuously recirculated flow through bed 1, the sump exhibited substantial back-mixing; each

parcel of solvent passed through the sump roughly three times before exiting the absorber. The

residence time in the rich amine tank and carbon bed was approximately 100 minutes, allowing

adequate time for DO consumption by readily oxidizable degradation intermediates.

 DO measurements collected throughout the NCCC campaign demonstrate how nitrogen

sparging reduces DO concentration in the absorber sump. Figure 3 shows that before sparging,

DO in the IC loop was 4 mg/L, while at the downstream location it was ~1.8 mg/L. This indicates

that oxidative reactions between the absorber and the downstream location consumed more than

half of the entering oxygen. When nitrogen sparging was turned on, DO in the IC loop decreased

by 90%, to ~0.5 mg/L. This reduction corresponded directly with decreases in measured

degradation products, confirming that DO suppression slows oxidative degradation.

 5

 Figure 3: DO measurements during the 2023 NCCC PZAS pilot campaign

 Controlled stripping tests showed that DO removal efficiency depended strongly on

nitrogen flow rate. Figure 4 demonstrates that all flow rates enabled sufficient gas-liquid contact,

but the lowest flow rate (0.5 scfm) consistently produced the highest stripping efficiency. At this

rate, DO removal exceeded 90%, and the sparger operated reliably without affecting column

performance. Higher nitrogen flow rates reduced stripping efficiency, consistent with mass-

transfer fundamentals: greater gas throughput in a large-area sparger produces larger bubbles and

reduces effective interfacial contact. This effect was most evident at very high flows (10 to 20

scfm), where stripping performance declined sharply. Importantly, the 0.5-scfm flow contributed

less than 0.05% of the total gas traffic through the absorber, indicating that effective DO stripping

can be achieved with negligible hydraulic impact.

 6

 Figure 4: Short-term DO measurements at varying nitrogen sparge rates during the
 2023 NCCC campaign

 These tests demonstrate that a low nitrogen sparge rate of 0.5 scfm can consistently

suppress DO in the absorber sump by more than 90%. This performance anchors the scaling

assumptions and degradation-rate modeling applied in this TEA.

1.2 Basis for Nitrogen Sparging Retrofit Design

 The TEA is benchmarked to Case B32B.95 of the NETL Cost and Performance Baseline

for Fossil Energy Plants, which evaluates a greenfield NGCC facility with 95% CO₂ capture

(Schmitt et al., 2022). The reference plant includes two 2017 H-class gas turbines, two heat-

recovery steam generators, and one steam turbine operating in a 2×2×1 configuration. While the

NETL baseline employs the CANSOLV process for CO₂ removal, this study assumes an

equivalent capture rate of 314 t CO₂/h using 30 wt % piperazine (PZ) solvent in the PZAS

configuration without pump-around intercooling. This distinction is important because solvent

stability, oxygen mass transfer, and catalytic degradation pathways vary significantly among post-

combustion CO₂ capture technologies, and the absence of intercooling alters the residence time in

the absorber sump where oxidation may predominantly occur.

 7

 The nitrogen sparging retrofit was developed by scaling operating experience from the

2023 NCCC campaign to a TEA design based on the NETL cost baseline. In the baseline case,

NETL specifies a CO₂ capture rate of 314 t/h. This capture rate was translated into an equivalent

rich solvent flow rate for PZAS by applying the ΔCO₂ loading measured during the 2023 NCCC

campaign. Under those conditions, the solvent achieved a net CO₂ uptake of 0.2 mol CO₂ per mol

alkalinity. Table 2 summarizes the resulting process rates for both systems, including solvent flow

rate, DO loading, and nitrogen demand required to remove 90% of the DO. The TEA design

solvent flow through the absorber sump (5,523 m³/h) is approximately 450 times greater than that

at NCCC, and all sparging parameters were scaled proportionally. A DO concentration of 4 mg/L

was applied in both cases to represent the solubility of oxygen in the amine because of O₂ ingress

from oxygen-containing NGCC flue gas, consistent with NCCC measurements and appropriate for

evaluating oxygen-removal requirements.

Table 2: Key process parameters for the TEA design (NETL basis) compared with the 2023
 NCCC campaign

 TEA (NETL baseline) NCCC 2023
               CO2 capture rate (t/h)                            314                    0.28
 Rich solvent flow (m3/h) 5523 3.9
 Total solvent flow through absorber sump (m³/h) 5523 12.2 (8.3 IC)
 DO loading at 4 mg/L (kg/h) 22 0.05
       N2 required for 90% DO removal (t/h)                      0.46                  0.001
 Equivalent N2 flowrate (scfm) 227 0.5

1.2.1 Removal of the Intercooling Loop in the Design Basis

 The TEA absorber does not include the pump-around intercooling (IC) loop that was

present at NCCC. In the NCCC configuration, the IC loop increased the solvent flow through the

absorber because solvent was continuously withdrawn, cooled externally, and returned to the

 8

column. This created a solvent movement through the section where nitrogen stripping occurred

that was higher than the nominal process flowrate. As a result, nitrogen demand at NCCC reflected

this elevated movement rather than the baseline solvent flow expected without intercooling.

 Eliminating the IC loop in the TEA design avoids this additional movement. The solvent

stream passing through the sparging zone reflects only the process flow required for CO2 capture,

and nitrogen requirement scales accordingly. This simplifies solvent routing and reduces total

nitrogen demand while preserving absorber hydrodynamics and CO2 capture performance.

1.2.2. Scaling Nitrogen Sparging Requirements

 At NCCC, oxygen removal was achieved using a single porous sparging pipe with a sparge

cross-sectional area of 0.26 ft² and a nitrogen flowrate of 0.5 scfm. In the TEA absorber sump,

the required nitrogen rate scales directly with the solvent-rate ratio from Table 2, giving a total

sparging requirement of 227 scfm. This flow corresponds to a sparging cross-sectional area of

118.2 ft².

 A single pipe cannot provide this area while maintaining an appropriate bubble size, so the

TEA design employs a modular array of sparging elements. Multiple parallel pipes or a grid-type

diffuser assembly increase interfacial area while keeping superficial gas velocity within the range

that maximizes oxygen desorption efficiency in large-scale oxygen-stripping systems. This

configuration aligns with industrial practice, where high-area diffusers enhance mass transfer

without causing excessive solvent agitation. Figure 5 presents a schematic of the nitrogen sparging

configuration implemented for the 942 MWeq NGCC with PZAS.

 9

 Figure 5: N₂ sparging configuration and integration into the PZAS process for the
 942 MWeq NGCC

1.2.3. Basis for Oxygen–Piperazine Reaction Stoichiometry

 The TEA quantifies solvent savings from DO removal using a mechanistic stoichiometry

of 2 mol O₂ consumed per mol PZ degraded. This value reflects the dominant oxidative pathways

observed during pilot testing. Formation of aminoethylglycine (AEG) requires roughly 1 mol O₂

per mol PZ, while pathways that generate ammonia require approximately 4 mol O₂ per mol PZ.

The selected 2:1 O₂:PZ ratio is a representative average of these competing routes under typical

absorber conditions. Using this mechanistic basis ensures that predicted PZ losses reflect actual

pilot-scale chemistry rather than a single-pathway assumption. A sensitivity analysis was

performed to evaluate how changes in assumed stoichiometry affect solvent preservation

outcomes.

1.3 Material Balance for the Base Case and the Nitrogen-Sparging Retrofit

 The material balances for the 942 MWeq NGCC with 95% CO₂ capture were based on the

NETL B32B.95 baseline, which defines the flue gas flow, CO₂ product rate and composition, and

 10

major process operating conditions. Solvent flows and compositions were established using

performance measured during the 2023 NCCC campaign and the Mustang FEED study. A DO

concentration of 4 mg/L was applied to the absorber-sump rich solvent and to the rich solvent

exiting the absorber in the case without nitrogen sparging, consistent with NCCC measurements

under oxygen-containing flue gas. In the nitrogen sparging retrofit, 90% of the DO in the absorber-

sump rich solvent is removed, so the downstream rich solvent stream has 10% of the baseline

oxygen concentration.

1.3.1 Solvent Circulation and Nitrogen Requirements

 The circulating solvent flow was determined by applying 0.2 mol CO₂/mol alkalinity net

uptake measured during the 2023 NCCC campaign to the NETL capture rate of 314 t CO₂/h. This

approach produces a lean-rich solvent circulation consistent with NCCC operation and aligned

with the Mustang FEED design basis (Rochelle, 2022). Using the measured Δ-loading ensures

that the TEA reflects pilot-scale PZAS performance without imposing assumed lean or rich solvent

compositions.

 For the nitrogen-sparging retrofit, the required N₂ flowrate was scaled directly from NCCC

performance using the solvent rate ratio in Table 2. This yields a total sparging requirement of

227 scfm to remove 90% of DO from the absorber-sump rich solvent. In the base case, no nitrogen

is introduced, and the rich solvent exits the absorber with its DO content determined by equilibrium

at absorber conditions.

1.3.2 Oxidative Degradation Rate and PZ Make-Up

 Oxidative solvent loss was quantified using formation rates of nitrogen-containing

degradation products measured during the 2023 NCCC campaign (Chen, 2024). Because the

campaign did not operate at steady state, a representative oxidation rate of 0.6 mol PZ degraded

 11

per hour was estimated from the time-dependent accumulation of identified products. Table 3

summarizes the corresponding PZ-oxidation rates for the base case and the N₂-sparging case.

 Table 3: Estimated PZ Oxidation Rates Based on Identified Degradation Products

 Case Estimated PZ Oxidation (kg/y) kg PZ/t CO₂

            Base case                            5.08 × 10⁵                        0.20

        N₂-sparging case                         2.73 × 10⁵                        0.10

 For the N₂-sparging case, the reduction in oxidation rate was determined by applying a 2:1

O₂:PZ stoichiometry to the 90% reduction in DO. The decrease in available oxygen proportionally

lowers the effective DO oxidation rate, which in turn reduces the overall oxidation rate.

 Nitrogen sparging decreases the overall oxidation rate by 46%. This reduction lowers the

annual PZ make-up requirement from 508 t/y to 273 t/y, yielding a net savings of 235 t/y, the

largest contributor to the net annual cost benefit.

 No intrinsic mechanism is expected to cause degradation of nitrogen sparging performance

over the life of the plant. Any sparger plugging or changes in gas distribution can be detected via

routine pressure and flow monitoring and corrected during normal plant maintenance.

 Short-term off-design operating conditions with increased oxygen input for periods on the

order of hours are not important, because ammonia and other degradation products take days to

accumulate. Furthermore, gas turbines already provide a high concentration of oxygen, so

additional oxygen is not expected to make a significant difference.

1.3.3 Integration of Reclaimer Design and Scaling Methodology

 Reclaimer mass and energy demands were estimated by scaling the process model

developed in a previous reclaimer TEA (Plantz, 2025). For the present analysis, the model outputs

 12

were adjusted to match the calculated base-case degradation rate of 5.08×10⁵ kg/y. This requires

a scaling factor of 2.0 relative to the reference case, and applied consistently to the reclaimer feed,

waste production, steam duty, and cooling-water requirements.

 For the N₂-sparging retrofit, the same method was applied using the reduction in oxidative

solvent loss determined in Section 1.3.2. The sparging case exhibits approximately half the base-

case degradation (2.73×10⁵ kg/y, or 0.10 kg/t CO₂), so a scaling factor of 0.5 was applied to all

reclaimer duties relative to the base case design.

 The lower oxidation rate reduces the frequency and volume of solvent sent to the reclaimer,

leading to proportional reductions in reclaimer throughput and utilities. This corresponds to a

decrease in reclaimer energy demand of 0.06 GJ/t CO₂.

1.3.4 Overall Material Balances

 The complete process material balances for both the base and N₂-sparging cases are

presented in the stream tables (Tables 4a–5b). Across both designs, the bulk CO₂-capture flows

remain unchanged:

 • CO₂ capture rate and CO₂ product quality are identical, as they follow the fixed CDR

 specification.

 • N₂ ingress occurs only in the sparging case, introduced solely through the injected

 nitrogen, while the base-case solvent flow contains no nitrogen.

 Together, these stream tables show that the retrofit modifies only the DO pathway, leaving

absorber–stripper operation and CO₂-capture performance intact.

1.4 Energy Reduction from N2 Sparging

 The reclaimer steam-heater and condenser duties in this TEA were scaled from the

reference thermal reclaimer model (Plantz, 2025). Because nitrogen sparging lowers oxidative

solvent loss, significantly less solvent must be periodically withdrawn and thermally reclaimed.

In this design, the required reclaiming frequency decreases from approximately 2.8 cycles per year

in the reference TEA to 1.5 cycles per year.

 The resulting reductions in reclaimer mass throughput and utilities are summarized in

Table 6 (base case) and Table 7 (N₂-sparging retrofit). Based on these scaled duties, the retrofit

saves 7.55×10⁷ kg/y of steam and 1.49×10⁵ GJ/y of heat input, equivalent to 0.06 GJ/t CO₂.

1.5 N2 Sparging Capital Cost

 Nitrogen sparging introduces two distinct capital requirements: an on-site pressure swing

adsorption (PSA) nitrogen supply system and the sparging hardware integrated into the absorber

sump.

 18

1.5.1 PSA Nitrogen Supply Capital

 The nitrogen requirement for this TEA (227 scfm or 11 t/d) was obtained by scaling the

0.5 scfm sparging rate demonstrated at NCCC to the full-scale solvent flow through the absorber

sump.

 Capital cost information for nitrogen production was provided by Honeywell for an on-site

pressure swing adsorption (PSA) system delivering 99.5 percent purity nitrogen. Their 2019 basis

expressed capital as annualized unit costs of $32.3/ton N2 for return on capital employed (10

percent ROCE) and $29/ton N2 for straight-line depreciation.

 To express these values in 2024 USD, the 2019 unit costs were escalated using the

Chemical Engineering Plant Cost Index (CEPCI), following standard process-design economic

practice (Chemical Engineering “Economic Indicators,” 2021; 2024; Sinnott and Towler, 2020).

This adjustment normalizes the supplier-provided basis to current market conditions before

applying scale corrections.

 To reflect the smaller nitrogen production capacity assumed in this TEA, the Honeywell

basis was further adjusted using Towler’s capacity-scaling method for modular gas-processing

equipment (Sinnott & Towler, 2020):

                                                 CEPCI2024      S2 1−n
                  Unit Cost 2 = Unit Cost1 × (             ) ×(    )                         (1)
                                                 CEPCI2019      S1

 where n = 0.8, appropriate for modern compression and separation systems, and S₂/S₁ =

0.55, representing the ratio between the TEA nitrogen flow (11 t/d) and the Honeywell 20-t/d

reference.

 Application of Equation 1 yields updated costs of $48/ton N2 for ROCE, $43/ton N2 for

depreciation, and an installed capital cost of $1.92 MM for the nitrogen-generation system at the

TEA scale.

 19

1.5.2 Sparging Hardware Capital

 The absorber sump requires distributed sparging hardware to deliver nitrogen uniformly

and achieve the target mass-transfer performance. During the NCCC campaign, Mott Corporation

quoted $1,063 for a porous 316-SS sparging element capable of 0.5 scfm delivery with 0.26 ft² of

active area.

 To scale this hardware to the full-scale absorber, Towler’s cost–capacity relationship was

applied using a flow-based scaling ratio of 455 (corresponding to the increase from 0.5 to 227

scfm). This results in an estimated scaled hardware cost of approximately $142,000 for the

required diffuser capacity.

 A cost multiplier of 5 was then applied to account for installation, connections, supports,

and integration within the sump.

1.5.3 Total Installed Capital and Annualization

 Combining the scaled PSA nitrogen generation system and the installed sparging hardware

yields a total installed capital cost of $2.6 MM for the nitrogen-sparging retrofit.

 The PSA nitrogen supply and sparging equipment are commercially mature, high-TRL

technologies. Accordingly, no explicit process contingency is applied, as technology-related

uncertainty is minimal. Any execution-related uncertainty is implicitly reflected in the vendor cost

basis and installation factors.

 NETL baseline studies typically annualize capital using a standardized Capital Charge

Factor based on a 30-year operating life. In this analysis, nitrogen supply costs are taken directly

from Honeywell’s vendor cost basis, which reports nitrogen production costs as a unit cost that

 20

embeds capital recovery through ROCE and depreciation. The nitrogen supply capital is therefore

inferred from the vendor cost structure rather than estimated independently.

 To maintain internal consistency with this cost basis, the Honeywell capital recovery

approach is retained. The resulting annualized capital charge is $0.49 MM/yr. This value is used

directly in the economic summary and cost-of-capture calculations that follow.

1.6 N2 Sparging Operating Cost

 The operating cost of nitrogen sparging reflects the expenses required to continuously

generate nitrogen using an on-site PSA system. These costs consist of electricity consumption and

operation and maintenance associated with PSA operation. Together, these operating elements

total $0.40 MM per year.

1.6.1 Nitrogen, Electricity, and Utility Charges

 Operating costs for nitrogen production were developed from the Honeywell PSA cost

basis. Electricity required a different escalation treatment because PSA power demand is a true

variable operating cost, not a capital-linked cost. Accordingly, cost adjustment was performed

using the change in electricity pricing and direct scaling with throughput rather than a capital-cost

exponent. Honeywell assumed $32/MWh, whereas the cost of electricity in the NETL cost

baseline is $66/MWh.

 Applying the capacity scaling and electricity adjustment results in a nitrogen electricity

cost of $41.7/ton of nitrogen, equivalent to an average PSA demand of 0.29 MW. This value is

consistent with expected energy intensities for modern PSA nitrogen systems and is incorporated

into the annual operating-cost calculation.

1.6.2 Operation and Maintenance (O&M) Scaling

 21

 The PSA cost basis provided only aggregate values for direct fixed cost (DFC) and

allocated fixed cost (AFC), without detailing their internal composition. To meaningfully scale

these charges to the retrofit design basis, cost drivers were interpreted using standard process-

economic guidance from Sinnott & Towler and Peters & Timmerhaus. DFC was separated into

maintenance, operator labor, and operating supplies (Peters, et al., 2003; Sinnott & Towler, 2020).

Maintenance and supplies were treated as capital-linked expenditures and scaled with Towler’s

cost scaling exponent for gas-processing systems (n = 0.8), while operator labor was handled as a

staffing requirement that varies more linearly with plant throughput than with physical equipment

size.

 AFC was interpreted as the sum of overhead functions such as maintenance overhead,

administration, and utilities infrastructure support. These charges scale with installed investment

and were therefore adjusted using the same n = 0.8 cost-scaling exponent.

 Before capacity scaling, the 2019 cost basis was escalated to 2024 USD using the Chemical

Engineering Plant Cost Index (CEPCI). Labor charges were adjusted separately using wage-

growth data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics Employment Cost Index (U.S. Bureau of Labor

Statistics, 2024). These updates were incorporated into the cost-capacity adjustment framework

of Equation 1, with CEPCI applied to capital-linked components and wage escalation applied to

labor components.

 After applying escalation and scaling corrections, the DFC and AFC contributions

converge to $33/ton and $25/ton of N2 delivered, respectively, resulting in a total O&M burden of

$58/ton. These adjustments preserve consistency with the logic of the Honeywell cost values while

enforcing the appropriate economic behavior when transferring the design basis to the smaller

retrofit scale.

 22

1.7 Total Annual Cost of Nitrogen Supply and Sparging

 Annual nitrogen supply totals $0.76 MM per year, equivalent to $191/ton of N2 delivered.

When the annualized capital burden for sparging installation is included, the total nitrogen-

sparging expense rises to $0.89 MM per year. These aggregate costs close the nitrogen-side

economics and frame the basis for evaluating the net benefit of DO removal in the following

section.

1.8 Net Economic Impact and Cost Basis for Nitrogen Sparging

 The retrofit alters key material flows, especially solvent loss and reclaimer throughput.

Table 8 summarizes the comparative mass-balance consequences for the base and nitrogen-

sparging designs, forming the foundation for monetized cost evaluation. Notably, the reduction in

PZ make-up corresponds to approximately 0.09 kg/t CO2 of solvent preserved.

 Table 8: Summary of Solvent Preservation and Utility Reductions from N2 Sparging

 Metric Base N₂-sparging Savings Unit
           PZ make-up                        508              273              235             t/yr
    PZ make-up cost ($5,000/t)               2.54             1.73            1.18           $MM/yr
 Reclaimer waste 927 498 429 t/yr
    Waste disposal cost ($80/t)              0.07             0.04            0.03           $MM/yr
           Steam mass                       163.2             87.6              76            kg/yr
         Reboiler energy                  3.23×105         1.73×105        1.49E×105          GJ/yr
        Reclaimer energy                  3.06×105         1.64×105         1.42×105        MMBtu/yr
    Steam cost ($4.42/MMBtu)                 1.35             0.73            0.63           $MM/yr
 Steam and disposal rates follow NETL reference values, while PZ pricing reflects an industry-provided
 commercial quotation.

 The decrease in PZ make-up constitutes the most significant chemical impact of the retrofit.

Curtailing DO suppresses oxidation pathways that convert active PZ into stable degradation

products. The resulting 46% reduction in solvent loss shows that limiting DO exposure materially

 23

improves solvent retention over prolonged operation, and this preservation of solvent inventory is

the dominant source of economic benefit captured in this TEA.

 This improvement cascades through the broader mass- and energy-balance of the system.

Lower solvent loss directly reduces annual procurement requirement, while less degraded material

entering the reclaimer allows reclaiming campaigns to be conducted less frequently, with a smaller

total mass of solvent processed per year. Correspondingly, residual solids requiring off-site

disposal decline. Because reclaiming is energy intensive, the reduction in solvent throughput also

decreases the required steam duty and cooling load. Thus, mitigating oxidative degradation not

only conserves chemical inventory but systematically reduces reclaimer waste generation and

auxiliary energy consumption, amplifying the value of the retrofit beyond simple solvent savings.

1.8.1 Nitrogen Supply Cost Basis

 Table 9 summarizes the baseline delivered nitrogen supply cost used in the economic

evaluation. This unit cost reflects the combined contributions of electricity, operation and

maintenance, and annualized capital recovery for the on-site PSA nitrogen system operating at the

design nitrogen flow rate.

 Table 9: Baseline Nitrogen Supply Cost Breakdown

 Cost Component Cost ($/ton N₂)
                         Electricity (0.29 MW)                 41.7
 Operation and maintenance 58
 Capital recovery 91
 Total delivered N2 cost 191

 This baseline nitrogen unit cost is carried forward into the net economic evaluation. To

assess sensitivity to uncertainty in nitrogen pricing, the delivered nitrogen unit cost is varied by

factors of 0.5 and 2 while holding nitrogen demand and all other economic assumptions constant.

 24

1.8.2 Net Economic Impact and Sensitivity to Nitrogen Cost

 Table 10 integrates all monetized effects of nitrogen sparging, combining reductions in

solvent make-up, steam consumption, and waste disposal with the costs associated with nitrogen

supply and sparging hardware. Results are reported for the baseline nitrogen cost and the two

bounding sensitivity cases.

 Table 10: Net Annual Economic Benefit of the Nitrogen Sparging Retrofit

 0.5× N2 cost Baseline N2 cost 2× N2 cost
 Economic Component (N2 = $191/ton) Units
                                        (N2 = $96/ton)                       (N2 = $382/ton)
      PZ make-up saved ($5,000/t)            1.18               1.18              1.18         $MM/yr

           Steam cost saved                  0.63              0.63               0.63         $MM/yr

    Waste disposal cost saved ($80/t)        0.03              0.03               0.03         $MM/yr

            Nitrogen supply                 − 0.38            − 0.76             −1.53         $MM/yr

 Spargers and piping CAPEX − 0.13 − 0.13 − 0.13 $MM/yr
                                             1.33              0.95               0.18         $MM/yr
 Net Annual Benefit
                                             0.48              0.35               0.07         $/t CO2

 All costs for N2 PSA plant are expressed in 2024 USD.

 At the baseline nitrogen cost, the retrofit yields a net annual benefit of approximately $0.95

MM/y, corresponding to $0.35/t CO₂ avoided. Even when the delivered nitrogen unit cost is

doubled, the retrofit remains net-positive, indicating that the economic case for sparging is robust

to reasonable uncertainty in nitrogen pricing.

 Although waste-handling savings appear modest at current U.S. disposal prices, this term

is highly site dependent. Industrial experience shows that hazardous waste charges escalate

substantially with increasing contaminant concentration, often reaching $600–$1,000/t for

regulated chemical residues. For this reason, $800/t (10×) was selected as a reasonable sensitivity

bound.

 25

 With waste disposal at $800/t, the waste-handling savings increase from $0.03 MM/y to

$0.34 MM/y. The net annual benefit increases to $1.26 MM/y ($0.50/t avoided CO₂). This

outcome illustrates that N2 sparging delivers its greatest economic leverage in systems where high

contaminant load or strict disposal requirements impose elevated waste-processing tariffs.

 Although the net economic benefit of sparging is positive, its magnitude ($0.35/t CO₂

avoided) is small relative to the underlying capture cost ($55/t CO₂) for NGCC post-combustion

systems (Schmitt et al., 2022). Nitrogen sparging is therefore not positioned as a cost-offsetting

retrofit but rather as a reliability and risk-mitigation measure that pays for itself over time. With

an installed capital requirement of $2.6 MM and annual savings near $0.95 MM, the implied 2.7-

year payback places the retrofit firmly within acceptable industrial investment thresholds.

 More importantly, the motivation for sparging extends beyond operating cost. Oxidative

degradation generates ammonia, nitrosamines, aldehydes, aerosols, and PM2.5, all of which impose

measurable toxicological and environmental burdens (Rochelle, 2024; Nielsen, 2018).

Suppressing DO contact reduces the formation of these degradation products and helps maintain

downstream emissions comfortably below compliance limits, a benefit that grows in value as

regulatory agencies tighten controls on amine slip, nitrosamine release, and hazardous waste

generation.

 Viewed from this perspective, the $0.35/t CO₂ economic benefit understates the strategic

value of sparging. Its principal contribution lies in reducing solvent-related environmental

liabilities, facilitating regulatory compliance, and strengthening the long-term viability of amine-

based decarbonization. These outcomes are difficult to monetize yet fundamental to commercial

deployment.

 26

 A key additional implication of DO stripping is its influence on CO₂ product purity.

Several jurisdictions impose impurity limits on CO₂ destined for transport and storage, including

low-ppm oxygen specifications to avoid corrosion, operational instability, and safety risks (ISO

27913:2016; EU CCS Directive, 2009). PZAS typically avoids this penalty because oxygen is

largely consumed at regeneration temperatures near 150 °C, but this outcome is not universal:

solvents that are more oxidatively stable, or operating strategies that depress stripper temperature,

can permit oxygen slip into the CO₂ product and trigger purification requirements. Nitrogen

sparging therefore confers an important process benefit because lowering DO in the solvent

decreases the likelihood of oxygen transfer into the CO₂ product stream and correspondingly

reduces the risk of requiring downstream gas purification. Although this TEA does not quantify

that value, the analysis highlights that DO stripping can mitigate future purification burdens as

CO₂ product specifications tighten across transport and storage markets.

 Figure 6 illustrates how uncertainty in oxidation stoichiometry and DO removal

performance translate directly into the expected magnitude of solvent preservation. Even under

conservative assumptions (4 mol O₂ consumed per mol PZ degraded), N2 sparging prevents more

than 100 t/y of solvent loss at 90% DO removal. At the lower stoichiometric limit (2 mol O₂ per

mol PZ), the predicted savings approach 250 t/y when DO removal increases to 95%,

demonstrating that the retrofit retains economic value even if degradation pathways prove less

oxygen-efficient than assumed in the base case. This sensitivity shows that nitrogen sparging

remains economically worthwhile even as assumed degradation stoichiometry or mechanistic

behavior changes. In other words, the benefit of DO control does not depend on a single reaction

pathway or stoichiometric assumption, but persists across varied representations of how PZ

degrades.

 27

 80% DO Removal
 240
 90% DO Removal
 220
 95% DO Removal
 PZ Saved (t/y)

 200

                        1.75    2    2.25    2.5      2.75     3     3.25     3.5    3.75   4
 Stoichiometry (mol O2 per mol PZ)

Figure 6: Sensitivity of PZ preservation to O2:PZ stoichiometry and DO removal efficiency

 A final sensitivity evaluated the effect of reinstating the pump-around IC loop.

Intercooling increases the solvent flow leaving the absorber, which raises the oxygen-stripping

requirement and therefore nitrogen demand. If oxidation occurs primarily in the absorber sump,

removing DO from this larger flow improves solvent retention, producing a $3.52 MM/y benefit

(equivalent to $1.28/t CO₂ captured). However, if a significant fraction of oxidation occurs

downstream in the hot rich line, the extra nitrogen required to treat the higher flow yields less

solvent preservation, eroding the economic benefit of the retrofit. This sensitivity shows that the

value of nitrogen sparging depends not only on oxidation stoichiometry but also on degradation

location within the capture system.

1.9 Conclusions

• For a 942-MWeq NGCC with 95% CO₂ capture, nitrogen sparging delivers a net benefit of

 $0.95 MM/y ($0.35/t CO₂) by reducing solvent loss and lowering utility consumption.

 28

• The cost of nitrogen supply is $191/ton N₂, and even when sparging hardware and installation

 recovery are included, the total annual cost is still outweighed by the value of solvent

 preserved, reinforcing that DO control remains financially favorable.

• Suppressing DO reduces PZ make-up from 508 t/y to 273 t/y, preserving 235 t/y of solvent

 (0.09 kg/t CO₂) and avoiding $1.18 MM/y of chemical replacement cost.

• Lower solvent degradation decreases reclaimer throughput and waste generation, yielding

 fewer degradation products to handle, lower waste-disposal requirements, and corresponding

 reductions in operational burden and environmental footprint.

• Reduced oxygen-driven degradation also produces $0.63 MM/y in steam savings because less

 solvent is sent to the reclaimer.
